Croll's Admonitory Preface — A Reading

Introduction

Oswald Croll (the book spells him Crollie) wrote an Admonitory Preface to his work on chemical physic. In 1657 Henry Pinnell printed it in English, at the head of Philosophy Reformed & Improved in Four Profound Tractates (London). This site reads that Preface, from its title to the end of its sixth chapter, and says in plain words what it teaches. The Paracelsus treatises later in the volume, and Pinnell’s own front matter, are not covered.

The book is the only source. Everything here was written from the English text of 1657 alone, with no outside knowledge about Croll, Paracelsus or alchemy. Quotes keep the book’s spelling.

References. Every statement points to the text. [CH2.17] is paragraph 17 of Chapter II; [OPEN.24] is paragraph 24 of the opening address to the reader; [MICRO.5] is paragraph 5 of the passage on the Microcosm, the Little World Man; paragraph 0 is a section’s head; [CH4.9–10] is paragraphs 9 to 10. [N003] is the third margin note, counted through the whole Preface. The numbering is that of text/preface.json in this site’s repository, made by script from the transcription below.

What is here

  • One page per section of the Preface, as the book divides it: the opening address, Chapters I to VI, and the passage on the Microcosm between Chapters I and II.
  • The Preface as a Whole: its argument in one page.
  • The two subjects the Preface carries, the True Physic and the one chief Medicine of the most ancient Philosophers, each on two pages: in Plain Terms, with the passages behind every point, and in Plain Language, the same in everyday words without references.
  • A word list: the words the Preface uses in a sense of its own, with the book’s spellings and each sense quoted and referenced.

Text and rights. The text is the keyed transcription of the 1657 printing made by the Text Creation Partnership (TCP, EEBO-TCP Phase 2, text A74670). The TCP has waived all copyright and related rights to that transcription under the CC0 1.0 Public Domain Dedication. The 1657 printing itself is in the public domain. No page images are reproduced.

The Admonitory Preface of Oswald Crollie, Physician: to the Most Illustrious Prince Christian Anhaltin

The heading addresses the Preface to Prince Christian Anhaltin [OPEN.0], but the first words speak to the reader: “COURTEOUS READER” [OPEN.1].

The section moves in four steps: why the author breaks the old silence about secrets; what he offers and how he tested it; the enemies he expects, group by group; and a numbered list of what the Preface will handle.

Breaking the rule of silence

He opens with the case against himself. The ancient Philosophers, by the example of Actaeon, “have strictly forbidden to prophane the Secrets, and rashly to prostitute the Treasures of Nature to unworthy and filthy minded men” [OPEN.2]. That rule is theirs (voice: QUOTED, the ancient Philosophers); he reports it under “ALTHOUGH” and sets himself against it with “Notwithstanding” [OPEN.2].

His ground is God’s example: the Heavenly Father shines on all, “for he communicateth his light, without respect or envy, to the good and bad” [OPEN.2], and “We are bound to immitate him whose Children we ought to be” [OPEN.2]. A margin saying, under the name Victor, adds: “The gifts of God grow, & are improved by communicating” [N003].

Stated: he has kept something hidden until now. He will no longer “hide as in a Napkin, the Talent committed to me by the Father of Lights, by an impious silence” [OPEN.2]. Even so, the opening is to some: “the Gates of Wisdome ought alwayes to stand open to ingenious men” [OPEN.2].

What he offers, and how he came by it

He will “set before thee two of the most excellent Spagyrik Mysteries” [OPEN.3], brought “out of the most deep secret of my heart” [OPEN.3]. Their preparations “have all of them been first proved by my own handy experience” [OPEN.3]. He refuses to “fill up a huge Volume with the copies of large Receipts” [OPEN.3]; the margin adds, “The greatest Receipts the greatest Deceits for the most part” [N005].

Much of his material was gathered “for the space of almost twenty years” of travel, from men learned in Chymistry, “partly by intreaty, partly by purchase and permutation” [OPEN.3]; other things he found himself “in the practice of Physick” [OPEN.3]. A margin note says: “Some of these have beene prepared by others for the Emperor Rudolph to whom I voluntarily imparted them” [N004] (note-only).

He tested before he published: he would “first take my eyes in my hand, as I may so say” [OPEN.3], marking that phrase as a figure, and “by the help of Vulcan search and diligently examine the chief and choycest things” [OPEN.3]. Interpreted: Vulcan stands for fire, since the next paragraphs speak of “Nature’s Vulcan” and of “Pyrotechnye, or the Art of Fire and Chymistry” [OPEN.4; OPEN.5]. His aim is “that so the Children of knowledge, those searchers out and lovers of Truth may attain that at a cheap rate which hath cost me dear the learning” [OPEN.3]. Many boasted secrets disappointed him: in exchanges the others “got an Egg for a Nut”, and he found “nothing but a worm-eaten kernel” [OPEN.3].

Trial by fire against hearsay

Of those who wrote in his time of the secret preparations “from other mens relation & not their own handy experience” [OPEN.4], he says the greater part brought students only lost labour. Since “many things fall out between the Theory and the Practice” [OPEN.5], “no man is further to be believed, then as every one findeth by his own proper experience” [OPEN.5]: by the trial of fire, “which discerneth and separateth that which is false, from that which is true” [OPEN.5].

Few medicines, and one set apart

A saying given to Damascen (voice: QUOTED, Damascen) grounds the choice of few things: “Let thy Medicines be few, whose vertue and operations thou hast often tryed” [OPEN.6]. Nothing in Nature, he claims, is more choice than what he offers, “except only that universall and most famous Medicine which the most ancient wise Philosophers had from the beginning of the world” [OPEN.6]. Interpreted: that Medicine belongs to some only, since the Philosophers “have extoll’d it as a miracle to their just and lawfull Heirs” [OPEN.6]. Going further needs work and grace: “if the grace of the most high favour be infused into him” [OPEN.6].

The readers he means

He admits: “I have proved but some of those virtues and operations” assigned to each medicine [OPEN.7]. This sits beside his claim that all the preparations were proved by his own hands [OPEN.3]; one place speaks of preparations, the other of virtues and operations. For the rest he trusts readers able to judge, “the Spagyriks, or those who know how to discern between true and false” [OPEN.7]: so the book explains its own word Spagyrik, and later speaks of “that Spagyricall or Divinely-distinguishing Sanctuary” [OPEN.11]. These are “the Sons of Art, who know the tearms and bounds thereof” [OPEN.7], men “who can even by a glance and sight only make a good construction and easily guesse of these things” [OPEN.7]. Stated: the work is pitched to experts, for “these preparations doe require such an Artist” [OPEN.7].

They keep apart from the common rabble of Alchymists because of a slander, “The errors of the Artists are imputed to the Art” [OPEN.7], whose speaker the text does not name (voice: UNCLEAR).

The true physicians are to use the medicines “rightly prepared by their own labour” and not trust “the Sophistical and fraudulent preparations of others” [OPEN.8]. They will find “far greater efficacies and operations by the cures they doe, then I can or ought to set down and assign” [OPEN.8]. Interpreted (wording-dependent, on the one word ought): he limits what he writes, not only what he knows.

The critics foreseen

He meets the danger of publishing with “the buckler of my sincerity” [OPEN.9], and numbers the groups he expects against him.

1. The Mystical Hermetic Philosophers

“The Mysticall Hermetick Philosophers who heretofore in part have pryed into these excellent secrets, will rise against me” [OPEN.10]. Their complaint, reported to be answered (voice: QUOTED, these Philosophers), is that things “hitherto concealed in deep silence; should so soon be brought to light, and made common to every one” [OPEN.10]. “They will accuse me for breaking open the seale of Chymistry” [OPEN.10], and for forgetting the Rule of Hippocrates, “To impart sacred things to sacred persons” [OPEN.10].

His answer: as heirs of wisdom they should be “without envy, or at least ought so to be” [OPEN.10], and they “shall know that in the true Cabala, Magick and Woarchadumie, there are laid up far better Treasures” [OPEN.10]. A margin note here reads “A word I doe not understand” [N015]; it names no word, and its voice is UNCLEAR (note-only). The way to those Treasures is the Oratory and Laboratory: “I mean by asking, seeking, and knocking with unwearied paines and watchfullnesse” [OPEN.10].

These Treasures are hidden only for a time: they are “to be manifest indifferently to all in due time by the light of Grace and Nature” [OPEN.10], namely “after the Bloody Judgement of the Son is first finished, which undoubtedly will follow the Watry (Judgement) of the Father, then in the third Age of the Holy Spirit” [OPEN.10]. Then comes “Elias the Artist” [OPEN.10], one “who is to restore all things” [OPEN.10]. He gathers the Ages from “the twelve Articles of the Apostles Creed, divided into three parts, very fitly answering to so many houres of the great and One Day, viz. The continuance of this world” [OPEN.10]; Stated, the text says what the One Day is.

What he publishes now he calls small: “these present crums to our thankfull posterity” [OPEN.10]. Yet he also says nothing in Nature is more choice than his offering, the universal Medicine excepted [OPEN.6].

The secrets also guard themselves. The slothful fail “by reason of their indisposition and unfitnesse to manuall operation” [OPEN.10], and the profane will hardly grasp “these Divine revealed Mysteries, which are mingled together in a fit place” [OPEN.10], things “which the wise-hearted can apprehend in few words” [OPEN.10]. Several defensible readings of “mingled together in a fit place” [OPEN.10]: (a) the Mysteries are set among other matter so that the unworthy pass them by, since the sentence is about the profane failing to discern them; (b) they are simply gathered in their proper place in the book, taking “fit place” [OPEN.10] in its plain sense. The margin adds: “Only they that are worthy shall understand these things” [N018].

So the Preface holds two things together: the duty to give like a Father who shines on good and bad [OPEN.2], and the claim that only the worthy will understand [OPEN.10; N018].

2. The Theophrasteans

Second come “the spurious and adulterous Theophrasteans (the worst and wickedest sort of men)” [OPEN.11]. Publication spoils their trade of “their cheating impostures up and downe the Country” [OPEN.11]. Their own are “forged Cachochymicall and Sophisticall Arts, which have not so much as a spark of truth in them” [OPEN.11]. Through such Cheaters “the most laudable and honourable name of true and noble Alchymistry, hath been unworthily made infamous and odious” [OPEN.11]. The margin: “These are not the vices of the Art, but of men abusing the Art” [N020].

3. The Galenists

Third, “the more single and subtle part of the Galenists, who like Nicodemus are of a milder temper” [OPEN.12], who “for fear of being excommunicated by some Athenian Rabbies, durst not openly professe the truth” [OPEN.12]. He expects them to welcome the light, but fears envy: “they will a little coldly commend those things, which notwithstanding are secretly reserved for their uses” [OPEN.12].

Then “the more dull and drossie sort of them” [OPEN.13], Interpreted as still Galenists, since the paragraph follows on the milder part. They are “plunged into Scholasticall dirt and dust over head and ears” [OPEN.13] and take their weapons from “their Captain, that railing Binarius” [OPEN.13]. The only reason he gives why their rage “should be sent to the Isle Anticyra” is “their ignorance of the just and due preparation of things” [OPEN.13]. The margin: “There is a two-fold Alchymy. Naturall, much esteemed by Artist. Sophisticall, as much detested by them.” [N023].

The Doctors and their titles

The Scholastic Doctors “who for want of wit could never find the way into Natures Sanctuary” [OPEN.14] condemn skilled physicians who lack degrees, titles “with which neither Hippocrates nor Galen, nor any Phisitians that we read of in their time were indowed or adorned” [OPEN.14]. The good physicians refuse them “least they should be made to swear to the Heathenish errors of the School-Gods” [OPEN.14]. Once “the spur of virtue, and the deserved reward of Learning”, titles are now, especially in Phisick, “bestowed either through flattery or bribery” [OPEN.15] on many unworthy of them. Their holders in time may come to question Galen’s method, seeing “the end doth not answer his beginning in the great Cures of Diseases” [OPEN.15]. The remedy: “they ought to become Schollars and servants, not Doctors and Masters of Nature” [OPEN.16], for “God onely is Lord and Master of Nature” [N027].

The converts in old age

Old physicians came to him complaining of studies in which, “like those that feed on Crabs and Crevises, they find much shell but little meat” [OPEN.17]. Some chose “to put off their old skin, like the wise Serpent, and putting on a new one, to become Schollars of Chymistry” [OPEN.17]; Stated, the text applies the figure itself. They study “those Secrets which are both in God and Nature” [OPEN.17].

Two books are named: “the great book of Grace, wherein the eternall health of our soules consisteth” [OPEN.19], and that of Nature, with its treasures “wherein the most High hath laid up medicines for our greatest and worst diseases” [OPEN.19]. The margin names them otherwise: “God is the first book that teacheth us eternall life” [N030], and “The firmament or heaven with all therein is the second Naturall book conducing to a mortall life” [N031].

These converts leave “the dead shadows of meer outsides” [OPEN.20], glossed as “or the externall Galenicall qualities” [OPEN.20], and by “Phylosophicall sequestration of the Creatures, or Naturall things” [OPEN.20] gain skill at the sickbed. The text names that work but gives no step of it here. The margin states the end of the art: “the end of Phisick is not to scrape up money, but to discover the secrets of Nature, and the love of the Phisitian to the sick” [N034] (note-only).

The thieves, and the closed bath

Some of the rabble “every where forbid, as poisonous, those Phisicall Secrets” [OPEN.21] and call the Chymists “Collier-like-Phisitians” [OPEN.21] (voice: QUOTED, these opponents, reported to be rejected), yet have “a secret and subtle desire to learne those things” [OPEN.21]. For them the door is shut: “admission to the Bath of Diana is not to be granted, because Pythagoras forbids to put our victualls into a close stool” [OPEN.21], “nor should we bestow our choicest herbs on such as rudely rush into the Chymists garden” [OPEN.21]. Interpreted: the Bath and the herbs stand for what he offers, withheld from the unworthy, since the paragraph is about who may receive these secrets. Still he will not “shut these doors against those that seek after the true and Ancient Phisick” [OPEN.21], who “give place to Paracelsus according to his desert in practice and preparations” [OPEN.21].

He limits the attack: “neither will this Discourse offend any holy and Learned Phisitian” [OPEN.22]; it is aimed at those “who oppose the Chymicall verity against conscience” [OPEN.22].

What the Preface will handle

Last, “before I come to describe the Remedies” [OPEN.23], he lists what is “to be explained by a Philosophicall Phisitian” [OPEN.23]:

  • I. What Phisick cures and “few Phisitians know it”, with “a perfect Philosophicall Description of the little World Man, almost forgotten and unknown” [OPEN.24]. Several defensible readings: what is almost forgotten may be the Man, since the words follow “the little World Man” [OPEN.24] directly, or the Description, since that is what the list item offers.
  • II. “Where that True Phisick lyeth hid, and may be found.” [OPEN.25]
  • III. “That it is to be fetcht out, and prepared truly, by fire.” [OPEN.26]
  • IV. How “that Phisick worketh on mans body, and expelleth his diseases” [OPEN.27].
  • V. “What manner of Phisitian that Phisick doth require for its Minister.” [OPEN.28]
  • VI. The universal Medicine, “commended of many, possessed, yea seen, or believed of very few” [OPEN.29].
  • Then “Some thing in defence of the published Truth, as an Epilogue or Conclusion.” [OPEN.30]

The list keeps the theme of hiding: true Phisick “lyeth hid” [OPEN.25], and the chief Medicine, set apart earlier [OPEN.6], is possessed by very few [OPEN.29].

Chapter I. Of the True Physick

The chapter treats one thing, named in its first words: “THE true Physick whereof by the Devine assistance I intend here to treat” [CH1.1]. It runs from where that Physick comes from, through the agreement of the great world with man, the Elements, the Astra’s and the three Principles, to the cause and cure of diseases.

Physick comes from God

In his own voice the author says the true Physick “is the mear gift of the most high God; it is not to be sought for or learned from the Heathens, but from God alone” [CH1.1]; the margin sends the reader to “Paracels. his Labyrinth of Physitians” [N036]. Hence “no mortall Master or dead letter can ever teach it so well as he” [CH1.1].

The Physitian “should be born out of the Light of Grace and Nature of the inward and invisible Man” [CH1.1], a teacher also called “the internall Angell, the Light of Nature” [CH1.1]. Physick rests on Nature, “upon which God hath written with his own sacred finger in sublunary things, but especially in perfect Mettalls” [CH1.1]: “God therefore is the true Foundation thereof” [CH1.1]. A margin note adds that teaching is vain for a scholar “whose Nativity is not disposed to that Art” [N037].

Stated, a definition: “Physick is nothing else but the created and incarnate Mercy of our Heavenly Father, bestowed upon poor afflicted Mortalls” [CH1.2], so that the sick may feel the Creator’s love and God be glorified. The margin denies that “University books are” its foundation [N038], and, Stated, note-only: “This Physicall essence in Phisick is called Gold.” [N038] Physick is also “written in the book of Nature” [N038], to be read “by Chyromancy and phisiognomy” [N038].

The Medicine and where it lies

“this Medicine, as naturall Mummy and kernell of Nature, is contained in the vitall Sulphur, as in the treasure of Nature” [CH1.3], and is founded in “the Balsam of Vegetables, Mineralls and Animalls” [CH1.3]. “By its onely power all diseases are cured” [CH1.3], if it be “rightly prepared, and separated from all impurity” [CH1.3] and given by “a Godly skilfull Phisitian” [CH1.3]. How, he promises for later: “as shall be shewed anon” [CH1.3].

The great world and the little world

The ground of all: “The Foundation of this Physick is according to the agreement of the lesser World Man with the greater and externall world” [CH1.4]. Philosophy teaches of Earth and Water, Astronomy of Firmament and Aire; together they “make up an internall and perfect Phylosopher” [CH1.4]. The great world is to the little “as of a parent to the little World as to the Son” [CH1.4], and one must “duly compare the Anotomy of the World with the Anotomy of Man” [CH1.4].

“The outward World is a speculative Anotomy, wherein we may see, as in a glasse, the lesser World Man” [CH1.5], since what a Physitian must know of man “cannot be understood from the man himselfe” [CH1.5]. They differ in outward form, but “in essence and internall form they are altogether one and the same thing” [CH1.5]. “Heaven and Earth are Man’s Parents” [CH1.5], so he who “can Anotomize them, hath attained the true knowledge of their child Man” [CH1.5]. Interpreted: Anotomy, the book’s word, means here knowing a thing’s parts and powers in its nature; CH1.38 sets it against the “Locall Anotomy of a man and dead corpses” [CH1.38].

Stated, under the margin head “What the Light of Nature is.” [N039]: “the Light of Nature, which is nothing else but a divine Analogy of this visible world with the body of man” [CH1.5]. The phrase is used in more than one way, and several readings are defensible: an inward teacher, beside “the internall Angell” [CH1.1]; the likeness of world and man [CH1.5]; or a gift from above, “that singular inspiration of God” [CH1.18], with “the Firmament is the Light of Nature” [CH1.41].

“Man is an hidden world, because visible things in him are invisible, and when they are made visible then they are diseases, not health” [CH1.6]. He may be known “visibly and invisibly or magically” [CH1.6]; Interpreted, wording-dependent: the or sets magically beside invisibly as its other name. Nature “is its own medicine, disease, and Physitian” [CH1.6]; “Nature, not man, maketh a Physitian.” [CH1.6] Because “the Matter of Man is the Extract of the four Elements” [CH1.6], man must have “a familiarity with all the Elements and their fruits” [CH1.6]; God created the Elements “that they might sustaine and preserve Man with food and Physick” [CH1.6].

Everything fourfold, and the inhabitants of the Elements

The Elements differ only in form: “in the foure Elements there is but one Anatomy essence and matter, all the differenc being onely in the Form” [CH1.6]. “There are Foure of every thing, one in the Firmament or Heavenly Element” [CH1.6], one each in Aire, Water and Earth: “So there is a fourfold Man” [CH1.6].

The next account is given as Paracelsus’s: “Paracelsus faith, that to avoyd an Emptiness in all the four Elements, he created living creatures” [CH1.7] (faith so printed), “inanimate, that is to say, without an Intellectuall Soule” [CH1.7], unlike “Men created after the Image of God” [CH1.7]. Four lists follow with no speaker named, so their voice is unclear: Nimphs, Undens, Melosyns for the Water, with Syrens their Monsters [CH1.8]; Gnoms, Lemurs, Sylphs, Montans, Zonnets for the Earth, with Pigmyes [CH1.9]; Umbratils, Satyrs and others for the Aire, with Gyants [CH1.10]; Vulcanals, Pennats, Salamanders, Superi for “the Fire or the Firmament” [CH1.11], with Zundell. The Flagae are Theophrastus’s, “which Theophrastus in his works affirmeth are in many thousands of severall forts incorporated to the Soul of the World” [CH1.11].

A fourfold Medicine; Astrology and Chymiology

“Thus also there is a fourfold Medicine” [CH1.12]: the fourfold Heart of the Macrocosm answers to the Heart of man, and so every member; “He that knows not this Fundamentall cannot be a good Phisitian” [CH1.12]. On the word of “ancient Records” [CH1.12], “Astrologers and Chymiologers were very near of kin” [CH1.12]; the Caelestiall Astronomy and the inferiour both “have their own Heaven, their own Sun, their own Moon” [CH1.12]. Interpreted: Sun and Moon are used of the inferiour too.

Here understanding is tied to some only. “Those Chymists who by the assistance of divine Grace have attained the Mind” [CH1.12] can unfold “all Phylosophicall difficulties that have been wrapt up in aenigmaticall obscurity” [CH1.12]. Stated: these matters have been wrapt up. Interpreted, narrowest and wording-dependent: the Mind is something reached by Grace, named together with knowing how to fit the properties of upper bodies to lower. Such men “need not travell to India or America to get the knowledge of Phylosophy” [CH1.12].

Given as Severinus’s: “as P. Severinus the Dane doth learnedly observe” [CH1.13], the constellation of the seasons is “contained in the Earth, Water, Aire” [CH1.13]. Rejected as a mistake of others: that to the Firmament alone “many of the common Phylosophers by a great mistake have ascribed all Astronomy” [CH1.13]. The margin: “The Astrum of Man and Heaven is but one.” [N046]

The twofold Heaven and each thing’s own Astrum

“There is a twofold Heaven” [CH1.14]: outer, the bodies of the stars; inner, Stated, “the Astrum or invisible and insensible body in all the Stars of Heaven” [CH1.14], which “is the Spirit of the World, or Nature, as Paracelsus calls it, the Hylech” [CH1.14]. Several defensible readings: “as Paracelsus calls it” names Paracelsus’s word for Nature, or for the Hylech, or for both. Man’s inner heaven is “the Olimpick spirit” [CH1.14], and the invisible Man is “one and the same thing with the Spirit of the world, as whitenesse is with snow” [CH1.14].

So all things, “even stones and mettalls” [CH1.15], have “a syderiall spirit, which is called Heaven or the Astrum, the secret Forger, from which every Formation, Figure and Colour of things proceedeth” [CH1.15]. Man’s own is “The Sun of the Microcosm” [CH1.15], which Paracelsus is said to call “the Ens or Being of the seed” [CH1.15].

The author narrows his words, Stated: form comes not from “the visible coales of Heaven, nor of the invisible body of the Astra’s in the Firmament, but of every things own proper Astrum” [CH1.16]. He rejects “the false Philosophers” who think “the stars of the Firmament do infuse virtue into herbs and trees; no in no wise” [CH1.16]. The upper stars “excite and stir up the growth of inferiour things” [CH1.16] and provide dew, rain and seasons, but “do not infuse the internall Astrum into things that grow” [CH1.16].

“the externall stars do neither incline nor necessitate Man” [CH1.16]; “Man rather inclines the Stars, and by his Magicall imagination infecteth them” [CH1.16]. Our conditions come “from the hand of God through the breathing in of the breath of life” [CH1.16], and “Mans Reason ought to rule the externall Stars” [CH1.16]; if “the children of Adam” [CH1.16] did not “provoke our Father with our sins” [CH1.16], “we should alwayes find him meek and gentle towards us” [CH1.16]. “The course of the externall Firmament is free with its constellations, and is governed by none” [CH1.17], and so the Firmament in man is “not at all governed by the outward Firmament” [CH1.17]. As “the Aire or Sun cannot set an apple or pear upon the tree” [CH1.17], the outer heaven gives no virtue to growing things.

The golden chain

The fruits of all the Astra’s help one another, and that help is named, Stated: “This visible and invisible fellowship of Nature is that golden chaine so much commended, this is the marriage of heaven and riches, these are Plato’s rings” [CH1.18]; in the margin, “a Divine Series and Order serving Providence” [N051]. It is “that dark and close Phylosophy so hard to be known in the most inward and secret parts of Natare” [CH1.18]. For it Democritus, Pythagoras, Plato, Apollonius and others travelled to the Indies and “to Hermes his Pillars in Aegypt” [CH1.18].

The three Principles

“there are THREE Principles of all things which are found in every compound body” [CH1.19]: “No body compos’d by Nature can by any dissolving skill be parted into more or lesse then Three, viz. Into Mercury or liquor, Sulphur or Oyle, and Salt” [CH1.19]. The second ground: “the Holy Triunity when it spake that Triune word FIAT created all things Triune” [CH1.19], seen in a Spagiricall resolution, glossed “A seperation of purity from impurity, or truth from falshood.” [N052] The world is “nothing else but a fume or smoak coagulated or curded together of the three substances” [CH1.19]. Green wood shows them: a watery moisture and an oily one, which the fire consumes, and the Salt that “remains in the ashes” [CH1.19].

“The Salt by coagulation gives Solidity, Colour and Tast to all bodies” [CH1.19]; the Sulphur “gives the Body Substance and Transmutation” [CH1.19]; Mercury, “like the Elixir giveth the vertues” [CH1.19] and cherishes the two others. A further view is others’: “Some Theophrastaeans, who have more narrowly and exactly searched out the causes of hidden things doe add a Fourth” [CH1.21], the Spirit, answering to Aire [CH1.21].

The Elements, out of Severinus

“we shall add a few things concerning them out of that short Treatise of Severinus” [CH1.22], the author says, closing later: “Thus much out of Severinus” [CH1.25]. Between these marks the matter is given as Severinus’s.

“The true and purely spirituall Elements are the keepers, nurses, places, Mines, wombs and receptacles of the whole Creation” [CH1.22], in two Globes like an egg [CH1.22]. In them the Creator planted “the Light and Seminall causes of all things” [CH1.23]. “The Seeds and Astra’s, those bonds of things, lay hid in the invisible Treasures of the Elements from the beginning of the Creation” [CH1.23], and “The knowledge of the Elements cannot be attained unto without these Seeds” [CH1.23].

Some bodies have “onely properties without Arcane or hidden secrets” [CH1.24], “in which there is no vertue for curing diseases” [CH1.24]; in others “a seperation made of the strong from the weak, of the pure from the impure” [CH1.25] is possible. What is said of Fire, Aire, Water, Earth and of their likeness in man is “to be understood of Elementated Elements” [CH1.25], “for the true Elements are Spirituall” [CH1.25]: Element has two senses. Then, in the author’s voice: “least that which he hath said should seem obscure to the inconsiderate Reader, we will now speak more clearly of the Elements” [CH1.25].

The Elements more clearly

He that would know “those four Pillars of the World” [CH1.26] shall “understand himselfe and his own Originall” [CH1.26]. “The body of the Element is a dead and dark thing; the Spirit is the life, and is divided into Astra’s” [CH1.27], and “no visible body is of it selfe and from it selfe, but from its own invisible Element and Astrum” [CH1.27]. Of the stars, “Nostoch saith, they feed on fire” [CH1.28]. “Our visible Elements are but the bodies and houses of others” [CH1.28], and things joined in a visible body choke “the force, power and operation of the inner Spirit” [CH1.28].

Each Element is twofold. Of the Earth: “The Externall is not the Element, but the body of the Element, and is the Sulphur, Mercury, Salt” [CH1.29]. Of the Water: its fruits “are digged out of the Earth” [CH1.30], for “the Astrum of every Element brings forth and bayes its fruits in a strange region or matrix” [CH1.30]. The Aire “is the Balsam of all created things” [CH1.32]. The stars grew out of Heaven and abide there [CH1.33], and “the seven Rulers of the world are nothing else but the fruits of Fire” [CH1.34]. Stated, of what Sun properly means: “not the body of the Sun, but the Spirit in the body, is the Sun properly; the like also may be said of Man” [CH1.34].

Seed, death and the second life

“the four Astra’s of the said Elements are the Seeds in the four matrices or wombs” [CH1.35]. “Angels cannot increase themselves because they want a body” [CH1.35]. When the Seed rots, the Astrum passes into a new body, “as Christ himselfe sets it forth by a similitude and example in a graine of Wheat” [CH1.35; N068].

“Putrefaction consumeth and separateth the old Nature, and bringeth new fruit.” [CH1.36] So of eternal life, “death is the cause of the glorifying of the body” [CH1.36]; so in the work: “’Tis necessary that the first life of hearbs and medicines should die that the second life by the Chymists help may be attained through Putrefaction and Regeneration” [CH1.37]. Then “the Three First discover themselves with their hidden vertues” [CH1.37], and “without Regeneration no hid Secret of Physick can be attained to” [CH1.37]. Interpreted: Regeneration is used of the Chymist’s work beside the words of the glorified body; the chapter lays the two together.

Man has “the Physicall body of Man, which is nourished from the Earth, and Sydereall body which liveth by the Firmament” [CH1.37]; growing things come “out of the invisible Seed” [CH1.37], not from the four humors.

The Anotomy of disease and cure

“It is not the Locall Anotomy of a man and dead corpses, but the Essentiated and Elemented Anotomy of the World and man that discovereth the disease and cure” [CH1.38]; “the Anotomy of the diseases and of the medicines is but one” [CH1.38]. The chief thing, Stated, is the Concordance of Nature: “how he may make the Astrum of the Physick or of the magicall Heaven agree with the internall Astrum and Olimpus of Man” [CH1.38].

Diseases “doe not proceed from the four fictitious humours or qualities, but rather from the Seed” [CH1.39]. “the Three First are the cause of all diseases” [CH1.40]: united, the body is sound; where not, “sicknesse and the root of the first death hath taken footing” [CH1.40]. Some diseases are “Astrall or Firmamentall” [CH1.40], from the Firmament in man. “Heaven is the Father and teacher of all Arts, except Divinity and holy Righteousnesse” [CH1.40], and “all Believers and Regenerate men are hid from, and unknown to Astronomers” [CH1.40]; for this the author sends the reader to “the Sage and deep Phylosophy of Paracelsus” [CH1.40]. The margin: “The Galenists doe know nothing of these Astral cures” [N074].

Not cured by contraries: the Arcana

The Astra’s or Elements, “which are Spirits” [CH1.42], are “without any quality neither hot, nor cold nor dry, nor moyst” [CH1.42], but what is produced out of them has qualities; Snow, Raine, Thunder and the like proceed “out of the Three First, i. e. Mercury, Salt, and Sulphur” [CH1.42]. Hence the conclusion: “diseases are not cured by contraries, as if heat were to expell cold” [CH1.43], but “by the secret things or Astra’s which the Chymist can reduce out of the last matter into the first” [CH1.43]. Stated: “These Arcana or hid things are actually neither cold nor hot” [CH1.43]. “Of this sort are the Fift Essences, Magisteryes, and the like.” [CH1.43]

Now by God’s assistance I shall say something concerning the Generation, Dignity, and Excellency of the Microcosm, or Little World, Man

The heading names one subject three ways, Microcosm, Little World and Man [MICRO.0]. The section runs from knowing oneself, through the making and parts of man, to the invisible man within him, his Imagination and a kind of Magick, and ends by naming those who cleared this foundation.

Knowing oneself

The author opens with a definition: “the most excellent Phylosophy is that which enlightens the mind to the right knowledge of it selfe” [MICRO.1]. He gives Trismegistus the saying that ignorance “is the greatest Enemy and principall Tormenter in every Man” [MICRO.1]. A warning follows to the man “who considerest not the Treasure that is hid in thy earthen vessell, and may thereout be digged” [MICRO.1]. The lesson God has set is printed in capitals, “KNOW THY SELFE”, which Agryppa ties to the Oracle of Apollo at Delphos [MICRO.1].

Self-knowledge comes first because man holds all things and the Creator’s image in himself; knowing himself, he may “as in a kind of Deified glasse, behold and understand all things” [MICRO.2]. This lasting knowledge is not got from books or reasoning: the mind is perfected “by a passive reception of Divine things; not by study and paines; but by patience and submission” [MICRO.3]. “The Notion of all things is created together with us and in us”; sin has put us to sleep, and “we are onely to awake out of our slumbring and snorting” [MICRO.3]. The author’s own proof: Christ would not have commanded us to seek unless the treasures of wisdom were laid up in us before [MICRO.3].

Interpreted (this page’s inference): the treasure hid in the earthen vessel is what MICRO.3 calls the notion of all things hidden in us; MICRO.16 speaks again of the “Talent and Treasure laid up in his Earthen Vessell” [MICRO.1; MICRO.3; MICRO.16].

Self-knowledge and the knowledge of God

We are to know ourselves “according to both kinds of Light.” and the next words are numbered, “(1) according to the Spirit and Nature” [MICRO.4]. Two readings are defensible: (a) the two Lights are Spirit and Nature, under one number; (b) the numeral opens a list, and Spirit and Nature stand under its first number only. MICRO.17 again says the knowledge of man is to be taken “from both Lights” [MICRO.17].

The knowledge of God rises at once from self-knowledge, “for neither can be absolute & compleat without the other” [MICRO.4]. Dionysius is given the view that “we cannot know God from his own Nature, but from that most orderly disposing of all the Creatures proceeding from himselfe” [MICRO.5]. Ignorance of God is “the Rise or Spring of all diseases, the root of wickedness” [MICRO.6]; to know him is life, as our Saviour says, “This is life eternall to know the Father the true God” [MICRO.7], and one who knows God in Christ becomes his temple “and so is Deified” [MICRO.7].

Against the philosophy of the Heathen

Hence “what the Heathen wrote concerning Nature is for the most part false” [MICRO.8], though many were “seduced by ignorance rather then malice” [MICRO.8]. The rule: “All true Phylosophy should be grounded on the Scriptures and so return into God” [MICRO.8]; “No Art can be perfected without Regeneration” [MICRO.8]. The margin: “Christians should not be ruled by Heathen Phylosophy.” [N089]

An order of steps follows: “For Faith, Hope and Love, follow knowledge, Adhaesion or cleaving to followeth Love, Union follows Adhaesion, in Union is Blessednesse and Wisdome.” [MICRO.9] Hermes and others of godly life before the Word was incarnate knew this Regeneration, “though they concealed it among other Secrets, they knew it better then many of us who call our selves Christians” [MICRO.9].

Man between God, Angels and the world, and his fall

Man stands “between Time and Eternity” [MICRO.10]: God above, the Angels beside him, “from whom he differeth onely in body and the Judgement to come”, and below “the visible World whereof he is a pattern” [MICRO.10]. He is “a true Proteus of a fickle & wavering disposition”, able by grace to be “regenerated into a quiet Angell” or, creeping downward, to “degenerate into a restlesse Bruite” [MICRO.10].

Turning from the mean to himself, man “learned by experience what his own proper Evill and Nothing was” [MICRO.10]; “like Lucifer, not content with his lot” he set himself against God and was “cast out of the Garden of pleasures into this dolefull and darksome valley of Misery and Ignorance” [MICRO.10].

How man was made: the bond of the world

“Man, the bond or buckle of the world” was made last, on the sixth day, from the slime of the Earth, which the text explains as the purest substance of the whole frame of the world drawn into one body [MICRO.11]. He was “made to supply the place of the fallen Angells” [MICRO.11].

“As an Oration is made up of letters and sillables, so the Microcosm or Limus Terrae, Man is compacted of all bodies and created things.” [MICRO.12] God “took the Quintessence out of all things created” and made man of it [MICRO.12], and the creatures honour him as “Gods steward set over the Orchard or Garden of this world” [MICRO.12].

The figure of Centre and Circle: God “is the Centre in that all things flow from him”, and “He is the Circle, because like an all-capacious Tabernacle he concludeth and comprehendeth all things.” [MICRO.12] So too: “Thus Man in imitation of his Creator is the Centre of the Creatures, and the Circle of them all.” [MICRO.13] Centre, because all creatures pour their virtues on him “as upon a middle Point and Retinacle or that by which they are stayed and supported”; circle, because he “leadeth back all things that gushed out of that Summum Bonum, or chiefest good, unto the fountaine of Eternity” [MICRO.13].

The chain of images and the parts of man

A descending chain: “The world was the first figure or image of God, Man is the image of the World”, and so down to stones [MICRO.14]. The author approves an old name: “the prudent Ancients wilsely called Man a Microcosm or little World, which few now a dayes understand, that the great visible World was made Man” [MICRO.14]. Stated: the text gives the sense of the name itself.

Man is twofold, visible and invisible; he has two Spirits, “one a syderiall Spirit from the Firmament, the other from the breath of life” [MICRO.14]; and “Man hath three parts, a mortall Body with a Syderiall Spirit, and an immortall Soule” [MICRO.14]. Three ways of living follow. By flesh and blood only, “he is but a Brute or Beast”, a matter deferred to “my Treatise of Signatures” [MICRO.15]. “If he live Rationally, then he is a Man” [MICRO.15]. By the God-like Spirit, “then hath he dominion over the Stars and all things else” [MICRO.16], for “He was made of the world, he beareth the world about in him, and is borne of the world.” [MICRO.16]

Seed, two Fathers, and the third mean

The first matter, “which Phylosophers call the Chaos and Hylen, or Mother of the world”, was the seed of the great world, as the great world is the seed of man [MICRO.17]. As “in every graine or seed of the Tree there lies hid another Tree”, so the first matter, “which Paracelsus calleth the Limbus, whose Earth was the WORD of the Lord”, was the seed of all things to be created, and man came last as the perfect seed [MICRO.17].

“Man hath two Fathers, an Eternall whose Image he beareth, and a Mortall one, which is the whole world with all the creatures” [MICRO.17], the Limus Terrae, called a “hidden Secret thing” [MICRO.17]. Between the body from the Elements and the soul stands a body from the Firmament, the soul’s vehicle, a “third mean which partaketh of the other two” [MICRO.17]. Through it the soul “is first poured and descendeth into the middle poynt of the Heart, which is the Centre of the little world” [MICRO.18]. “The whole Firmament is in us with the Planets and Stars” [MICRO.18], “so that of the syderiall spirit of the Firmament we may learn all Naturall things” [MICRO.18].

One in person, three in essence

“As God is One in Essence, Trine (or Three) in persons, so Man is One in Person, Trine (or Three) in distinct Essence” [MICRO.20]: earthly body, heavenly spirit, and the soul God breathed in. The witnesses are Scripture and “the unanimous consent of all that truly professed Phylosophy from the Light of Nature” [MICRO.21]; the margin adds Paul, who “shewes three parts of Man, Spirit, Soule and Body” [N114].

Even one who denies the three parts must grant the body from the earth and the breath of life, “which is that Linum Caelorum, or slime of the Heavens from the Lord” [MICRO.22]. The life in the body “is the Syderiall Spirit, and is properly the Man, not flesh and blood” [MICRO.23]; in the soul dwells “the Spirit of the Lord, the WORD of God, the eternall man” [MICRO.24]. So “it followeth that there are three worlds or Heavens in Man”, and “some have called him the Fourth World” [MICRO.24]. The author asks: “for what else is the Spirit of Man which God breathed into him but God himselfe dwelling in us?” [MICRO.24] And, on a condition: “if he be a true Magician, he is not inferiour to the Angells in all Magicall operation” [MICRO.24].

Five things man partakes of

Five numbered heads follow [MICRO.25–29]. (1) With God: “the Intellectuall Soul is a certaine particle of the Divine Soule” [MICRO.25]. (2) With the Angels, for he is “Gabalis socius Angelorum, a curdled companion of the Angells, or one mixed with in fellowship” [MICRO.26]. (3) With the Firmament, from which comes the astral spirit; but “This Spirit is mortall; onely the Intellectuall Soule which God breathed into man is immortall.” [MICRO.27] (4) With the Elements, of which his body is made; the world, “the Parant of Man according to Paracelsus”, holds four Elementary Spirits and a fifth kind [MICRO.28]. (5) With all animals, vegetables and minerals [MICRO.29].

Hence man is the most excellent creature, and he rises so far that “he is made the Son of God, transformed into the same image which is God and made one with him” [MICRO.30], a thing “not granted either to Angells, or the world, or any other Creature, but to Man only” [MICRO.30].

The Sydereall or invisible man

The author now treats the invisible man more fully: “If Aristotle had taken notice of this Olympick Spirit, and Galen made more account of it, there had not such errors crept into Philosophy and Physick” [MICRO.31]. “Thus Adam had two bodies, that is, a visible Elementall, and an invisible Sydereall body.” [MICRO.31] At every birth the visible man is the instrument of “an incorporeall Astralick man which moveth, guideth, and performeth all skilfull matters” [MICRO.31], and it is “the invisible Sydereall body that is capable of attaining Phylosophy” [MICRO.31].

This inner man is one with the Firmament, “like rednesse with wine, whitenesse with snow” [MICRO.32]. It has many names, each with its reason: the Genius; “it is called Penates, because it is in our power and born with us”; Paracelsus his Adech, “that which first inwardly formeth in our mind what we afterward outwardly fashion with our hands”; and the imagination [MICRO.32].

The author explains his word Astra’s: not “the seaven visible coales of Heaven which are but the bodyes of the Astra’s”, but the invisible body of all things; and “they are nothing else but the verue or powers of the Angells” [MICRO.32]. Stated: the text gives this sense itself. His example: “the seeds of any graine or Wheat is the Astrum, viz. the invisible body” [MICRO.32]. “Bodyes are nothing else but the Excrement of the Astra’s” [MICRO.32], and “There is no body without an Astrum” [MICRO.32].

Imagination

Man’s imagination is all the Astra’s, “the Gate of Wonders, the spring and originall of all Magicall operations” [MICRO.33]. The child in the womb is marked by the imagination without touch, and “Thus the Imagination breedeth the Plague and such like Firmamentall diseases; it brings sicknesse and health.” [MICRO.33] The sydereal spirit “is the Load stone and hath a magnetick nature in man” [MICRO.34].

“This fourth kind of Naturall Magick called Gamahaea doth all things invisibly and spiritually, by the help of Art, which Nature is able to doe visibly and corporally without such help.” [MICRO.35] “There is nothing so hidden in the world which is not praefigured in the Astra’s” [MICRO.35]. As a cloud rises in a clear sky, so in the first Creation all things came “out of the DIVINE NOTHING, or invisible Cabalisticall Poynt, into something” [MICRO.35]. The Firmament too has an Imagination, “but without Reason” [MICRO.35].

Man, the Quintessence of the greater world, “may not onely imitate Heaven, but rule it also at his beck” [MICRO.36], on a condition: “when by holinesse we draw vertue from him who is the true Archetype” [MICRO.36]. Such works are done by Imagination “through the Naturall inborn Faith”, and “The Imagination of Man is the Loadstone that attracteth above a 1000 miles off” [MICRO.36]. The margin limits it: “By Faith we may do good or evillus God permitteth.” [N126]

Gabalisticall Magick and its three Pillars

“Thus by Imagination and true Gabalia all hearbs, all growing things, all mettalls may be produced. This part of Magick is called Gabalisticall, and is supported with three Pillars.” [MICRO.37] First, inward prayer, “not with noyse of words, but in a sacred silence, without opening the mouth and groaning” [MICRO.38]. Second, “NATURALL FAITH, or in-born Wisdome”, given to all men alike [MICRO.39]. Third, “a strongly exalted IMAGINATION”, shown in Jacobs Rods and in pregnant women, which draws other men’s fancy, “as we see in those that gape and yawn” [MICRO.40].

Two outcomes stand side by side. Some by melancholy imagination “have exposed themselves to the temptation of the uncleane Spirit” [MICRO.42]; others, by firm faith and prayer, “on a sudden they have become the Temples of the living God” [MICRO.43]. The margin: “True Faith is the cure of a false Imagination.” [N130]

The sum, and where the author breaks off

“The Sum of all is, that we worship God devoutly in honesty and holinesse” [MICRO.44], as “the more secret Theosophers or wise-hearted to God well know” [MICRO.44]. Inward prayer “uniteth the mind with God, and learneth and knoweth all things of God” [MICRO.44]. Then the author breaks off: these contemplations “will seem harsh and crabbed to the rude and vulgar sort of men; for few read them, and fewer understand them”, and “they require a larger narration then can be made of them at this time” [MICRO.44]. Stated: the text says it gives only part, and why.

Who cleared this foundation

All Chymists, he says, should know “this true Fundamentall of the occult Phylosophycal Physick”, because of “the Harmonicall concord and conspiration between the superiour and inferiour things of the greater and lesser world” [MICRO.45]. Next to Paracelsus he ranks Petrus Severinus the Dane, with Pratensis, for his “Idaea of Paracelsean Physick”, and names Joseph Quercetan, Th: Bovius and Th: Muffet as having confuted Severinus’ adversaries, “the best Hermetick Physitians of this age” [MICRO.45].

Chapter II. Where that True Physick is to be Found

Physick is the book’s word for medicine: “the true medicine (or Physick)” [CH2.1]. The chapter looks for it in steps: the kernel of natural things, the Spirits in them, the Word of God, and last the honour due to God and to the Physitian.

The twofold nature of lower things

What God created is perfect, “as the heaven” [CH2.1]. But whatever is in “these sublunary inferiour things” has two natures: “a perfect and an imperfect, that is, a fift Essence and the dreggs may be separated one from the other by fire” [CH2.1]. So the medicine lies covered. It is “wrapt up in rindes, barks, matrixes, receptacles, husks, garment and cottages, as Almonds and all kernells are covered with bark and rind” [CH2.1]. It therefore “must be seperated from the impure Elements by the artificiall Anotomy of Chymists before we can come at the pure Medicine” [CH2.1]. That way “the bonds are loosed by art and industry, and the faculties of healing set at liberty” [CH2.1].

Reading: Stated. The author gives the likeness himself, kernel within rind, and says what it stands for [CH2.1]. The margin adds more likenesses, “nor can we have meale without bran, neither is hony without a sting”, and speaks of “Such Secrets” [N132] (note-only).

The specific virtue in the seeds

In “those three Families of Nature, the Vegetable, Animall, and Minerall” [CH2.2] “there is contained that True and specifix Physick of materiall distempers” [CH2.2]. It does not lie in “the four outward, naked, superficiary and Relollaceous qualities” [CH2.2], and the author names an authority for this: “as Theophrastus learnedly discourseth” [CH2.2]. It is “a certaine specifix vertue concealed in the very Seeds, naturally proceeding without art” [CH2.2]. The Creator planted it at the beginning “by vertue of that omnipotent Word” [CH2.2].

The virtue is “like the soule in mans body from the beginning of the Creation” [CH2.3] and “is not from without” [CH2.3]. Other sources are rejected. It is not “infused into them by a momomentaneous position of the Stars, nor made up of a fortuitous meeting of Atomes” [CH2.3]. Nor is it from the body, “for then it could not be separated without the destruction and corruption of the body and forme” [CH2.3]. The rule is that “the actions of Cures proceed from the Spirits and vitall Tinctures of the spirits, not from the bodies or dead Relloceous qualities” [CH2.3]. The margin agrees: “Heaven is the framer of the externall edifices, not of the Secrets, Wonders and mysteries that dwell in them.” [N133] (note-only).

Man the Microcosm and the three shops of cures

The author builds on what is “granted by the Interpreters of Secret Nature” [CH2.4]. These are other people’s words, which he accepts. They hold that “there is nothing in the whole Universe, every particular whereof is not also in that Microcosm Man” [CH2.4], and that “the seeds of all things lye hid in him” [CH2.4]. If “the internall Heart of the Microcosm or Man should be sick” [CH2.4], it has been the part of True Physitians to enquire how they might borrow remedies from “his Parent or the externall Heart of the Microcosm which is one like the other” [CH2.4]. The two Hearts are alike “if not in outward, yet in inward figure and forme” [CH2.4]. The remedies come from “those three shops of Cures” [CH2.4]; the margin beside them divides inferiour Nature into “three principall parts, i. e. Vegetable, Animall, and Minerall” [N134].

Several readings (wording-dependent). Both Hearts are printed as the Heart of the Microcosm [CH2.4]. (1) The outer Heart belongs to the world outside man. It is called his Parent and external [CH2.4], and later the outer brain is “The external Macrocosmicall Braine” [CH2.7]. (2) The words stand as printed: the outer Heart too is of the Microcosm.

Minerals: gold and what is withheld

Gold comes first among Mineralls, with an aside: it is gold “which cheareth up the Spirits when a man hath enough of it in his purse” [CH2.5].

Here the chapter first says that some knowledge is open only to some men. Mineralls bear “Magicall and Hieroglyphicall Characters, which sage Antiquity hath without rashnesse or superstition attributed to them” [CH2.5]. These “doe sufficiently insinuate to Inlightned men, and Magically disclose their hidden vertue to those more secret Phylosophers that are instructed therein” [CH2.5]. Reading: Stated. The choicest things, “by Natures just decree, with-hold their benigne and vitall Element from those that possesse them” [CH2.5]. The margin says the same of gold: “As he that hath much Gold may know nothing of its medicinall vertue.” [N135] (note-only).

Gold “would discover most Divine actions if it were rightly refined, and had its power reduced into act by a Naturall and due Resolution” [CH2.5]. A parenthesis adds that “there is but one way to Resolve and to compound things, because Art and Nature, like mother and daughter, consent to each other” [CH2.5]. Yet “few men have this gift of God bestowed on them, so as to make solid and massy Gold potable, that it may be drunk” [CH2.5]. The chapter calls this a gift given to few.

Vegetables, animals, and the Spirits that direct

A list of Vegetables follows, “with six hundred of the like” [CH2.6]. Then come animal remedies, such as horns and the bone in a stag’s heart [CH2.7]. Rightly prepared, they cure the diseases of the Heart, “but by an Internall, proper, specifick, harmonious, similer virtue” [CH2.7]. All healing lies in the Spirits: “the earthy part is altogether dead, husks and rinds beget nothing, the Spirits onely in the bodies of things doe all” [CH2.7]. The author glosses his own terms: “not the body, but the Astrum or hidden Heaven gives all the direction” [CH2.7]. These Astra, the Formes of medicines, are “separated by Alchymie from their bodies” [CH2.7]. Reading: Stated.

The Spirits “onely know how and where to find out the disease” [CH2.7], and the medicine finds its way by likeness: “every medicine striveth to get to its place, and seeketh after that member that is like unto it” [CH2.7]. What draws it is a magnetick vertue “which may well be called the inexpressible property” [CH2.7].

Next comes “Celsus the Roman Physitian confesseth of all Arts” [CH2.7], followed by “where by many good old country women have gone beyond great learned Physitians” [CH2.7]. Voice: UNCLEAR, between Celsus and the author. The margin beside it reads: “Experiment, as also judgement, without knowledge is but fallacious difficult and fortuitous, but with Science it is true and infallible.” [N138]. Several readings (note-only). (1) The note limits the praise of the country women. (2) The note backs the rule of “the true Phylosophicall Phisitians” [CH2.7], which rests on long Experience.

The same is said of “the other six principall members and parts of mans body” [CH2.7]. Two are named. “The external Macrocosmicall Braine is the Oyle of Silver, Water of the Sophire Emerold, Mosse, Vitrioll” [CH2.7]. And “The flower or CHIBUR or Brimstone are a Balsam for the lungs and the whole breast.” [CH2.7].

Every disease has its medicine

Even “Chronicall, Astrall and fixed diseases” [CH2.8] can be cured. The common sort of Physitians, who “know not the seat, seeds, nativities, roots, and centres of diseases” [CH2.8], judge them incurable. The author reports their view in order to reject it. His rule: “there is no disease, as it is a disease, but there is a medicine for it” [CH2.8]. The one exception is disease “from the anger of the Divine Majesty by secret predestination” [CH2.8], whose cure was given “to the Apostles, who by true faith in Christ healed all Diseases” [CH2.8]. The margin sets out two origins of disease: “the Caelestiall is cured by the Word of the Lord, the Naturall is recovered by naturall means” [N139].

He rejects the view he gives to Pliny, of Nature “as an unjust Step-mother” [CH2.8]. In his own words, “she is the benigne Parent of all Things” [CH2.8]. So “It were therefore great folly and sottishnesse to think that the great Creator would hide these things from men” [CH2.8].

Interpreted (mine). This stands beside the withholding in CH2.5 and the Word “not known to every one” [CH2.20]. The margin shows how the two may fit: “There is no cause to complaine of the want of medicines but onely of our ignorance of them.” [N139]. On the narrowest reading, God does not hide the remedies, and men fail to know them.

The beasts and their herbs

“Every Creature knoweth his proper remedy.” [N140]. “The Stork having eaten a Serpent, is cured with Organy.” [CH2.9]. From the Toad the author draws a rule: “therefore it is not good to eat Sage before it be washed” [CH2.9]. No man in his right wits, then, will think the Father would “neglect his own children, which he created after his own Image, and prefer the Beasts before them” [CH2.10].

Medicines work by the gift of God

“The most High Author of Nature ath created Medicines out of the Earth” [CH2.11]. The margin points to Eccl. 38 [N141]. These medicines are “not defectives but perfect” [CH2.11], yet they work “not of it selfe, but by the gift of God” [CH2.11]: “unlesse God be present and infuse virtue into the Hearbs what good can Dittany doe” [CH2.11].

Power comes down a chain: “All these inferiour things, as living creatures, hearbs, stones, mettalls have their force by subministration from the Heaven, and the Heaven from the Intelligences, and these from the great Worker” [CH2.12]. So “we must not trust so much to them as to God” [CH2.12]. The example is “Asa dyed because he trusted more to the Physitians then to God.” [CH2.12], with 2 Chro. 16.12 in the margin [N143].

Several readings. The virtue was earlier “not from without” and not infused by the Stars [CH2.3]. Here God infuses it [CH2.11], and force comes from the Heaven [CH2.12]. (1) CH2.3 denies only a momentary or fortuitous cause, the Stars’ position or the Atomes, while CH2.12 describes a lasting chain that ends in God. (2) The two passages stand against each other as they are.

The Word, the heavenly medicine

“The Caelestiall Medicine onely, or the WORD of God” [CH2.13] “is that which healeth all things” [CH2.13]. The opening likeness returns: “As the bark is not the kernell, so hearbs are not the medicines, but a signe onely of the Word signified.” [CH2.13]. “Physick is two-fold in the Earth” [CH2.14]. The Visible Physick “ought not to be administred before there be a separation of the pure from the impure” [CH2.14]. The Invisible Physick is “from the Son by the Word, and is but one” [CH2.14]. And “the Hearb is not the medicine, for that is invisibly hid in God himselfe” [CH2.14].

Interpreted (mine). The medicine has now been placed in the kernel [CH2.1], the Seeds [CH2.2], the Spirits [CH2.7], the Word [CH2.13] and God [CH2.14]. CH2.14 itself holds two of these together: it speaks of “the Hearbs in which the medicine is” and of the medicine hid in God [CH2.14]. The narrowest reading assigns the medicine in the Hearbs to the Visible Physick and the medicine hid in God to the Invisible.

Healing by a word: the Name and Characters

God “could (and can) make men whole by the Prophets and True Cabalists with a word onely” [CH2.15]. The text goes on: “the NAME also of the living God is lively, and so the Letters of the living Name are also lively” [CH2.15]. Great things have been done by “True Magicians (by whom I doe not mean Nicromancers or them of the Black Art)” [CH2.16], “by a Word written and Characters or Signes” [CH2.16]. The author cites Agrippa: Characters “have no force from the Figureors Pronunciation” [CH2.16], but from what God or Nature ordained for them.

“Medicines are visible bodies; Words are invisible bodies” [CH2.17]. Both heal “by the Spirit of God made One with Nature by his Word FIAT” [CH2.17]. For cure by words “written, carved and hanged about the neck” [CH2.17], he sends the reader to Rog. Bacon [CH2.17].

The Word incarnate and the daily bread

The great cures of Physitians are done “by the efficacy of the Triune and Divine Word onely” [CH2.18]. The Saviour healed the deafe and dumb, whom no Pills or Syrups could help. “he did it not by Nature, but by himselfe; he did it by One Word, and he is that Word, to wit, the increated Mercy of God” [CH2.18]. Note (wording-dependent): the same paragraph also says “Created Word” [CH2.18]. One Spirit fills all things and “may well be called The Fulnesse” [CH2.18]. “Nothing is made out of God, for in him all things live, are moved and doe subsist.” [CH2.18].

This Word “is truly our Dayly Bread” [CH2.19], “the supercaelestiall Mummy, the supernaturall Balsome” [CH2.19]. “Man liveth not by bread alone, but by that which is in Bread” [CH2.19].

Here the author says plainly that this knowledge is not open to all. The Word “is the true medicine that healeth all things, but is not known to every one” [CH2.20]. Nor can every Scholler “treat and write of it though plunged over head and eares in the dusty learning of School-Divinity” [CH2.20]. He names instead “our friend Theophrastus Paracelsus” [CH2.20], who wrote “of the WORD of God INCARNATE which may be found in the Creatures” [CH2.20]. And “by this Word, FIAT, the seed of the whole world, were Heaven and Earth created” [CH2.20].

The Magus and the true Cabalist

What the Physitian does by herbs “working successively by the space of time” [CH2.21], “the MAGUS, the Wise man or Caelestiall Physitian performeth suddenly and much sooner by Characters and Stones with a most powerfull impression” [CH2.21]. The impression is given in covered words: “the Gamahaea of Influentiall Wedlock to the Terrestiall signe” [CH2.21]. Reading: Stated. The text explains the marriage itself: “like a stretched cord, all the Superiour vertue floweth through every inferiour thing even to the utmost” [CH2.21]. It names this tie “Divine Matrimony” [CH2.21]. The margin heads the paragraph “The explication of a common saving” and gives it: “in Hearbs Stones and Words there are great vertues” [N154] (note-only).

The True Cabalist, “whom Paracelsus calls the Naturall Divine” [CH2.22], “willeth onely what God doth” [CH2.22]. He works in “that Only Divine Name ISHUH in which all things are reckoned up and contained” [CH2.22]. The author glosses this himself: “he doth performe it in the WONDERFULL WORD by the Mind, Faith and Prayer” [CH2.22]. “The New Birth is the Field of Caelestiall Physick which healeth with a word without Externall means” [CH2.22]. “in these Three principall Poynts also consisteth the whole Foundation of the Magicall and Cabalisticall Art” [CH2.22].

Several readings. (1) The three points are Mind, Faith and Prayer, the three named earlier in the paragraph [CH2.22]. (2) They are herbs, stones and words, as in the margin [N154], matching the order of Physitian, MAGUS and Cabalist [CH2.21–22].

Honour to God, and to the Physitian

“Honour and Praise and Glory therefore belongeth to the Creator, who worketh all in all” [CH2.23]. The Physitian is “the obedient Minister of God and Nature” [CH2.23]. “there is nothing at all of his own in it besides Art and right preparation” [CH2.23].

Yet the true Physitian “is to be honoured as the Scripture commandeth” [CH2.24], for three reasons. First, “God worketh and doth his own will by him as by his Minister” [CH2.25]. Secondly, “he ought to excell all mortalls in the search and knowledge of Nature and the Light thereof” [CH2.26], as Homer required [CH2.26]. Thirdly, he shows God’s works “not onely by opening his mysteryes and hidden secrets, but also by curing the sick” [CH2.27].

So Physick is “the most excellent” of the Sciences [CH2.28], and the chapter ends: “It taketh its rise from Theology or the Light of Grace, and endeth in the Light of Nature.” [CH2.28].

Chapter III. How the True Physick, which is covered bark and rind, is to be got out, and rightly prepared by Fire

The heading names the subject: a “True Physick” that is “covered bark and rind”, how it “is to be got out”, and how it is “rightly prepared by Fire” [CH3.0]. The covering, the getting out and the Fire are the threads the chapter follows.

Poison and Physick in the same thing

The chapter opens: “ALL things in respect of the first matter were created perfect, but the Chymist perfecteth the last matter by Fire” [CH3.1]. The margin gives the same work to the physician: “The Physitian perfecteth the Creature of God by Fire.” [N160].

The reason is that “nothing in this lower world (which is subject to generation and corruption) is so noble but hath Poyson in it selfe” [CH3.1]. In God’s chief works “where there is hurt there is also help, where there is venome there is vertue” [CH3.1].

Witnesses follow. Firmianus is given the words that when God gave power to man, “presently he raised up an enemy, least the Power growing lazy should loose its Nature” [CH3.2]. Roger Bacon, “the English Phylosopher”, is given the image: where God “of his MERCY he appoynted plenty of Physick, even there also by the power of his incomprehensible Justice he substituted Poyson to guard it” [CH3.3]. Hermes, “in his Key”, with Ecclesiasticus, holds that “all sublunary things ought to consist of contraposition and contrariety” [CH3.4].

Reading of the guarded Physick. Stated: the margin says what the two are: “The severe Justice of God is the disease & venome in all things: on the contrary, the Mercy of God is the Physick in Nature and in all things.” [N163]. Interpreted (mine): the Poyson that guards the Physick is the covering the heading calls bark and rind; the narrowest support is that the chapter later calls the husks of Physick “enemyes to mans body” [CH3.14].

A margin note to the next paragraph names “The cause of Simpathy and Antipathy.” [N165]: God ordained “that Sympathy and Antipathy should be alike good”, to stir men “to contemplate and search out his secrets” [CH3.5]. Heraclitus “called Nature the daughter of War”, and Homer “called it Contention” [CH3.5].

Man his own enemy; the physician a minister

The strife is in man too: “Man is his own enemy” [CH3.6]. In “the little world, the body, there lurketh perpetuall strife, in it lyeth hid the preserver and destroyer of health” [CH3.6].

Hence the physician’s office: “Nature hath commanded the Physitians to be ministers, and to seperate, not masters to compose” [CH3.6]. Remedies “require preparations, separations and exaltations before they can impart their hidden and restrained vertues” [CH3.6]. The virtues are there already; the work frees them.

The trial by Fire: Chymistry

“As all things are proved by Fire, so also the Tryall of the knowledge of Physick is to be made by Fire: Physick and Chymistry cannot be separated” [CH3.7]. This Chymistry is “not that which Mountebanks use to paint Faces with” [CH3.7].

It works “in imatation of the Archaeon Ventricle or Naturall In-bred Chymist”, frees medicines “from those scurvy raggs wherein they were wrapt up”, so “that pure and Christiline matter may be administred to our bodies” [CH3.7]. Freeing it is deliverance “from prison and captivity”, a hard task [CH3.7].

Reading of the rags and the prison. Stated: the same sentence gives the plain sense, “a due separation from the impurities and corruptible and filthy mixture of superficiall and externall Elements” [CH3.7]; the rags are that outward mixture. Interpreted (mine): the prisoner is the pure matter, which the next sentence calls “this” [CH3.7].

Without this, “all Physick is but livelesse” [CH3.8]. The author rejects common physicians “who patronize their sloth under other mens paines and study” and leave preparation to “some carelesse and covetous Apothecary”, but excepts “the conscientious Apothecaries” [CH3.8]. A physician should be practised in “this true Analysis and vitall Anatomy of bodies”, because “there is no constant quality of any body which is not to be found either in the Salt, or Mercury, or Sulphur of the same body” [CH3.8].

Three orders and three First things

First, “all compound bodies of the inferiour Globe are to be distributed into three orders or companies; into Animalls, Vegetables, and Mineralls” [CH3.9], each examined to its parts, to find “the three First things (viz. Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury)” in each [CH3.9].

How impurity came into things

“The first face of Things was pure, sound, perfect without corruption and death” [CH3.10]. After “the transgression and fall from unity to alterity”, “new Tinctures came in (even infinite evills)”, and “the beauty of the whole Creature was transplanted” [CH3.11]. The margin explains: “The transplantation of the Creature is by the calamity and coming in of sicknesse upon it” [N169]. So “Impurity was joyned to the pure roots, which was the predestination of diseases” [CH3.11].

Hence “Nature therefore, as it is now, gives us nothing that is pure in the world” [CH3.12], as a spur, “that we should begin to learn the knowledge of Chymistry from our cradles” [CH3.12]. Shut out of Paradise, “we ought to till and manure the EARTH, to wit, the whole frame of the world” [CH3.12], in “this LAND of LABOUR” [CH3.12].

Reading of the tilling. Stated: EARTH here means “the whole frame of the world”, tilled “by admiration, searching into, and knowledge” [CH3.12]. The margin to the next paragraph says: “He that hath learned to know God & himselfe hath ordered the Earth with good husbandry” [N170].

This is “the true end of Mans Creation”: to manage the Earth, “recovering what he hath lost” [CH3.13].

The Separatory art: kernel from husk

“But to passe from Food let us come to Physick” [CH3.14]. No one will dislike “this Separatory art which teacheth rightly to discerne and seperate the Good from the Bad” [CH3.14], and a row of pairs follows: “Poyson from healing Medicines and Balsoms, Light from Darknesse, Life from Death” [CH3.14].

Reading of the heading’s bark and rind. Stated: the art separates “that which is pure Celestiall, the kernall and Marrow” from “the Rinds, and Membranes, the Covers, Shells, Husks and dreggs” [CH3.14], which are “the Coate aed Cottage of Physick which are enemyes to mans body” [CH3.14]. What they cover is “the Soul the Inhabitant thereof, the Super-elementall mystery the Quint-Essence” [CH3.14]. Interpreted (mine): the True Physick of the heading is this Quint-Essence, its bark and rind these husks; both places speak of Physick under a covering.

Every venomous thing has a Balsom: “there is no poysonous Creature but hath in it an Antidote against its own poyson” [CH3.14]. But “these Formes of Physick work better when they are extracted, then whilst they are drowned in Matter” [CH3.14]. The margin: “As Death separateth eternall and perishing things, so doth Fire the good from the bad, the Quint-Essence from the body.” [N171]. It also says “Putrefications onely are the true Correctives of all Physick.” [N171].

The ground is the Form: “by how much the more it hath of the Form, by so much the more is there of the Entity” [CH3.15].

Three advantages

“Hence many advantages follow.” [CH3.16]. First, “many sick people will take it more easily and willingly” [CH3.17] than “muddy and pudly potions” [CH3.17]. Second, “Though these medicines be often used, yet they hurt not the stomack” [CH3.18]. Third, “all the hurtfull quality is altogether cast out of, or at least easily kept under in these Essences” [CH3.19].

So “he can be no safe Physitian who hath it not”: one simple may hold contrary substances, “as we see in Opium and honey” [CH3.19]. Only this art lets the Galenists defend their axiom that “all bitter things are hot” [CH3.19].

Reading of the knife. Interpreted (mine; wording-dependent): in “The knot of this Answer must be cut with an Anatomicall knife fire” [CH3.19] the knife is Fire, and the anatomy is the separation CH3.8 calls “vitall Anatomy of bodies” [CH3.8]. The result is stated: “in Opium there is a sweet Narcotick Sulphur, and a bitter hot Salt” [CH3.19].

Even the poison of metals may be “corrected and amended by the help of this Art and of Fire” [CH3.20]. The margin: “Poyson reduced to its Arcanum is not poyson, but a soveraign nedicine” [N173].

Minerals and stones above Vegetables

Minerals and precious stones, rightly prepared, “doe much excell all Vegetables in effecting cures” [CH3.21]. Reasons follow: Vegetables subject to corruption cannot free the body from it “as the Spirits of perfect mettalls doe” [CH3.22]; imperfect metals already heal, so “perfect things doubtlesse have received of the Omnipotent far greater force and vertues” [CH3.23]; plants and living things cannot “admit of the solid and stable Nature of a Balsom” [CH3.24]; and a long generation gives stones “a greater faculty of working”, while “perfect bodies are generated in a shorter space” [CH3.25].

By the way, stones take Tincture from metals “by formation of the Stars, for they are transplanted Mertalls” [CH3.27]. The margin: “Gemmes are Elementary Stars.” [N174].

One seed, one Nature, one Spirit

The author answers those who say “mettalls are of no use in Physick, at least in the civill life” [CH3.28], a view he reports to reject: “the Animall, Vegetable and Minerall sperm or seed have but one rise or originall, and differ onely in Quality of Place and Receptacle” [CH3.28].

That one kind “is the generall seed of all things or subject of the first matter” [CH3.29]. “All things are from one Principle, and tendeth to one” [CH3.30], as in “Anaxagoras his Pamsperm, which Aristotle unjustly condemned” [CH3.30]. The Universal Spirit “liveth in and quickneth this whole Masse” [CH3.31]; Avicen calls it “the Soule of the world powred into all things” [CH3.31]. The author bounds this: “without any superstition or Idolatrous worship, giving honour onely to One God” [CH3.31].

In his own voice: “Nature, I say, is that medium which by an Harmonicall consent joyneth the lowest things to the highest” [CH3.32]. His witness is a child born “with a gilded tooth in the left and lower jaw, which I did both see and handle” [CH3.32]. Those of “more hid and secret Phylosophy” know “that same Spirit and minerall Nature which produceth Gold in the bowells of the Earth is also in Man” [CH3.33].

Witnesses: the matter in man

Albertus Magnus is given that gold was found “in the bodyes and heads of some that were hanged” [CH3.34]; “the Phylosophers say that the matter of their Mystery may be had every where” [CH3.34]. Morienes, asked by King Calid for the matter of the Elixer, answers “It is of thee O King, said he” and “thou are in its Mine” [CH3.36]. Raymund Lully “got his matter out of a mean and worthlesse thing” [CH3.36]. Riplaeus à Portis: it “is of little worth, and may be got out of its Mine by art” [CH3.37]. Rhasis: “in every thing there is every thing potentially” [CH3.38].

Reading of where the matter is. Several readings. (a) It is in man: Morienes’s “of thee” [CH3.36], Riplaeus [CH3.37], and the mineral virtue “in the head among the teeth” [CH3.35]. (b) It is in every thing, man one case: the Phylosophers [CH3.34], Rhasis [CH3.38], and the margin, where “The matter of a stone is said to be in every thing” [N180] (note-only).

“But these things are by the by.” [CH3.39].

Galen and Paracelsus

In Galen’s days “they knew not how to separate the bark and husk from the kernell” [CH3.40]. Galen, in his reported words, tried to “discern the hot parts of vineger from the cold” [CH3.40], and might have succeeded “if he had been well skild in the Distillatory Art” [CH3.40]. This is no disgrace: God and Nature “doe communicate particular gifts to severall ages” [CH3.40].

Had Galen lived when Paracelsus did, “he would not have envyed, but reverenced his learning” [CH3.40]. Against Paracelsus stand physicians “who have not learned so much as their A. B. C. in the Spagyrick Physick” [CH3.40]; their words, which the author rejects, call him “a circumforaneous or rambling rayler” [CH3.40]. The author calls him “the immortall glory of Germany” [CH3.40]. Interpreted (mine): Theophrastus [CH3.41] is the same man, since CH3.40 passes from Paracelsus to “Theophraestus” in one sentence [CH3.40].

Reading of Paracelsus’s Secrets. Stated: had he been borne with, “he would have more clearly have manifested to thankful posterity his Secrets which God disclosed to him” [CH3.41]. The true medicines “require solutions, mortifications, cohibitions, resuscitations, &c. truly Phylosophycall” [CH3.41].

Why the true medicines are few

“But truly I think this wicked generation is not worthy of such medicines.” [CH3.42]. God “will never suffer those Secrets to be known, especially in these corrupt and perilous times” [CH3.42].

Almost all men “have a burning desire after making Gold” [CH3.43], not knowing “the Metaphysicks of Paracelsus, the true Phylosophy” [CH3.43]. Most Chymists are turned aside “by the glitter of Court-service” [CH3.44]. So “this Divine Spagyrie” is disparaged [CH3.45]; but “the bettter the thing is, the more destructive is the abuse of it” [CH3.45].

Closing prayer

The chapter ends: “O fountaine of Truth and Wisedome, consider our condition” [CH3.46], hoping God will stir up some to “confute the delusions and errours of the schoolmen not with words, but deeds” [CH3.46], “the time of Revocation and Regeneration drawing nigh” [CH3.46].

Chapter IV. How, and with what Vertue, Physick worketh upon Mans body and cureth his Diseases

The chapter asks how a medicine works on the body and by what power it cures [CH4.0]. It ends with two stories from Prague, given as instances of what Nature does when she is kept safe [CH4.12–14].

The question physitians dispute

The author opens with what physitians “commonly dispute and contend for”: “Whether, according to Hippocrates his Maxim, that Contraryes are to be cured with CONTRARYES, or Like with LIKE, according to Paracelsus?” [CH4.1]. Each maxim is given to its owner by name.

Both maxims can stand

His answer: “these Maxims may be both received in the Anatomy of Nature, though they seem thwart one the other” [CH4.2]. Many miss the philosophers’ meaning “because they cannot understand how to reconcile them by a seasonable and sutable interpretation” [CH4.2]. Stated: the text itself says the maxims only seem to be at odds and are to be reconciled by interpretation.

Physick is “nothing else but an opposition of those things that are desired”, namely “a refreshing of the strength and Balsom, and a removing of superfluityes or impurities that cause diseases” [CH4.2]. Paracelsus “therefore doth not find fault with Hippocrates” for saying that “hunger is to to be cured with meat, thirst with drink”, labour with rest, and that contraries cure contraries [CH4.2]; these examples are Hippocrates’ words as the author reports them.
