The quarrel is with Galen: Paracelsus “is poynt blanck against Galen”, who applied the contrariety of Hippocrates “chiefly to those bare and naked qualityes which Hippocrates utterly disclaimed”, and “by an unhappy mistake referred the first and principall Idaea’s of Cures to Refrigerations, Calefactions, Humectations, Exsiccations” [CH4.2]. Galen’s view is reported in order to be rejected.

Nature is the physitian

The cure is done by “The onely and alone NATURES of Physicall medicines”, called “those Hippocraticall vertues”; “the Physitian is but the minister or servant” [CH4.3].

The Nature within us is “our Life and Balsom, or balsomicall Mummy, that preserveth the body from all corruption” [CH4.3]. Mummy is thus the book’s word for this preserving Life. It works by the Saline moysture, explained as “the inferiour Balsom springing up in the inferiour from the superiour” [CH4.3]. It “is its own proper Physitian in Mans body, who asketh nothing of the outward Physitian but Instauration”, which the text glosses as fortification “applyed to the diseased part by a most pure medicine when the like Nature is not at hand” [CH4.3].

The Medicinall Balsom assists “the Vitall or Radicall and Naturall Balsom” “like a coadjutour or privy counsellour”, “because of the simpathy and common agreement between them” [CH4.3]. Restored, the natural strength works “as an inward and unknown Antidote to chase all its enemies out of its Monarchy by the onely power of the Vitall facultyes” [CH4.3]. The margin note here reads: “Nature cureth with like things.” [N187].

Against cure by contrary qualities

Two reasons follow. To cure with contrary qualities is “to raise and stir up intrinsick commotions to the utter overthrow of Nature”, already weakened by “intestine quarrells” [CH4.4]. And “Contraryes will not willingly entertertaine each other”; if they do not act on or suffer from each other, “where there is no true action and passion, there can follow no true Naturall effect” [CH4.5].

Medicine agreeable to the part

Medicines therefore “should not be contrary to the grieved part, but very agreeable to it”, having outwardly the Nature the place has inwardly, “because of the Harmony between the great and little world” [CH4.6]. The text adds: “Man is therefore called a Microcosm or little world, because the whole world preserveth, nourisheth and cureth him” [CH4.6]. When the fruits of Earth, Air, Water and Fire in the Microcosm are sick, “they must be restored by fruits like themselves of the Macrocosm” [CH4.6]. So Nature helps its own Nature: “Nature strengthened and assisted by its own Nature doth more forcibly drive out all its enemyes, seeing every Nature is Naturally the best preserver of it selfe” [CH4.6].

Galen is here called as a witness for the author’s side: Nature alone is the physitian of all diseases, “as Galen witnesseth in his 13 Book of Method” [CH4.6]. Nature “is the First Mover of every cure, without whose strength and vigour all Physick is in vaine and to no purpose”, and heals itself “by the innate Mummy” [CH4.6]. Daily experience of wounds is the proof [CH4.6].

Vertues, not qualities

Now the maxim of contraries is allowed in the author’s sense. Those who hold it “are not much mistaken if they have not respect to the qulaityes, but the vertues of a contrary Nature” [CH4.6]. For “there are as many crosse and hurtfull vertues to nature as there be good and healthfull for it”, and the good drive out the hurtful [CH4.6]. Qualities do not: “contrary qualityes, are not rooted out by contrary quallityes, but rather are irritated and provoked to strife by each other” [CH4.6]. The ground is a definition: “Nature is not a quality but a vertue” [CH4.6].

The order of service is stated again: “its not the Physitian that hunteth out the disease, but Nature her selfe (who is the Internall Mummy or inward Balsom)”, helped when she fails by “her servant the Physitian” [CH4.6]. Sometimes “it may be the best medicine not to use any medicine at all but to leave the ooperation to the sole Archaeus or Art of Nature” [CH4.6]. Interpreted (wording-dependent): the word or between them gives Archaeus and Art of Nature as two names of one thing. The margin: “The Inward Physitian doth the work, when the Naturall Physitian faileth.” [N188].

If anyone is preserved in a pestilence by Opium, which is most cold, “it is not by the coldnesse of Opium, but but by the Specifick venemous vertue of the Opium”, which “hath a greater degree of poyson then the occult venomous power of that Pestilence” [CH4.6]. “Thus Nature destroyeth one poyson by another, it subdueth a weak evill by a stronger” [CH4.6]. Of qualities: “As winter doth not destroy summer, nor summer winter, but one gives place to the other, so one quality doth not destroy another”, for “without vertue the quallity is dead and wholly accidentall” [CH4.6].

The root of diseases

“Roots of diseases in the body of Man are neither hot nor cold”; “those Accidents and Excrements are but the signes of the disease, and not the disease it selfe” [CH4.6]. The most pernicious diseases “do not spring out of the matter of the body, or out of the four Humours, but from the Nature of the Seed or Astra’s and Invisible mechanick Spirits of the Three principles”, Spirits that “build their outward house and habitation with shells” [CH4.6]. “These Forgers and Invisible Astra’s of diseases were not knowne to the Ancients.” [CH4.6]. Forgers is the book’s word for these makers of disease.

Physick a Spirit, seen by the wise only

“Physick is a Spirit (not a body) which the Magician or wise man only can discerne” [CH4.6]. Stated: the text says this is open to some only. A step of work follows: “the body or Earth of Simples is to be cast away”, and “the vertue Heaven or Astrum of it only to be taken” [CH4.6], so that “the life should worke upon the life, and the Spirit upon the Spirit by sepration of the impure body”, “as the intangible Sun Melteth Snow and causeth it to vanish away” [CH4.6]. “Such is the Nature of all Secrets that they worke without the matter and body because the diseases also are not bodyes” [CH4.6]. The author names this: “This is the true and lively Anatomy.” [CH4.6].

Cure at the root

“This Mechanick and Forger of diseases is to be subdued and destroyed in his Roote and originall” [CH4.6]. Beside it the margin reads “Paracelsus in the Tincture of medicines.” [N189]: evidence that the words beside it are Paracelsus’. Likenesses follow: “the whole Tree cannot be destroyd in the branch but in the Seed”; grass is hindered from growing “not by evultion but corruption of the Earth” [CH4.6]. A note adds: “Degrees and complexions are not considered in diseases.” [N190]. “Not the smoak arising from fire, but the fire it selfe is to be quenched”; the physitian who cures by complexion is like one who would “extinguish the hurtless flame and let alone the fire in the coals” [CH4.6]. The cure takes in hand the root of the seed, which holds the vertues [CH4.6]. A note names “his Book of ancient Physick” [N191].

What Paracelsus means by like and contrary

“When Paracelsus saith that like preserve their like, and are destroyed by their contraryes”, “he doth not meane the first nor second qualityes which he alwayes calleth Recollaceous and invalid ones, but the substance”, or at least “the Cherionic, Hippocraticall powers and vertues” [CH4.6]. Stated: the text says what the saying means. The margin note at this point is a gap in the transcription [N192].

Then both maxims get their sense. “Like things are said to be the Remedies of diseases because they are of the same Anatomy of Nature, and because they have the like Signatures, Qualities and Roots” [CH4.6]. “Contraryes cure because they piece up the defects and wants”, “because they appease the spirits and consentaneous impurities with a friendly saturity”, and “because they attempt resolutions, consumptions and tacit ablations” [CH4.6]. Interpreted: this is the reconciling of the two maxims that CH4.2 holds possible [CH4.2]. Interpreted (wording-dependent): one strain remains: Qualities stand among the grounds of like cures, while the same paragraph says that “without vertue the quallity is dead and wholly accidentall” [CH4.6], and CH4.4 rejects cure “with contrary qualities” [CH4.4].

Salt, Sulphur and Mercury

The margin note to CH4.7 reads: “Diseases are cured by applying that which is proper for them.” [N193]. “That Like are to be preferved with their Like is thus to be understood”: “the Salt, Sulphur and Mercury of the Microcosm is preserved by the Salt, Sulphur, and Mercury of the Macrocosm analogically agreeing together” [CH4.7]. Stated: the text gives its own meaning. Man has “various Sulphurs (one of the head, another of the heart, &c.)”, answered by a like variety in herbs and minerals [CH4.7], and “The ministry of Fire discovereth their agreement, operation, and difference” [CH4.7].

An unnamed “he” “distributeth all materiall diseases according to those three substances of which our bodies are composed” [CH4.7]. Interpreted: he is Paracelsus, the last person named before [CH4.6] and named in the margin beside CH4.9 [N194]. Diseases from kindled Sulphur “he called them Sulphurean diseases, such are all Inflamations and Feavers”; “Those that proceed from moysture he Mercuriall.” [CH4.7].

Mercury “may three manner of wayes be exalted above the Naturall degree”: “By the heat of digestion it is distild, and so causeth all sorts of Apoplexies”; by exercise it is sublimed, “whence follow madnesse and Phrensye”; “by the heat of the stars it is precipitated, and then causeth the Gout in hands and feet” [CH4.8]. Diseases of Salt he calls “Saline and Nitrons” [CH4.8]. “Salt destroyeth health 4 wayes”, by Resolution, Calcination, Reverbertion and Alcalization, breeding ulcers, scabs and itch [CH4.8]. Surfetting destroys the Salt, excess dissolves it, the stars thrust it “out of its degree”, and “such as is the Transmutation such also is the disease” [CH4.8].

Reading his sayings by their drift

He says “the Sulphur kindled in mans body is to be quenched with the Sulphur of the great world, which harmonically agreeth with the other” [CH4.8]. But “he that considereth to what end he spake it will find that such a remedy will be contrary to the disease” [CH4.8]: his meaning is an appropriate Sulphur from “Natures Garden”, “as Sulphur of Vitriol, Niter, common Salt, and such like” [CH4.8].

Likewise “he saith that Ulcers proceeding from Salts must be cured by Salts” [CH4.9]; yet “if we observe his drift, such Salts are contrary to that which was the cause of the disease”, for “they are incarnative and consolidative, as Frankencense, Mastick, Myrrhe, Aloes” [CH4.9]. Hence “sometime he calls that Salt whatever it be that will melt”, dissolve and be hardened by fire [CH4.9]. Both are Stated: the text says the like-named remedy is in truth contrary, and that Salt is used in a wider sense; the second is wording-dependent.

The margin adds: “All Terresterity is the Salt of Paracelsus.” [N194]. “Cumfry cureth the corrosion of Salt.” [N194]. “Gold thickneth the too much sublimation of Mercury.” [N194]. It closes: “we are to assist Nature against these impediments which are the causes of diseases.” [N194]. These are note-only.

Three sorts of disease, three rules

“As there are Three from which all Physick proceedeth, Mercury, Sulphur, Salt”, so there are three sorts of disease [CH4.10]: “All Ulcers are cured by incarnating Mercury; All mattery gluish sores by Salt; All Inflamations by Sulphur.” [CH4.10]. And: “This needs no Reasons to confirme it.” [CH4.10].

Here ulcers are cured by Mercury; just before, ulcers from Salts are cured by Salts [CH4.9–10]. Several readings: the two rules disagree; or, Interpreted (wording-dependent), since both remedies are called incarnative or incarnating and Salt is said to name such things, the two rules may name one remedy under two words [CH4.9–10].

Courteous to Nature

Medicines, “though they be contrary to the disease, yet they must of necessity be altogether courteous to Nature”, “for she seeks a sweet Peace in every controversie” [CH4.11]. Should she sink or miscarry, “the Physitian can doe no good” [CH4.11]; but “if she abide and be kept safe and sound she worketh incredible wonders” [CH4.12].

Two stories from Prague

The margin vouches: “A stupendious and true Story.” [N195]. At Prague in 1602 a Bohemian countryman “whose name was Mathew, about 36 years of age” would for two years “hide an Iron knife of a good bignesse in his huge and wide throat as in a sheath” [CH4.13]. Once “he swallowed it so far that it went down quite into his stomack” [CH4.13]. After “seven full weeks and two dayes”, “by application of drawing Plaisters made of the Load-stone and other things”, the point began to force its passage “by a naturall impulse”, and at his request it was cut out by “Florianis Mathis of Brandeburge chiefe Surgion of the Kingdome and City” [CH4.13]. The stomach had changed the knife’s colour “just as if it had laine so long in the fire” [CH4.13].

That after a few weeks he could eat, drink and sleep without pain is the man’s own report to the author, “as he told me” [CH4.13]. By God’s help and many men’s liberality “he was made as sound as ever he was in his life and cost him nothing (contrary to the determinate assertion of Physitians Aphorismes)”, and “shortly after he was marryed” [CH4.13].

In 1606 at Prague a Silesian, for money, did “swallow six and forty white flints which he gathered at banck side” [CH4.14] and, “without impairing his health, he went up and down getting his living for many years together” [CH4.14].

Chapter V. The Duty of Natures Minister, the Physitian

The chapter announces one subject: “The Duty of Natures Minister, the Physitian.” [CH5.0].

Free from every sect

The author opens by denying that one ancient holds all learning: “ALL common Philosophy was not bound up in Aaistotle, as P. Ramus hath soundly proved” [CH5.1]. In the same way, “nor was the whole Light of Nature drawne into Galen and limited in him only, witnesse Paracelsus” [CH5.1].

Here the Light of Nature stands beside two other phrases as one thing: no man may deprive another of “the liberty of human ingenuity, that Light of Nature, the power to discern and judge as well as himselfe” [CH5.1]. Then comes a figure: “the Grecian Monarchy is at an end” [CH5.1]. Interpreted (my inference from the naming of Aristotle and Galen just before): the rule of the Grecian authorities over learning is declared over.

The conclusion: “he that would be an Excellent Physitian he must be free from every kind of Sect.” [CH5.1]. He is “not to be tyed. to the opinion of any one Author, but to follow the naked Truth, and subscribe to it alone” [CH5.1]. A couplet given to Horace seals it: “I think and judge as cause I finde” and “My rule is not anothers minde” [CH5.1–2]. The margin sets other lines beside it [N196].

Yet no sect is to be slighted

The author at once qualifies this: “Not that other mens inventions are altogether to be slighted, by stickling onely for one sect” [CH5.3]. All sects may be admitted, in words he gives to Picus Mirandula, whom he calls “the Phaenix of Phylosophers”: “in every one of them there is some thing excellent which is not common to another” [CH5.3].

In his own voice he adds: “There is no book so base and bad but hath some good in it” [CH5.4]. Fabius is quoted: “This latter age, saith Fabius, hath endeavored to make the former more compleat” [CH5.4]. Whose voice carries the rest of that sentence is UNCLEAR.

Secrets still hidden

The margin states: “All Secret are by divine Ordination to be discovered.” [N197]. The text agrees: “Doubtlesse there are more secrets yet concealed in the Treasures of Wisdome and Nature then we perceive” [CH5.5]. They are ordained “for Times and Nations, by an immutable decree” and “are to be sought out by wise-hearted men” [CH5.5]. The reason so much remains: Nature “can hardly be wholly comprehended by any mortall man by reason of the shortnesse of his life” [CH5.6]. Nature is said to be “Circularly”; this rests on one word (wording-dependent) [CH5.6].

Neither the Ancients nor Theophrastus wholly

Therefore “neither the Physick of the Ancients, nor that of Theophrastus, is totally to be rejected” [CH5.7], “nor yet so to be imbraced but that if there be a better found out it also is to be received” [CH5.7]. The rule: “both should be compared together, what is best in both let that be cetained” [CH5.7], “For being but men, they have their failings” [CH5.7]. The margin adds: “Many errours of the Ancients are discovered by dayly experience, which yet hath not attained its end neither.” [N198].

Full knowledge belongs elsewhere: “The holy Spirit alone hath the plenary or full knowledge of all things” [CH5.7]. He gives each man his measure, “reserving many things to himselfe that we might alway acknowledge him to be our only teacher” [CH5.7]. Stated: things are held back, and the text names who holds them and why.

Nature’s minister, and two kinds of philosophers

The chapter’s head returns: “A true Phisitian should be the minister, not a master to Nature” [CH5.7]. That he be a philosopher skilful to cure is given as “the conclusion of Hippocrates & Galen” [CH5.7].

The author then divides the philosophers. Some “after the Vulgar manner will be looking below the Moon after the Elementary Nature of things” [CH5.7]. Others, “more truly deserving the name of Philosophers investigate the Arcana and more secret things of Nature” and “go into the very inner roomes and Sanctuary of Nature” [CH5.7]. Interpreted: the inner rooms are a figure for the secret things named in the same sentence. These men have “the true knowledge and Expeeience of Nature’s Light, which maketh a true Physitian indeed” [CH5.7].

“A Physitian is compleated by 3 things” [CH5.7]: the innate virtue of things that grow of the earth, “the Celestiall influence causing that vertue”, “the uniting of it by Chymistry with the Constellation of the firmament”, and the physician’s dexterity “mediating the same” [CH5.7]. Four items follow the number three; whether the last is a fourth the wording does not settle.

First: the interpreter of Nature

“But first, as Paracelsus saith, let him be the legetimate INTERPRETER of NATURE” [CH5.8]. The margin names a writing: “In Chirurgia magna.” [N199]. In the same sentence, under “as Paracelsus saith”, this interpreter searches out Nature’s “oeconomy, and the universall latitude thereof”, pries into all kinds of creatures, “and then comes to consider and looke into man” [CH5.8].

Whose voice speaks next is UNCLEAR: “Phylosophy teacheth the vertues and qualityes of the Earth and Water, as Astronomy doth of the fire and Ayre” [CH5.9]. The physician needs both, and “Chyromancy, Pyromancy and Geomancy are the Elements of Astronomy and Phylosophy” [CH5.9].

The Theophrasteans are then described by their work. They “contemplate and admire the workmanship of Nature throughout this mighty frame of the whole Creation” [CH5.10], inquire into the heavens, “and lastly into the seeds, principles, dimensions, and instincts of all sublunary bodies” [CH5.10]. Meditation, which the margin defines as “a frequent cogitation” [N201], together with their “earnest desires” [CH5.10], brings them “not onely to understand, but also really to imitate the greatest mysteries and secrets of Nature” [CH5.10].

The two callings are set in order: “When the Phylosopher comes to a stand in the Naturall Light of the Macrocosm, then the Physitian begins” [CH5.11]. His field is “the Analogicall Concordance of the Naturall Light of the Microcosm with that of the great world” [CH5.11].

Secondly: a good Spagirus

“Secondly, a Physitian must be a good SPAGIRUS” [CH5.12]. Stated: the text says what that is, “one that can seperate the pure from the impure, and restore his Patient to health by a wise Alchymicall preparation” [CH5.12]. “As Gold is tryed seven times in the fire, so should a Physitian be p{G160: 1 letter illegible}oved by Chymistry, which sepereteth the good from the bad” [CH5.12]. The good physician is “born of the Light of Nature, to whom Nature communicateth his Experience” [CH5.12].

The margin binds the two: “Phylosophy is the mother of Physitians” [N202].

Paracelsus above all

No man “ever knew and publisht such hard and hidden secrets in all Phylosophy and Physicke” as Paracelsus [CH5.12], “the true Monarch of Physicke, and first Physitian of man” [CH5.12]. He alone “since Noe’s time hath written of the Internall Astrall Man” [CH5.12], and of the origin of diseases “which none of the former Physitians did ever so much as dream of” [CH5.12].

Students of “Heathenish Phylosophy from whence all errour springeth” missed “that twofold unknown body of the Creatures” [CH5.12]. Stated (to wit): a visible body “of the Elements; and the Astrall Sydereal and invisible of the Firmament and the stars” [CH5.12].

A difference stands here: at CH5.7 the physick of Theophrastus is not to be so embraced as to shut out a better, yet here his skill “no man ever yet attaind unto, much lesse exceeded” [CH5.7; CH5.12]. It is recorded, not judged.

Invisible Phylosophy and the New Birth

“The Intellectuall Soul of Man, that divine Light flowing out of that spiracle of God and Divine springs pertaineth to the Invisible Phylosophy, whose foundation is CHRIST” [CH5.13]. So philosophy must be “Christian-like, not after the manner of the Heathen in hollow empty language and temporaneous Arts” [CH5.13]. Knowing Nature is not the whole task: by “the supernall help of the Light of Grace” we are to possess eternal life, and “this is true Theologicall Phylosophy” [CH5.13]. Hence “the New Birth is first to be sought for, and then all other Naturall things will be added without much labour” [CH5.13].

Back to Theophrastus: an ancient art

“But let us return againe to Theophrastus.” [CH5.14]. He was skilled in Chymistry, “though he were not the Auther of that Art” [CH5.14]; earlier philosophers were famous in it, “from whom Paracelsus hath secretly borrowed many things” [CH5.14]. “For that noble Pyronomicall Art hath been most ancient”, once “known only to Kings Princes, & some few of most diligent searchers out of Naturall Phylosophy” [CH5.14].

Paracelsus, “that Monarch of Mysteryes” [CH5.14], restored a lost doctrine, Stated as “the True and Philosophicall Physick” [CH5.14]. He laboured to unfold its “great obscurity” and to recall the sciences “from the compasse of the whole circle unto the one only Centre” [CH5.14]. Against this the Devil stirred up “his Emissaries and catch-poles” [CH5.14].

Cures laid to the Devil

The margin to CH5.14 says: “The Physitian that God maketh can doe all things.” [N206]. Theophrastus “by peculiar influence was born a Physitian” [CH5.15]. His enemies, “like the Pharisees of old”, ascribe his gifts to the Devil, “as though the wicked one were the worker of those cures” [CH5.15]: their view, reported to be rejected. The author replies that they “make the Devill stonger then the blessed and glorious God” [CH5.15]; the Devil “hath no power over the Will and Understanding of Man” [CH5.16].

It does not become the German physicians to “contemn those Secrets which God hath granted to their Nature” [CH5.17]; as the Aristotelians rose against Ramus, “even so doe the mingle mangle Physitians against Theophrastus Paracelsus” [CH5.17].

His Divinity and the two Lights

They also attack “his Divinity, which they have neither seen nor read” [CH5.18], though “he onely is able to judge of Truth who is inspired with Divine Wisdome” [CH5.18]. What his divine writings sought to make known is Stated (that is): “that our mind should be raised up to God” [CH5.18].

Then the two Lights: “The Light of Grace, begetteth a true Theologer, yet not without Phylosophy” [CH5.19]; “The Light of Nature, which is the Treasury of God confirmed in the Scriptures, maketh a true Phylosopher” [CH5.19]. Hence “every true Theologer is a Phylosopher, and every true Phylosopher is a Theologer” [CH5.19].

The third school and the treasure within

Others came after Paracelsus, “such as Paulus Brawn of Norimberge, Valentinus Weigelius, and Petrus Winzius” [CH5.20], taught in “the third school of perfect Men, that Mentall or Intellectuall school of Pentecost” [CH5.20]. Their manuscripts “will in their time come to Light, unlesse the indignity and ingratitude of the world keep them back” [CH5.20].

Readers are to turn “into the Temple of the heart” and fetch out “that huge Talent and Treasury which is hid and shut up within themselvs” [CH5.20], not “seek those things without them by many books, mortall masters, long journeys” [CH5.20]. That God enlighten the mind is “the very determinate and appointed End of the Cabala or secret wisdome” [CH5.20]; the text glosses Cabala itself. Books here differ from CH5.4, where every book has some good; the things sought may differ, so this is recorded only [CH5.4; CH5.20].

“The heart of a Regenerate man is Gods Eden” [CH5.21]. God is not now discerned “by reason of the Dark Point of the Quaternary” [CH5.21]; the world is renewed “by the Ternary of Man” [CH5.21]. Interpreted (narrowest reading): the Ternary is the threefold Regeneration “according to Soul, Spirit, and Body” named next [CH5.21]. The Soul turned into the Mind “hath no need of outward teaching” [CH5.23], and “The Academicall spirit cannot not understand this Mystery” [CH5.23].

Withholding, and the defence of obscurity

The author stops himself: “nor may I in this unfit place openly speak more of these Mysteries” [CH5.24]. Stated: he withholds, and says so.

Returning to Paracelsus, he answers those who hate him for “using such words which every one doth not understand” [CH5.25]. He pleads that “the unusuall custome of Phylosophers plead for the strange novelties of names” [CH5.25]. Stated reason: philosophers have always driven off “those sluggish and lazy Drones” [CH5.25]. Plato is quoted: “Arts wrapt in Riddles safe doe lye” [CH5.26].

Finally, let any man ask “if he had that which Theophrastus knew, whether he would tell it to every man” [CH5.27]; to do so would “break the Oath of Hippocrates” [CH5.27]. This differs from CH5.12, where Paracelsus “knew and publisht” hidden secrets [CH5.12]; recorded, not judged.

A secret kept, and handed only to sworn disciples

The author says plainly: “A Secret indeed is to be concealed, it belongs onely to God to reveale it”, for disclosure “commonly it exposeth to death, or prison, or reproach” [CH5.28]. “The true Hermetick Phylosophers took an Oath” not to “defile the virginity of Nature”, yet “have they bequeathed their secrets to their sworn disciples”, and even these “are with much sweat to be searched out” [CH5.28].

Stated: the secrets pass to sworn disciples [CH5.28]. Interpreted: “the virginity of Nature” is a figure; the narrowest reading is a state of Nature “which hath been preserved from the begining of the world”, which the oath binds them not to defile [CH5.28].

Whom Paracelsus condemns, and what true Method is

Men of shallow judgement think Paracelsus would be “the sole and onely Physitian in all the world”; the author reports this to reject it [CH5.29]. In his own voice: “he only condemns the common rable of Physitians who are altogether ignorant both of Theory & Practice” [CH5.29].

The “fallacious Medodists” charge “the genuine Chymists that they have lost the right method”; the author calls it false, holding that “Paracelsus observed onely that method that was agreeable to Reason and Experience” and that “we are not so much to regard by whom, as what it is that is spoken” [CH5.30]. He defines: “True Method consisteth in the knowledg of the Disease, and the Cure of it” [CH5.31].

Three respects; the first opens on Tartar

Wimpeneus “learnedly sheweth that greatest and most grievous Diseases now a dayes are in three respects cured by the Paracelsians” [CH5.31]. The three follow as First, Secondly, Thirdly; their voice is UNCLEAR, Wimpeneus’s or the author’s, though within the first the author speaks as “I will speak a little more of it” [CH5.32; CH5.45; CH5.46].

First, “the Diseases are more perfectly known”: while all were reduced to the four humours, diseases from Tartar could not be cured [CH5.32]. The author turns aside, “since we are here fallen upon Tartar”, and closes the turn with “So much for Tartar.” [CH5.32; CH5.44].

How food is separated

“The first Ens or Being toward life” arises “by the Archaeus, or digestion of the Stomack” and is reduced to “Sulphur, Mercury, and Salt, as is plain in the three chief Emunctories”: urine, entrails, sweat [CH5.33]. A margin note beside it says “The first digestion of the stomack is not digestion, but apparation for the digestions of the particular members” [N217].

The Archaeus is Stated: the stomach is “the instrument of Mans Archaeus, or the Internal, innate Chymist which God hath planted in Man” [CH5.34]. All food holds a Tartar “hurtful to our Health”; a weak stomach sends the impure to the liver, which “seperateth the pure from the impure, viz. the Rubie from the Chrystall” [CH5.34]. The two names are Stated: “The Rubie is the nourishment of all the members”; the Christall becomes urine [CH5.34]. If the liver is weak, the Spirit of the Salt coagulates the matter in the kidneys “into Sand or Tartar” [CH5.34].

The definition: “Tartar therefore is the excrement of meat and drink, which by the spirit of Salt is coagulated in Man” [CH5.35].

Kinds of Tartar, and how they are known

“Paracelsus distinguisheth Tartar into that which is strange or forraine proceeding from meat and drink, and that which is innate of the cruor or hereditary blood” [CH5.36]. The innate kind yields “onely by the Quint-Essence of Gold which reneweth the whole body”; voice UNCLEAR, Paracelsus’s or the author’s [CH5.36]. Tartar “is the mother of almost all diseases of all coagulate bodies” [CH5.37].

Four sorts spring “from the fourfold fruits of the Elements”: Earth, Water, the flesh of beasts and fowls, and the Firmament [CH5.38–42]. From the last come “Astrall diseases”, which “the universall medicine powerfull expelleth” [CH5.42]. This four is counted by source; the “four kinds of Tartar” of CH5.35 by form in the body, “the stone of the bladder, the Sand of the Kidneys” and the rest [CH5.35; CH5.38].

The kinds “shew themselves in the Urine, and are judged of by the art of separation”, and “The disease is cured by the same Element that was the cause of it.” [CH5.43].

The heat that separates

What parts Tartar from nourishment is “our Naturall Heat, or the microcosmicall heat of the Sun and Moon that is in us” [CH5.44]. Weak, it must be strengthened “by the macrocosmicall Heat of Sun and Moon, if it could be got”, that is, “the most simple matter which the most Great God generated of the spirit of the world” [CH5.44]. Stated: that spirit is “one and the same with the spirit of our body” [CH5.44; N220]. Interpreted: “if it could be got” marks that matter as hard to obtain; the alternative the text gives is “those things wherein the power of the Sun and Moon is” [CH5.44].

Hence “the Archaeus of the stomack must destroy the Tartar of the Elements least it be turned into the Tartar of Man”, for “the spirit of the Sun which is Lord and Master of coagulation in various subjects will breed the Stone” [CH5.44]. In CH5.35 it is the spirit of Salt that coagulates Tartar [CH5.35; CH5.44].

The second and third respects

“Secondly, Because we have more perfect medicines now then heretofore, that is to say, of Mineralls”, whose preparations were “discovered to those skilfull Chymists the Sons of Cadmus”; Paracelsus “would have a steely medicine applied as proper for an iron disease” [CH5.45]. Thirdly, “the Harmony between the great and little world” shows which medicine suits which member: Gold, Pearles, Saffron for the heart [CH5.46]. Interpreted: the little world is man, since the author of diseases “afflicteth us, by destroying the frame of the little world” [CH5.44].

Physicians quarrel among themselves

“Whereas Paracelsus offendeth Galen, Galen did the same to others” [CH5.47]. Five disputes are named, such as Argenterius against Fernelius [CH5.48]. The author asks how many millions “will still perish” before they agree [CH5.49].

Read Paracelsus, but not as an oracle

He exhorts “all Students of Hippocraticall and Galenicall Physick” to read “this new Phylosopher and Physitian” with diligence, like bees “segregating the best from the worst” [CH5.50]; not as “Gospel-Oracles”, since “he himselfe hath recanted something of his over hasty writings” [CH5.51]. To those who “throw away the kernell and pick up onely the husks”, he says: pray “for the spirit, that ye may understand the dead letter of Theophrastus” [CH5.53]. Interpreted: the husks answer to “the dead letter”, the kernel to what “the spirit” lets one understand [CH5.53].

Why Paracelsus wrote in a magical style

Against the charge of contradiction: “he wrote in a Magicall stile; not for the vulgar sort, but for himselfe and the understanding children of Wisdome” [CH5.54]. Had he written plainly, vulgar Alchymists would have “prostituted the Art” [CH5.54].

Stated: “He concealed his mysteries under vulgar and various names”, so “we must not take the similitudes for the truth it selfe” [CH5.55]. Reading them needs “a Delian swimmer, a most acute and sharp wit, a Magicall Understanding” [CH5.55]. Magick, a word with one letter unread in the print, is Stated: not witchcraft, but “the plenary knowledge of occult Nature”, working “by a fit application of Agents and Patients, observing the consent and dissent of things” [CH5.55].

Physician and surgeon in one, working by hand

“every Surgeon should be a Physician, and every Physitian a Chyrurgion, that there may be a sound Bridgroom for a sound Bride” [CH5.56]. The physician prepares his own medicines, “to reduce them to their pure simplicity”, for “neither is Sugar free from faeculent impurity” [CH5.56]. “judgement without practice is altogether barren and fruitlesse”; Nature hands remedies “to her Servant to make them up” [CH5.57]. The Rationall Physitians, who cry out against the Chymists “as if they were no Physitians”, stand helpless at the bed “because they can prepare their medicines but onely with their Reason, not at all with their hand” [CH5.57].

False followers

The author will not defend those who claim Paracelsus but “neither acknowledge the minde of the Author in his Theory, nor doe any thing praise-worthy in Practice” [CH5.58]. The “false and lying Theophrasteans” cure “the party and his disease at once with a turse of the Church-yard” [CH5.59]. Interpreted (wording-dependent): the cure is the patient’s death. Though they “doe sometime exceed many Galenists”, “no conscientious man will ever approve” their practice [CH5.60].

Old and New Physick reconciled

Since “the Paracelsian Theory of Chymicall Physick as yet is wrapt up in perplexities and some obscurities”, students should learn of the Chymists to prepare medicines but “observe the method of Hippocrates in curing diseases”, so “these two Schools of Old and New Physick may be reconcil’d without contradiction” [CH5.61].

Prayer, and Physick made common

What an honest physician gives “is to be accounted of as the hand of God” [CH5.62]. Many physicians cannot endure that Physick be imparted to all, lest, made common, “or as they say Prophaned, they should lose their gaines” [CH5.62]. The author calls it a foolish conceit of the vulgar “that a Secret looseth its vertue when tis made known”, and says the gain-getters will cease murmuring when they consider “that neither God nor Nature hath made every man a Physitian” [CH5.62]. The margin: “Paracelsus would not have Physick be concealed.” [N227].

Interpreted: CH5.62 does not deny the dangers that CH5.28 gives for disclosing a secret; what it rejects is the belief that telling destroys a secret’s virtue [CH5.28; CH5.62].

Physicians should imitate the Hermetick Physitians, “who always use to pray for a blessing upon their endeavours”; to use a creature without a blessing God counts “theft and rapine” [CH5.62]. Pray before, give thanks after; their neglect is “the chief cause of thier ill successe” [CH5.63–65].

Eight causes why the best medicine fails

First, the term of life. “No Medicine can preserve the corruptible body from death”, yet “there is one thing that hindereth corruption, reneweth youth, and prolongeth the short life, as in the Patriarchs”; even “the universall and chiefest medicine is of no effect” against the appointed end [CH5.68]. Several readings of the unnamed “one thing”: the universal medicine of the same paragraph [CH5.68]; or the Quint-Essence of Gold, “which reneweth the whole body”, by the same verb [CH5.36].

Second, unskilful physicians have made the disease incurable; those who let a patient perish rather than yield credit men “commonly (and justly) call Honest Hangmen” [CH5.69]. Third, “the Physitian is sent for too late” [CH5.70]. Fourth, the sick breaks “the Golden law of the Locrians” [CH5.71]. Fifth, hard natures and adverse season: “A ripe pear or apple will fall of its own accord” [CH5.72]. Sixth, “what Nature hath once taken away the Physitian can by no means restore” [CH5.73]. Seventh, the patient’s meanness and doubt; “There is a twofold Physick; Visible or created, and Invisible even the WORD of GOD” [CH5.74].

Faith and imagination

Within the seventh cause: Stated, “Naturall Faith (I speak not of the Faith of Grace by Christ) inborn of God the Father in the first Creation, which is our IMAGINATION” can cause and cure diseases [CH5.75]. In plague, fear “breedeth that Basilisk of Heaven”, and “the Iliaster or Evestrum of the Sun” punishes sin [CH5.75].

“Faith giveth the Imagination”, and “the Imagination giveth a Sydus or Constellation” [CH5.76]. The Spirit that helps is Stated: “I mean the Spirit of Nature or the Sydus of the medicine” [CH5.77]. A new spring in Misnia and Bohemia cures by “the constant excessive affection of them that use it” [CH5.77]. Avicen is quoted: “Nature obeyeth the cogitations and vehement desires of the Soul” [CH5.78]. “God will doe nothing without Man, as his Organ or Instument” [CH5.80].

Eighth, lest the healed sin more: “every sicknesse is as it were an expiation or propitiatory of sacrifice” [CH5.81].

Diseases from the wrath of God

Here no bodily medicine helps, “because God hath shut the doors of Nature”; the cure is repentance through “the Celestiall Physitian of our Soules” [CH5.82]. Paracelsus calls them “Deal diseases, which are from the Ens of God”: “the Cure, saith he, is by Faith” [CH5.82]. The margin: “A Physitian beginneth in appeasing Gods wrath.” [N252].

Chapter VI. Of that one onely chiefe Medicine of the most Ancient Phylosophers

The head names one single chief Medicine of the most ancient philosophers [CH6.0]. This half praises it, says who may seek it, why its holders hide it, attacks those who falsely claim it, and gives the author’s own witness and the older philosophers’ account of it [CH6.1–50].

The greatest gift

The author calls this physick the gift “than which a greater gift of wisdom we never read that divine bounty bestowed on man”, and “this most secret Secret of secret Secrets” [CH6.1]; the margin calls it an occult Minerva and a Jewel beyond price [N253]. It purges the “Plannets of inferior Astronomy”, for “it segregateth all extraneous Sulphureity and impure Terrestreity from metallick and Humane bodies”, and it restores human health [CH6.1]. Interpreted: since the bracket names metallic bodies, these planets most narrowly stand for metals.

A further use he withholds: “to say nothing now of the Magicall and super Celestial use” [CH6.1]. He gives only its condition, the influence of the Sun and Moon and a fourth revolution finished upon its native earth [CH6.1; N254].

He rests all this on antiquity and on eye-witnesses of his time. As God is wonderful in all his works, “so doth he usually hide his wonderfull Gifts in wonderfull men”, whose writings are “woven of so many Hieroglyphicall, Magicall, and Mathematicall Coverings” [CH6.1]. Stated: the truth comes under covers. It promises “a removall of old age and renewing of youth, perpetuall health, and honest food and rayment”, but only “to the Pious and holy Phylosophers dignified by Nature and Education” [CH6.2].

Neither denial nor boast

The author will not deny these wonders, nor revile them, as most do, “as if they were but the speculations of idle men” [CH6.3], a view reported to be rejected. Revilers, “whom the Phylosophers call fools”, are shut out [CH6.3]. Some wait to hear whether he will claim the preparation, as “greedy Phylosophists use to doe” [CH6.4]. His margin answers: “Here I would be taken as an Index, or as one standing at the door to direct others the way they should goe” [N255]. Having promised “I would set down at least those things which I had made tryall of”, he will falsify nothing [CH6.5].

Who may seek it

The Art is “not of Sophisters, but Phylosophers”, though “none aboundeth with so many Imposters as this” [CH6.6]. Its true seekers are furnished, since “it cannot be that a poor or covetous man should spend his time in the study of Phylosophy”, and, after Solomon’s example, pray “not for wealth, but wisdome” [CH6.6]. They love Heaven, secrets and Nature, and refuse no honest labour of the hands [CH6.7–9; N256]. They desire to use the gift for the end the text names itself, “to wit, the Praise and Honour of God, and the good of his needy neighbor”, “in a constant Taciturnity, without pride” [CH6.10].

They are “the Children of the golden doctrine (whose gold is the Omnipotent God)” [CH6.11]. Stated: the text glosses its own word golden. They seek it for men’s health, “leaving the mettalls to those who with an impious hunger” long to be rich [CH6.11; N257]. “A Phylosopher must covet Nothing but Wisdome” [CH6.12]. The gift comes “either by the immediate inspiration of the most High, or by the expert ocular demonstration of a wise Master in this Art” [CH6.12].

Few reach it, and only by God’s will

Few reach “the mount of Chymistry” [CH6.13]. None helps another without long proof of his “pure and Harpocraticall Faith” [CH6.14]. The margin asks where such a Phaenix is found, and says entrance is only by revelation or from one “that hath the Secret, & teacheth it while he liveth” [N258]. Voice unclear: Paracel. is cited beside these words.

The seeker “must not be a slave to other mens judgment, nor live upon their purse, and must retire himselfe” [CH6.15], for “Too much acquantance hinder them / Who labour to find out this Gem.” [CH6.16]. The verse’s voice is unclear [CH6.16; N259].

The author gives the agreed word of reverend antiquity: none found the secret by wit or experience, for “it is somthing Divine and hid, and that which is above Reason” [CH6.17]; God “hath reavealed and made it plaine to his faithfull wise ones” [CH6.17; N260]. So among thousands “there is not one to whom the doors of Nature are unlockt” [CH6.18], since “it is not expedient for all to their salvation, that they should have Honors & riches with their health” [CH6.18; N261]. This holds “when men seek the Kingdome of God, and study to finde out the Celestiall Stone in the last place” [CH6.18]. Several readings: the Stone is to be sought only after the Kingdom of God; or men put both last, and fail.

Some get “the key of the Phylosophicall Garden (as I know some that have)”, yet cannot enter “because the Gate was bolted, that is, the Divine Grace and Benediction was denyed and did resist” [CH6.19]. Stated: the text glosses its gate. Impostors holding a true ferment “prepar’d by others” could not multiply it, “by reason of the ignorance of the principle or beginning” [CH6.19].

Why the philosophers write in covers

The work is “hid under the robe of the Phylosophicall Virgin, which even one brother would not teach another” [CH6.20].

The genuine philosophers “doe study and strive to be harsh and rugged” [CH6.21], have “covenanted and sworn they would never write it plainly in any Book”, and none has made it known except to “such who had an understanding allegorically” [CH6.21]. “God would not manifest this knowledg to all, to avoyd offence.” [N262]. They have “power to dispence or give Names at their pleasure” [CH6.21]. Interpreted: their names are not to be read at face value.

What they affirm: none reaches the end “before the fat or blood of the Sun and the Dew of the Lunary, be joyned into one body by the circular wheel of the Elements with the help of Art and Nature in the form of an Hexagon” [CH6.21]. Interpreted: these are covered words [CH6.21; N264]. The margin says the whole business turns as “Gold animated by the Salt of Niture is made the chief subject of the Phylosophers metallick Physicks” [N263]. Several readings beside CH6.11: the seekers leave metals only as a road to wealth, or the note speaks covertly. And all hangs on grace, “for the Spirit proceedeth from Grace, who inspireth whom he will” [CH6.21].

Against the impostors

Since “all mans endeavor is but vaine except God prosper it” [CH6.22], the author wonders that great men trust “the golden promises of circumforaneous, vitious, and most lying Impostors” [CH6.22; N266]. These Gymnosophists “inrich the eares of credulous men that they may make their pockets poor” [CH6.23], and deal in “painting white and red, and pargetting womens faces” [CH6.24]. Several readings: cosmetics, or a figure for false colour. Alphidius foretold that “instead of the Tincture they should find only the colour, instead of the Hermetick Stone a pibble or glasse” [CH6.25]. The margin adds Lully’s curse: “He that publisheth this Art, let him die an evill death, saith Lully” [N268].

The adept’s silence and the last day

The adept, who “hath happily labored like Hermes more then twenty years”, “will be lesse afraid to die an hundred most cruell deaths” than publish the treasure to the unworthy, for he must account for “the Talent committed to him” [CH6.27]. At the last day “the heat of the Centre united to the heat of Sun shall set all corruption of the Elements on fire” [CH6.28], and what is pure remains “like a pure, cleare, incorruptible and fixed Essence in a serene resplendent Chrystalin Earth” [CH6.29]. “The property of fire is to seperate the impurity of the Elements.” [N269]. Interpreted: the last day is drawn in the terms of the medicine’s purging.

A wish for princes

The author wishes princes would set skilled men to the Three Families of Nature, “viz. Animalls, Vegetables, and Mineralls”, “separating them by Fire into their Three First (things.)” [CH6.30]. This would show “the TRIUNE naked DIANA” with “a most limpid or clear MERCURIAL, a most garnished SULPHUREAN or oyly, and a most bright pure SALINE form” [CH6.31]. Stated: the three forms are named as hers. Francis the First meant to try one, “but was prevented by death” [CH6.32]. With “the sole fountaine being reserved to it selfe for its own uses”, the spare streams would serve the poor [CH6.33]. God gives “by asking, seeking, and knocking” [CH6.34; N271].

The King’s highway

“For this onely is the Kings high-way”, to Nature’s secrets and to God, “alterity being swallowed up of unity” [CH6.35]. “There is no recovering or returning to what we have lost but by the Crosse and Death” [CH6.35; N272].

The author’s own witness

The author sought it “not to the end that I might make Gold and Silver” and, hindered by envy, says “I comfort my selfe with the cogitation onely” [CH6.36; N273]. Yet he was granted “that I should see and taste it, at some Great mans house” [CH6.37]. Words he does not English follow, with odd capitals, and then: “Which I forbear to English.” [CH6.37]. Several readings: the capitals hide letters to be read apart, or are misprints.

The metamorphoses he saw were made in the cold, “the Moyst way of the Ancients not as yet intensly exalted to the eye of the Basilisk” [CH6.38]. A dying man was saved “by giving one onely drop of that Latex or liquor”, a storehouse “in which the whole world was centred”, in one night [CH6.38]. But “many diseases are a Divine innate punishment, for which there is no cure in Nature” [CH6.38], and “There is no Physitian can cure death, but Christ.” [N275]. The margin’s Basilisk “like Lightning suddenly and unawares burneth up any imperfect mettall whatsoever” [N274].

Metals and bodies alike

Metals are cleansed like men’s bodies and “restored to their True Health, which is aureity or goldness”, “for mettals doe not differ in specie but onely in accidents” [CH6.39]. This is “justly not to be made known (though it be truer then true) to our Vulgar sort” [CH6.40]. Their disbelief is a gain, protecting true students [N276].

Paracelsus and Lullius

The Astral physician needs this to cure “the four Monarchs of all diseases, the Epilepsie, Gout, Dropsie, Leprosie” [CH6.41]. Paracelsus cured them “through the help of Christ, not of the Devill”, “proving in some of his works that God taught him the medicine” [CH6.41; N277]. It heals “as a Carpenter that squareth all kind of timber” [CH6.42]. Lullius, approved, held that “between the Artist and God the first cause, there ought to be an agreement without contrariety” [CH6.44]. Some eminent men before, and “two publick Phylosophers of divers Nations in our time”, came to a wretched end, “answerable to the unworthy publishing of this most True Phylosophicall Art” [CH6.44].

The ancient philosophers and their silence

The ancients were “these private not publick Secretaries of Nature” who rejoiced “in Quiet Silence” [CH6.46; N279]. Paracelsus is given as calling Phylosophy Adept “Long Life and free from all infirmity, even till the Naturall death” [CH6.47]. Its virtues are “concentred into one masse” [CH6.49]. Those of Hermes school were “altogether dumb in keeping close the secret of the Art by a constant Taciturnity” [CH6.50], speaking only “in a picture of Aenigmaticall words” [N281]. Yet the reason they give is quoted: the Medicine is “the Life and inlightning Light”, “the Fift Essence compounded of the four Elements” [CH6.50].

The Fift Essence and its names

The author now says what the one chief Medicine is. God created a Fift Essence as “the root of life in Nature for the preservation of the four qualities of the Humane body” [CH6.51]. “The celestiall Fire that burneth not, is the soul and life of all Creatures”, and in it the influences of all the heavenly bodies are gathered into one [CH6.51]. A run of names follows, among them “the Glasse of God’s Mysteries, and the Miracle of universall Nature” and “the Son of the Sun and Moon” [CH6.51]. It is “altogether one and the same with the spirit of our body, the bond between the Body and Soul” [CH6.51]. The margin adds “in this Stone the universal Nature of things shineth forth” and “The Tincture is the Fife Essence of the Microcosm” [N283] (note-only).

This matter “hath been altogether unknown to the Physitians of our time”, who “goe not into the Temple of Apollo through the right door, but break through the Roofe” [CH6.51].

How it cures: equality of the Three First

The author builds on the cause of disease “in the judgement of the best Physitians”: “the enormity of the Naturall proportion of the Three First”, or, “as common Physitians say, the disorder and ill disposition of the four Elements or Humors” [CH6.52]. The Medicine “is in it selfe the matter of our Creation”, and “nor doth it admit any malignant spirit, but they all fly from it” [CH6.52]. The margin gives the reason: “the inequality and dominion of one over another is it that breedeth diseases” [N285]. “Mans health consisteth in the agreement and union of the Three First Substances”; when the Stars set them on fire, “the intestine wars follow” [CH6.53]. And because “the Three First Substances of diseases are valitile”, they “give place to the Essence of Fire which consumeth the disease and separateth the pure from the impure” [CH6.53].

The Fift Essence of the body “reduceth to the true Temperament by making equall the unequall” [CH6.54]. The author states its limit: it keeps a man sound “even to his Naturall death, that is to that Terme of Life which the Omnipotent God hath appointed to Man for disobedience”, and only one “who shall use it prudently and seasonably with a devout calling upon the Name of the Lord” [CH6.54].

The first in physic

The author calls it “this true Triacle of Phylosophers” [CH6.55]. His ground: “For in every kind there is a certaine One that holdeth the first degree in that kind” [CH6.55]. Made of “the Soule or Spirit of the world, which hath in it the force of all Celestiall and Terrestriall things”, it ranks first in physic, and its user “may live as long as the ancient Fathers” [CH6.55].

The Spirit of the world and our natural heat

From Sun and Moon, “as Suchtenius learnedly discourseth”, springs the Spirit of the world, so that “there can be nothing in all the world that may or can be without a spark of this Spirit” [CH6.56]. Whose words these are, Suchtenius’s or the author’s, is UNCLEAR. “This Celestiall Spirit which is one and the same with our Naturall spirit” is our natural heat; when it is hindered, “that breeds bad blood”, and its full dissipation at last is “the Naturall Death” [CH6.56].

So it must be strengthened, and by a rule: “every agent when it begins to act, doth not move toward any thing below it selfe, but to that which is equall like and sutable to it” [CH6.57]. The strengthening is by the celestial heat of Sun and Moon, or by things where their virtue abounds, for from such things “the spirit or that supernall Fire is got out of them by art” [CH6.57]. Two fires are parted: “burning Heat, consuming all things, and darknesse, making all things barren, are proper to the Elementall and Inferior Fire” [CH6.57].

Why gross medicines fail

“Medicines ought to be preserving and very durable, and remote from corruption” [CH6.58]. “tis but in vaine to think to preserve a corruptible body by a putrid and corruptible thing” [CH6.59]; the margin says “One Like added to its like maketh it more Like.” [N291]. This is aimed at “too many Physitians of our time who labor in vaine to cure Men of their maladies by their grosse and corporall compositions of Medicines”, for “diseases are not corporall, but spirituall and lurking in the spirits” [CH6.59].

One note does not fit: “The vitall spirit in Man & the Elemental is one spirit.” [N292]. Another makes our spirit one with the Celestiall [N290], and the paragraphs set the Elementall against the Celestiall [CH6.57; CH6.60]. The clash rests on the one word Elemental (note-only, wording-dependent).

The vital spirit is “the innate Mummy, and hath its seat in the midst of Mans heart” [CH6.60]. To keep it, “they must seek after not the Elementall, but that Celestiall Heat of the Sun and Moon that dwelleth in the more incorruptible substance that is to be found under the Globe of the Moon” [CH6.60]. It is “prepared and made up into a medicine and most pleasant meat” and taken by mouth [CH6.60]. Such a physician might keep youth to the point of death and, “were it not for the wages of sin, withstand death” [CH6.61].

Paracelsus cleared

“Paracelsus calleth it the Element of FIRE” [CH6.62]. The author gives his meaning “that of right he may here be vindicated from the unjust calumny wherewith he was branded”: the Balsom of Nature may be preserved “to the last gasp of Life without any sicknesse, paine and griefe” [CH6.62]. He was stopped by “being Poysoned in his body by his malicious and inhuman adversaries” [CH6.62]. The scoff rejected is “that he by this his medicine would presumptously prepare himselfe an entrance and way to the immortall health of his body” [CH6.62]. The author says Paracelsus “came to the Naturall Terme of his Life by an untimely and violent death of a draught of Poyson” [CH6.62], though natural death was earlier “a full extinction, consumption and dissipation of that spirit” [CH6.56]; the strain rests on that wording (wording-dependent).

The true long life: union with the Centre

The author turns to God. “GOD is the Centre of all Creatures”, and “Blessednesse is in unity, not in the circumference; in Christ, not in the world, is Peace and the Rest of Soules.” [CH6.63]. Whoever is united to that Centre, “he waxeth young rather than old: And this is the true Long Life of the Cabalists, and of Paracelsus”, “the true Enochean Life” [CH6.64]. This reading of long life is Stated by the text itself [CH6.64]. The margin foresees those “who exclaime that this interpretation is forced and far setch’d” [N296]. Earlier, long life meant the body’s life by the Medicine [CH6.55; CH6.60–61]; here it is used in a second sense (Interpreted).

Whoever is not united must “be separated from the Light and Life by the second death” [CH6.65]. The true philosophy is to pass into God, “whereby thou mayest be transform’d, and made (as it were) a God” [CH6.66].

The Adept Phylosophers “alwayes look upon these transitory things with the left eye, but on Heaven with the right” [CH6.67]. Their mind “hath ever been far off and estranged from the Cavill above mentioned” [CH6.68]; Interpreted: the cavil is the scoff of CH6.62. They see all in “one simple (intuitive vision or) sight from within, a kind of an Essentiall touch of the Divinity”, a beauty “incomprehensible to the Old Creature” [CH6.68]. Hermes “doth also acknowledge in his Monade” that the knowledge and contemplation of the one and perfect is “a sacred, Heavenly, and hid silence” [CH6.68]. The author says what it is (Stated): “It is the intimate vision of GOD, and the Intuitive knowledge of GOD” [CH6.68].

He names a spiritual death of the Saints, “which the Jewes call the kisse of Death”, “if the fulnesse of Life may be called death” [CH6.68]. “We must die to the World, Flesh, Blood, and the whole Animall Man” [CH6.68].

Nothing is used twice. “the most beautifull Nothing (that is, the shadow and region of Darknesse and Death)” is cast behind [CH6.68]; the margin says “Whatsoever is not God, is Nothing” [N299]. Yet the friends of God cast themselves “into the Sea of Nihilitude or Nothingnesse” [CH6.68]. Several readings: two different nothings, the world left behind and the self given up, which the words on going “out from themselves” support [CH6.68]; or one word used of unlike things. It rests on the word (wording-dependent).

Two reasons life may be prolonged

“it is repugnant neither to Nature nor Reason that a Man may prolong his life beyond the common ordinary age of Men”, for two reasons [CH6.69].

First, “in NATURALS there is no certaine appointed Terme apparent what day we shall die” [CH6.70]. “I speak here Phylosophically of the Naturall death, which is onely the wasting of the Naturall Moysture and Heat, as may be seen in a burning lamp”, not of the Fatall death God has set [CH6.70]. “As God hath numbred our hairs, so also doth he reckon our years, leaving them in our power”, and a long life is not displeasing to him, yet always short of the term “of DIVINE PREDESTINATION which is unknown to mortalls” [CH6.70]. The margin speaks of “a two fold death, from the Iliad or first principle, and from the Ens or Being” [N307].

Second, God created the Medicine for life. “Paracelsus saith that the death which is from the resolution of the Iliad may be hindred by the industry of the Physitian, but that which is from the Ens or Being cannot” [CH6.71]. Life is fed like fire with wood, but “we being destitute of wisdome know not that wood wherewith our life might be cherished and prolonged” [CH6.71]; wood here stands for the unknown means (Interpreted, from the fire comparison). Adam lived long “by the help of Secrets and by Wisdome which was revealed but to few” [CH6.71], and had the Medicine “in his Family, as Lactantius witnesseth” [CH6.71]. “the Flood did not wast the things that grow, but wash’d away our wisdome of knowing them” [CH6.71], and “These most secret of secrets have ever been hid from the common sort of them that professe Phylosophy” [CH6.71].

Two ways life is shortened

“the life is broken off, or shortned, two manner of wayes” [CH6.72].

First, by the MIND, through diseases “as Inchantment, Imagination, Estimation, Influence; Superstition” [CH6.73]. Against them avails “onely Faith which is able to resist them”, or magical means, and these diseases “which only Adept Physitians know, are healed without the help of Natural Physick” [CH6.73]. The mind has “a kind of a hidden Virtue, of changing, attracting, and binding that which it desireth”, like the Loadstone that does “(as it were) bewitch spiritually and invisibly” [CH6.73]. Their “malignant Astra’s must be averted by a supernaturall cure and magicall help into something else” [CH6.73]. “Godlinesse is the chiefest remedy” [CH6.73].

Second, “by the Being, as by Entall or Corporall diseases” [CH6.74]. “Health is preserved by fastings, and a moderate Diet is the best Governance to prolong the life” [CH6.74]. Each kind needs its own cure: “Corporall medicines doe no good to mentall or supernaturall distempers” [CH6.74].

The conclusion: what he imparts, and to whom

Not to “wander wider then the bounds of a Preface”, the author closes [CH6.75]. He has imparted “so far as was lawfull, and so much as was allowable by God” to “the Children of Learning and Heirs of Wisdome” [CH6.76]; the margin bids “Read them over and over, & over again, and againe” [N313]. For “it concerns the publick good to make known also in publick writing what belongs to the publick” [CH6.76].

He tells of “above ten years” in the families “of ESNE at Lyons in France, and in that of BAPPENHEIMIE in the Segniory of Mareschall”, of turning over “the BOOK of NATURE (in which every region is a leaf)”, and of his patron, “Heroick Prince CHRISTIAN ANHALTIN” [CH6.76].

He says plainly that he leaves things unhandled and obscure (Stated): “I have not handled all things here in this place, to avoyd prolixity”, and “It is provided for by the Philosophical law that some tedious things should be left obscure to young Schollers and for intelligent & wise men to find out” [CH6.76]. “He that can receive it let him receive it” [CH6.76]; to the sincere reader “the doors and Inner rooms are unlockt” [CH6.76]. “A few things or words are sufficient for him that understandeth.” [N317].

Critics should publish their own, “for surely Physick is not yet come to the Limit of perfection”, and become Bees in a union “divorcing the multitude and abstaining from all fornication” [CH6.76]. Interpreted, narrowest: these words are figurative, following the Bees, for workers kept apart from the crowd. He hopes “that Ancient, True, & Philosophical Medicin” may “be restored to its lost lustre & ancient splendor” [CH6.76]. The Preface ends: “Which that holy Triunity grant, whose unspeakable Name be blessed for ever and ever” [CH6.77].

The Preface as a Whole

What the Preface is

The heading addresses Prince Christian Anhaltin [OPEN.0], named at the end as the author’s patron [CH6.76]; the first words address the “COURTEOUS READER” [OPEN.1]. The aim is to break an old silence about secrets and to “set before thee two of the most excellent Spagyrik Mysteries” [OPEN.2; OPEN.3]. Before the Remedies, the author lists what is “to be explained by a Philosophicall Phisitian” [OPEN.23]: six numbered heads and an epilogue [OPEN.24–30]. Our reading is that the chapters follow this list: head III, that Phisick is fetched out by fire, matches Chapter III’s heading [OPEN.26; CH3.0].

Its sections, in order:

  • The opening, with no chapter number: why the silence is broken, the critics expected, the heads to come [OPEN.2; OPEN.10; OPEN.23].
  • Chapter I, Of the True Physick: this Physick is God’s gift and rests on the agreement of the great world with man [CH1.1; CH1.4].
  • The Microcosm, or Little World, Man: man’s generation, his parts and his invisible, sydereal side [MICRO.0; MICRO.14; MICRO.31].
  • Chapter II: the true Physick lies wrapped like a kernel in rinds, in the Spirits of things, and last in the Word of God [CH2.1; CH2.7; CH2.13].
  • Chapter III: how it is got out of its bark and rind and prepared by Fire [CH3.0].
  • Chapter IV: how Physick works on the body and cures [CH4.0].
  • Chapter V, “The Duty of Natures Minister, the Physitian” [CH5.0]: what manner of physician this Physick requires [OPEN.28].
  • Chapter VI: the one chief Medicine of the most ancient philosophers; it closes the Preface [CH6.0; CH6.75].

The argument in its own order

The opening: the Admonitory Preface

The author reports the ancient Philosophers’ rule against those who “prophane the Secrets” only to set himself against it [OPEN.2]. His ground is God, who “communicateth his light, without respect or envy, to the good and bad” [OPEN.2], so he will no longer hide his Talent “by an impious silence” [OPEN.2]. What he offers he says he proved by his own hand and trial, unlike writers who wrote from others’ report, for “no man is further to be believed, then as every one findeth by his own proper experience” [OPEN.3; OPEN.4; OPEN.5]. One thing he sets above all he offers: “that universall and most famous Medicine which the most ancient wise Philosophers had from the beginning of the world” [OPEN.6].

He then foresees how each sort will take his publishing. The Mysticall Hermetick Philosophers will accuse him of “breaking open the seale of Chymistry” [OPEN.10]; he answers that better Treasures lie in the true Cabala, to be shown to all when in the third Age “Elias the Artist” comes [OPEN.10]. The spurious Theophrasteans will accuse him, since they lose their cheating trade [OPEN.11]. The milder Galenists will embrace this light, the dull ones scorn it, and the Scholastic Doctors hold titles got by flattery or bribery [OPEN.12; OPEN.13; OPEN.14; OPEN.15]. Some old physicians become scholars of Chymistry, lest they be buried in “the dead shadows of meer outsides” [OPEN.17; OPEN.20]. The list of heads follows [OPEN.23–30].

Chapter I. Of the True Physic

The true Physick “is the mear gift of the most high God” [CH1.1], to be learned from God, not from the Heathens [CH1.1]. Its foundation “is according to the agreement of the lesser World Man with the greater and externall world” [CH1.4]: the outer world is a glass in which man is seen, the two one in essence and inward form [CH1.5]. Everything is fourfold, so there is a fourfold Man and a fourfold Medicine [CH1.6; CH1.12]. The Astrum is “the secret Forger” of the form of things [CH1.15]; each thing carries its own, and the outer stars neither infuse virtue into things nor necessitate man [CH1.16].

All compound bodies resolve into Three Principles, Mercury, Sulphur and Salt [CH1.19]. The first life of medicines must die so that a second life is reached through Putrefaction and Regeneration [CH1.37]. Diseases come not from the four humours but from the Seed and the Three First [CH1.39; CH1.40], and are cured not by contraries but by the Arcana, which the Chymist draws out of the last matter into the first [CH1.43].

The Microcosm, or Little World, Man

The section opens on the Divine lesson “KNOW THY SELFE” [MICRO.1]. The notion of all things is created in us, and the mind is perfected “not by study and paines; but by patience and submission” [MICRO.3]. True philosophy should be grounded on the Scriptures, not on the Heathen [MICRO.8]. Man is “the bond or buckle of the world” [MICRO.11], made of the Quintessence of all created things [MICRO.12], the centre and circle of the creatures [MICRO.13]. He has three parts, a mortal Body, a Sydereal Spirit and an immortal Soul [MICRO.14]; the body from the Firmament is the soul’s vehicle, knitting soul and earthy body together [MICRO.17].

The invisible, sydereal man is also called the imagination [MICRO.32], “the Gate of Wonders”, which breeds the Plague and “brings sicknesse and health” [MICRO.33]. The Gabalisticall part of Magick rests on three Pillars: prayer in sacred silence, natural faith and a strongly exalted imagination [MICRO.37–40]. The author breaks off, since these matters “require a larger narration then can be made of them at this time” [MICRO.44], and names those who cleared this foundation, Paracelsus first and Severinus next [MICRO.45].

Chapter II. Where that true Physick is to be found

Lower things have two natures, “a fift Essence and the dreggs”, which fire can separate [CH2.1]; the medicine lies as a kernel in its rind, got out by the Chymists’ art [CH2.1]. It is a specific virtue planted in the Seeds by the Word [CH2.2]; cures come from Spirits, not bodies [CH2.3; CH2.7]. Remedies come from the three shops, Vegetable, Animal and Mineral [CH2.4; N134], among them gold, which few can make potable [CH2.5]. Every disease has its medicine, except disease from God’s anger [CH2.8].

Herbs are “but a signe onely of the Word signified” [CH2.13]; the Word of God “is that which healeth all things” [CH2.13]. The Magus heals by Characters and Stones, the true Cabalist by Mind, Faith and Prayer [CH2.21; CH2.22]. The medicine is placed in kernel, Seeds, Spirits, Word and God [CH2.1; CH2.2; CH2.7; CH2.13; CH2.14]. Our reading is, narrowest, that the medicine in herbs belongs to the Visible Physick and that hid in God to the Invisible [CH2.14]. The chapter ends by honouring God, and the Physitian as his minister [CH2.23–28].

Chapter III. How the True Physic is got out and prepared by Fire

Nothing below is so noble that it has no poison, yet where there is venom there is virtue [CH3.1]; the margin names God’s severe Justice the venom and his Mercy the Physick [N163]. Physicians are ministers “to seperate, not masters to compose” [CH3.6]; “Physick and Chymistry cannot be separated” [CH3.7]. In each of Animals, Vegetables and Minerals the Three First are sought [CH3.9]. Since the fall, impurity is joined to the pure roots, and Nature gives nothing pure [CH3.11; CH3.12].

The Separatory art parts the kernel and Quint-Essence from the husks [CH3.14]; medicines so prepared bring many advantages [CH3.16–19], and prepared minerals and stones excel vegetables [CH3.21]. All things come from one seed and Principle, quickened by one universall Spirit [CH3.28–31]. Two readings stand of where the Elixer’s matter lies: in man [CH3.35–37], or in every thing, man among them [CH3.34; CH3.38]. Galen lacked the art of separation; Theophrastus would have shown more had his failings been borne with [CH3.40; CH3.41]. The chapter judges that “this wicked generation is not worthy of such medicines” [CH3.42] and closes with a prayer [CH3.46].

Chapter IV. How Physick works upon the body

Physicians dispute whether contraries are cured by contraries, as Hippocrates holds, or like by like, as Paracelsus holds [CH4.1]. The author answers that both may be received; Paracelsus faults not Hippocrates but Galen, who referred cures to bare qualities [CH4.2]. Nature cures, and “the Physitian is but the minister or servant” [CH4.3]; the medicine helps the body’s Balsom “like a coadjutour or privy counsellour” [CH4.3]. Contrary qualities do not cure, because “Nature is not a quality but a vertue” [CH4.6]. The worst diseases spring from the Seed or Astra’s, not the humours [CH4.6], and “Physick is a Spirit (not a body)” [CH4.6].

Man’s Salt, Sulphur and Mercury are preserved by those of the great world, and diseases are sorted by these three [CH4.7–10]. Paracelsus cures ulcers from Salts by Salts [CH4.9], the next paragraph all ulcers by Mercury [CH4.10]; two readings stand: the rules disagree, or, wording-dependent, two words name one remedy [CH4.9–10]. The chapter ends with two Prague stories, of a swallowed knife and swallowed flints [CH4.13; CH4.14].

Chapter V. The Duty of Nature’s Minister, the Physician

The physician must be “free from every kind of Sect” [CH5.1], yet all sects may be admitted [CH5.3]. Secrets remain for wise men to find [CH5.5], so neither the Ancients nor Theophrastus is wholly rejected or embraced [CH5.7]. The physician is first an interpreter of Nature [CH5.8], second a Spagirus who separates pure from impure [CH5.12]. Paracelsus is praised as “the true Monarch of Physicke” [CH5.12] and defended against the charge, which the author reports to reject, that his cures were the Devil’s [CH5.15]. Every true theologian is a philosopher, and every true philosopher a theologian [CH5.19].

Tartar, the excrement of meat and drink coagulated in man [CH5.35; CH5.44], explains the first of Wimpeneus’s three respects in which the Paracelsians cure the greatest diseases [CH5.31; CH5.32]. Paracelsus is to be read, not as an oracle [CH5.51]; he wrote in a magical style for the children of Wisdom [CH5.54]. Physician and surgeon should be one [CH5.56], and Old and New Physick may be reconciled [CH5.61]. Eight reasons are given why cures fail [CH5.68–81]; diseases from God’s wrath yield only to a celestial and spiritual cure, by repentance and faith [CH5.82].

Chapter VI. Of that one only chief Medicine

The chief Medicine is “this most secret Secret of secret Secrets” [CH6.1]. To pious and holy philosophers it assures renewed youth and perpetual health [CH6.2]; its seekers must want wisdom, not wealth [CH6.6; CH6.12]. Few reach it, and only by God’s will [CH6.17; CH6.18]. The philosophers swore never to write it plainly, and spoke only to those who understood allegorically [CH6.21]. The author attacks impostors with golden promises [CH6.22–25] and was granted to “see and taste it, at some Great mans house” [CH6.37]; one drop of a liquor restored a dying man in one night [CH6.38].

The Medicine is named a Fift Essence, “the root of life in Nature” [CH6.51]. It consumes the disease, brings the Humors to their true Temperament, and can keep a man sound to his natural death [CH6.53; CH6.54]. Paracelsus is cleared of the scoff that he sought bodily immortality [CH6.62]. The true long life is also union with God, the Centre [CH6.63; CH6.64]. The chapter weighs how life is prolonged and shortened [CH6.69–74] and ends with a prayer to the Triunity [CH6.77–78].

Process or not, and what the Preface carries

The division made from the readings finds that the Preface does mark a process. This is its finding, not something the book says of itself. Its basis is the heading of Chapter III and head III of the opening list: Physick got out and prepared by fire [CH3.0; OPEN.26]. It adds the Separatory art [CH3.14] and “The Chymicall way of Subtiliating Extracting, and Separating” [CH3.40]. Its end is “that pure and Christiline matter may be administred to our bodies” [CH3.7]. For the chief Medicine, the division cites “the greatest miracle of Nature wrought by Art” and the “Moyst way of the Ancients” [CH6.38]. It also cites the joining of Sun and Lunary “with the help of Art and Nature”, in a matter the philosophers swore never to write plainly [CH6.21], and a margin note on Gold animated by the Salt of Niture, which it interprets as the chief Medicine’s work (note-only) [N263]. It counts two units.

The True Physic. This is Physick of the kind got out and prepared by Fire [CH3.0]. Chapter I treats it as God’s gift, resting on the agreement of the great and little worlds [CH1.1; CH1.4]. Chapter II places it covered as a kernel in rind, in the Seeds and in the Spirits of things [CH2.1; CH2.2; CH2.7], and Chapter III tells how Chymistry frees it, the trial being made by Fire [CH3.7; CH3.14]. “By its onely power all diseases are cured”, the author says, if it be “rightly prepared, and separated from all impurity” [CH1.3]. Chapter IV says it works by helping Nature’s own Balsom, not by contrary qualities [CH4.3; CH4.6]. See the True Physic in Plain Terms and the True Physic in Plain Language.

That one only chief Medicine of the most ancient Philosophers. The opening sets it above every medicine the author offers [OPEN.6], and the list of heads calls it “commended of many, possessed, yea seen, or believed of very few” [OPEN.29]. Chapter VI says it purges impurity from metallic and human bodies and restores health [CH6.1]. It “reduceth to the true Temperament by making equall the unequall”, and keeps the fore-qualified man who uses it prudently sound to his natural death [CH6.54]. The author says he saw a dying man saved “by giving one onely drop of that Latex or liquor” [CH6.37; CH6.38]. It is named a Fift Essence, “altogether one and the same with the spirit of our body” [CH6.51]. See that one only chief Medicine of the most ancient Philosophers in Plain Terms and that one only chief Medicine of the most ancient Philosophers in Plain Language.

What is covered, and how

The Preface begins by breaking a rule of silence [OPEN.2], yet keeps silences of its own and says where. The author breaks off contemplations that “require a larger narration then can be made of them at this time” [MICRO.44]. In Chapter V he says “nor may I in this unfit place openly speak more of these Mysteries” [CH5.24]. Of the chief Medicine he withholds “the Magicall and super Celestial use” [CH6.1]. Of what he saw at a great man’s house he writes: “Which I forbear to English” [CH6.37]. He has imparted “so far as was lawfull, and so much as was allowable by God”, and “some tedious things should be left obscure to young Schollers and for intelligent & wise men to find out” [CH6.76].

Covered speech is named and defended. Chapter I speaks of difficulties “wrapt up in aenigmaticall obscurity” [CH1.12]. The writings that witness to the chief Medicine are “woven of so many Hieroglyphicall, Magicall, and Mathematicall Coverings” [CH6.1]. Of Paracelsus the author says: “He concealed his mysteries under vulgar and various names”, and warns against taking the similitudes for the truth itself [CH5.55]. A verse runs: “Arts wrapt in Riddles safe doe lye” [CH5.26]. The true philosophers made their knowledge known only to “such who had an understanding allegorically”, and may give names at their pleasure [CH6.21]. Some figures the text explains itself: the kernel and rind [CH2.1], the Spagirus [CH5.12], the bolted Gate [CH6.19]. Others the pages read only as figures, such as Vulcan for fire [OPEN.3]. The joining of the blood of the Sun and the Dew of the Lunary stays in covered words [CH6.21].

To whom are things given? The opening says the Gates of Wisdome “ought alwayes to stand open to ingenious men” [OPEN.2], and the margin says “Only they that are worthy shall understand these things” [N018]. Mineral virtues are disclosed to enlightened men [CH2.5], Physick is discerned by the wise man only [CH4.6], and the Hermetic philosophers left their secrets to sworn disciples [CH5.28]. The chief Medicine is promised to the pious [CH6.2], and even one who holds the key may be refused, “because the Gate was bolted, that is, the Divine Grace and Benediction was denyed and did resist” [CH6.19]. The slothful will never finger these secrets, and there is little danger of the profane grasping them [OPEN.10].

The pages record strains in the book here. Chapter V praises Paracelsus as one who “knew and publisht” hidden secrets [CH5.12], yet asks whether any man with his knowledge would tell it to every man [CH5.27]. It says “A Secret indeed is to be concealed, it belongs onely to God to reveale it” [CH5.28], and later calls foolish the belief “that a Secret looseth its vertue when tis made known” [CH5.62]. Our reading is, with that page, that the later passage rejects the belief about lost virtue, not the dangers of disclosure [CH5.28; CH5.62]. Chapter II calls it folly to think the Creator would hide remedies from men [CH2.8], beside the gift of making Gold potable, which few men have [CH2.5]. The page’s narrowest reading is that God does not hide the remedies, and men fail to know them [N139].
