Our reading is that the Preface also speaks of time in two ways. The opening says the Treasures are “to be manifest indifferently to all in due time by the light of Grace and Nature” [OPEN.10]. Chapter V says secrets are ordained for times and nations [CH5.5]. Chapter III says God “will never suffer those Secrets to be known, especially in these corrupt and perilous times” [CH3.42].

What the Preface leaves open

  • Whether the Preface’s gift is to all or to some. It defends giving like the Father who shines on good and bad, and also says only the worthy will understand [OPEN.2; OPEN.10; N018].
  • What “mingled together in a fit place” means: Mysteries hidden among other matter so the unworthy pass them by, or simply set in their proper place [OPEN.10].
  • What the Light of Nature is: an inward teacher, the likeness of world and man, or a gift from above. The pages give all three as defensible, and Chapter V sets it beside the liberty of human judgement [CH1.1; CH1.5; CH1.18; CH5.1].
  • Whether the medicine’s virtue comes from within or is infused from Heaven. Chapter II says it is not from without, then that God infuses it and Heaven gives force [CH2.3; CH2.11; CH2.12]. The page gives two readings: the passages deny different causes, or they stand against each other.
  • Where the matter of the philosophers lies: in man, or in every thing [CH3.36; CH3.38; N180].
  • Whether the three points of “the Magicall and Cabalisticall Art” are Mind, Faith and Prayer, or herbs, stones and words [CH2.22; N154].
  • How the rules for ulcers fit, by Salts in one place and by Mercury in the next [CH4.9–10].
  • What the “one thing” is that prolongs life in Chapter V: the universal medicine, or the Quint-Essence of Gold [CH5.68; CH5.36].
  • What the Gold animated by Salt of Niture is: the road the seekers leave to the greedy, or a covered speech [CH6.11; N263].
  • What the capitals in the words the author forbears to English are: hidden letters, or misprints [CH6.37].
  • Whether Nothing names two things, the world left behind and the self given up, or one word used of unlike things [CH6.68].
  • What the Magical and super Celestial use of the chief Medicine is, which the author leaves unsaid [CH6.1].

The True Physic in Plain Terms

This page sets out what these passages of the Admonitory Preface say about the True Physick, the subject of the author’s first heads (OPEN.24, OPEN.25, OPEN.28) and of the chapters CH1 to CH5. One wish in CH6 is read beside it, and the Description of the little World Man (MICRO) is added to its first head. Stated. means the text says it outright. Interpreted. marks my inference, with the passages it rests on. Several readings. gives each defensible reading with its basis. Plain. means the words mean what they plainly say.

Process or not

The division made from the readings of the whole Preface finds that the book marks a process here. Its basis is the head of a whole chapter, “How the True Physick, which is covered bark and rind, is to be got out, and rightly prepared by Fire” [CH3.0], announced as “That it is to be fetcht out, and prepared truly, by fire.” [OPEN.26]. It names the operation “this Separatory art which teacheth rightly to discerne and seperate the Good from the Bad, the Profitable from the Unprofitable” [CH3.14], and three operations in a row as a way: “The Chymicall way of Subtiliating Extracting, and Separating” [CH3.40]. Their end is “that pure and Christiline matter may be administred to our bodies” [CH3.7]. The finding is the division’s, not a claim the book makes about itself.

In brief

  1. Stated. The author gives the True Physick heads of its own: what it is, where it lies hid, how it is got out and prepared by Fire, how it works, and what Physitian it requires [OPEN.24; OPEN.25; OPEN.28; CH1.0; CH2.0; CH3.0; CH4.0].
  2. Stated. Every natural body resolves into three and only three, Mercury, Sulphur and Salt, and this is seen in a Spagiricall resolution [CH1.19].
  3. Stated. The True Physick is contained in the three Families of Nature. It is not a matter of qualities but a specifix vertue hidden in the Seeds and planted by the Creator [CH2.2].
  4. Stated. The True Physick is covered with bark and rind and is got out and prepared by Fire. Without Chymicall Phylosophy all Physick is lifeless [CH3.0; CH3.8].
  5. Stated. Nature is a vertue, not a quality, and is helped by vertues rather than qualities. Nature is the healer, and the Physitian is her servant. The body of the simple is cast away and only its vertue or Astrum is taken [CH4.6].
  6. Stated. The most hurtful diseases spring from the Seed or Astra’s and the invisible Spirits of the Three principles [CH4.6]. As there are Three from which all Physick proceeds, there are three sorts of diseases; Ulcers, mattery sores and Inflammations are cured by Mercury, Salt and Sulphur [CH4.10].
  7. Stated. The Physitian must be a good SPAGIRUS, one who can separate the pure from the impure [CH5.12].
  8. Interpreted. The Description of man is joined to the Physick because the Physick works on man as on a little world made of all things [OPEN.24; CH4.6; MICRO.12].
  9. Stated. The author says he has not handled all things, and that by the Philosophical law some things are left obscure [CH6.76].

What the author sets before the reader

Stated. The author offers the reader “two of the most excellent Spagyrik Mysteries” [OPEN.3]. He says of them “whose preparations, after the mistakes of expence, time and labour, have all of them been first proved by my own handy experience” [OPEN.3].

Stated. He refuses “much lesse to fill up a huge Volume with the copies of large Receipts” [OPEN.3], and the margin says “The greatest Receipts the greatest Deceits for the most part.” [N005]. The many descriptions of the men he sought out were “all reduced into one onely, and that same first proved by my own Chymicall triall” [OPEN.3].

Several readings. He examined what he gathered “by the help of Vulcan” [OPEN.3]. (a) Vulcan stands for the work of fire, as in “the Alchymy of Vulcan” [CH3.8], and the “School of Vulcan” stands in the same paragraph as medicines “which must be prepar’d and try’d by Fire” [CH6.76]. (b) A margin note says “Astrum, Vulcanus and Archaeus are the same thing” [N123] (note-only).

Interpreted. Two things here point ahead to the True Physick: preparations proved by his own trial, and many descriptions reduced to one tested one. A margin note in CH3 makes a like contrast when it sets “the Spagyrick Physick duely prepared” against the mixtures in the shops [N172] (note-only). That OPEN.3 already speaks of the True Physick is my inference.

Stated. The author expects that the Mysticall Hermetick Philosophers “will accuse me for breaking open the seale of Chymistry” [OPEN.10].

Stated. His answer is that the heirs of true wisdom will get far better Treasures “by asking, seeking, and knocking with unwearied paines and watchfullnesse” [OPEN.10], while the sluggish despisers of these secrets will never finger them “by reason of their indisposition and unfitnesse to manuall operation” [OPEN.10].

Stated. He also says that the mysteries are “mingled together in a fit place” [OPEN.10]. See the open questions.

What that Physick is

Stated. The first head reads “What that Phisick is which cureth mens diseases, and few Phisitians know it.” [OPEN.24]. The chapter that answers it is headed “CHAP. I. Of the True Physick.” [CH1.0]. The book spells the word both Phisick and Physick.

Stated. CH1 speaks of the Three Principles: “No body compos’d by Nature can by any dissolving skill be parted into more or lesse then Three, viz. Into Mercury or liquor, Sulphur or Oyle, and Salt” [CH1.19]. The author grounds this in creation: “For the Holy Triunity when it spake that Triune word FIAT created all things Triune, as in a Spagiricall resolution is plainly to be seen.” [CH1.19].

Stated. The margin note glosses the Spagiricall resolution: “i. e. A seperation of purity from impurity, or truth from falshood.” [N052] (note-only). Like words return in the duty of the Physitian, who must “seperate the pure from the impure” [CH5.12].

Stated. The author says that “The Spagyricks can make this plaine by visible experience and uncontroulable certainty.” [CH1.19]. His example is green wood, which has three moistures. Of these, “these two are consumed by the fire; The third, viz. the Salt is unctuous, very little, thin and lasting, and remains in the ashes” [CH1.19].

Stated. Each of the Three has its office: “The Salt by coagulation gives Solidity, Colour and Tast to all bodies” [CH1.19]. Sulphur “gives the Body Substance and Transmutation” [CH1.19]. Mercury is the one “which like the Elixir giveth the vertues” [CH1.19].

Stated. The same Three make man’s body: “Thus also the Earth as it is indued with that threefold substance of Salt, Mercury and Sulphur, is the cause of the materiall body of man” [CH1.19].

Several readings. The margin says “When the Salt or Mummy is spent things” breed nothing but worms; a gap stands in the next word [N053] (note-only). (a) Mummy is a second name for Salt, the lasting part; the note joins the two names with “or” [N053]. (b) Mummy is the preserving power in a body, as in CH4, where Nature heals itself “by the innate Mummy” [CH4.6] and is called “the Internall Mummy or inward Balsom” [CH4.6].

Interpreted. The names Mercury, Sulphur and Salt do not mean only the common substances. The text explains them as liquor, oil and the lasting part that remains in the ashes, and finds them in green wood. Sulphur in wood is only “like brimstone” [CH1.19]. So the names stand for three parts that every body has.

The little World Man, added to the first head

Stated. The author joins this Description to the first head: “Whereunto is added a perfect Philosophicall Description of the little World Man, almost forgotten and unknown.” [OPEN.24].

Stated. Man is made of all things: “Man is compacted of all bodies and created things” [MICRO.12], and “So that all the properties of Animalls, Vegetables and Mineralls entred into him” [MICRO.12]. In the same way, “all Externall things are nothing else but the Body of Man” [MICRO.24].

Stated. Man has two bodies: “Thus Adam had two bodies, that is, a visible Elementall, and an invisible Sydereall body.” [MICRO.31].

Stated. The Astra’s are not “the seaven visible coales of Heaven which are but the bodyes of the Astra’s” [MICRO.32]. In growing things “the seeds of any graine or Wheat is the Astrum, viz. the invisible body” [MICRO.32], and “Bodyes are nothing else but the Excrement of the Astra’s” [MICRO.32].

Stated. A margin note gives the Astra’s a part in disease: “The Astra’s send the plague into us through our skin as the Sun doth his beams thhrough the glasse.” [N124] (note-only).

Interpreted. The Description bears on the Physick. The Physick is “a certaine specifix vertue concealed in the very Seeds” [CH2.2]. The seed is the Astrum, the invisible body [MICRO.32]. In the cure “the vertue Heaven or Astrum of it only to be taken” [CH4.6]. CH4 gives the reason for the name: “Man is therefore called a Microcosm or little world, because the whole world preserveth, nourisheth and cureth him.” [CH4.6]. That this is why the Description is added is my inference [OPEN.24].

Where that True Physick lies hid, and may be found

Stated. The head reads “Where that True Phisick lyeth hid, and may be found.” [OPEN.25]. The chapter that answers it is “CHAP. II. Where that True Physick is to be Found.” [CH2.0].

Stated. It is found in all three kinds of natural things: “in those three Families of Nature, the Vegetable, Animall, and Minerall” [CH2.2], and the author adds that from these “are commonly had medicines enough to preserve the health and cure all bodily diseases” [CH2.2]. In them “there is contained that True and specifix Physick of materiall distempers” [CH2.2].

Stated. It is not a quality. It “doth not consist in the four outward, naked, superficiary and Relollaceous qualities” [CH2.2], and the author notes that Theophrastus “learnedly discourseth” of this [CH2.2]. It is something else: “it is a certaine specifix vertue concealed in the very Seeds, naturally proceeding without art” [CH2.2]. The Creator planted it in every growing thing by his Word, “whereby all things were brought out of darknesse into Light” [CH2.2].

Interpreted. The vertue proceeds “without art” [CH2.2], yet the next head says it must be got out by Fire [CH3.0]. On my reading, nature makes the vertue and the art only frees it from its cover.

Stated. The CH6 wish asks that each worker be set “to find out one out of those Three Families of Nature” [CH6.30].

Interpreted. Man, made of the properties of “Animalls, Vegetables and Mineralls” [MICRO.12], is on my reading cured from the same three Families [CH2.2; CH6.30].

How it is got out, and rightly prepared by Fire

Stated. The head reads “How the True Physick, which is covered bark and rind, is to be got out, and rightly prepared by Fire” [CH3.0].

Interpreted. Bark and rind are figures for the outward body of a thing. The text says that in Galen’s days “they knew not how to separate the bark and husk from the kernell” [CH3.40]. In CH4 it says “the body or Earth of Simples is to be cast away” [CH4.6]. The narrowest reading is that the bark and rind are the gross body, and the kernel is the vertue.

Stated. Chymicall skill is needed for any Physick: “for without this Chymicall Phylosophy all Physick is but livelesse” [CH3.8]. The author blames physicians who “use to leave the preparation of their medicines most commonly to some carelesse and covetous Apothecary to the great dammage of their Patients” [CH3.8].

Stated. The operation and what it yields: “By this artificall resolution of bodyes the propertyes which before layd in the compositions of them are now brought to light.” [CH3.8]. Hence “Therefore a Physitian should be exercised much in this true Analysis and vitall Anatomy of bodies” [CH3.8], because “there is no constant quality of any body which is not to be found either in the Salt, or Mercury, or Sulphur of the same body” [CH3.8].

Stated. A margin note gives “Reasons why the Spagyrick Physick duely prepared is to be preferd before other Hetroclite mixtures in the shops.” [N172] (note-only). The first is that many sick people “will take it more easily and willingly” [CH3.17].

Stated. The text names the way: “The Chymicall way of Subtiliating Extracting, and Separating being imperfect, was not much used in Galens dayes” [CH3.40]. Galen, as the author reports his words, tried to find a way “to separate the contrary parts of vineger as well as of milk” [CH3.40], and “might have obtained his desire if he had been well skild in the Distillatory Art” [CH3.40].

Stated. The margin gives the three in milk: “Whey, Butter and Cheese are the three first things of milk, for all Terresterity is Salt.” [N181] (note-only). The text says Galen would gladly have served Theophrastus (Paracelsus) “to separate the three first (qualities) in vineger” [CH3.40], “but especially to learn the preparations of those high Magisteryes and Elixirs” [CH3.40].

Several readings. The vinegar passage calls the three first “(qualities)” [CH3.40] (wording-dependent). (a) The word in brackets is an added gloss, and the three first are the Three of CH1.19, as the milk note’s “all Terresterity is Salt” suggests [N181]. (b) Qualities is meant as the word says, though CH2.2 denies that the True Physick lies in qualities [CH2.2].

Stated. Men neglect it for gold: “almost all men have a burning desire after making Gold” [CH3.43] “but regard not the due preparation of their Medicines” [CH3.43]. The margin names “four Pillars of Physick, Phylosophy, Alchymistry, Astronomy, and the Physicall vertue or medicine.” [N185] (note-only).

Stated. The author calls the Art “this Divine Spagyrie, the most wonderfull and approved Art” [CH3.45], which “hath been very much disparaged by the ruder sort” [CH3.45]. Yet abuse does not condemn it: “the bettter the thing is, the more destructive is the abuse of it” [CH3.45].

How, and with what Vertue, Physick worketh upon Mans body

Stated. The head reads “How, and with what Vertue, Physick worketh upon Mans body and cureth his Diseases” [CH4.0].

Stated. Like, not contrary: “medicines should not be contrary to the grieved part, but very agreeable to it” [CH4.6]. The medicine has the same outward Nature as the part affected has inward, “that the Internall Nature which stands in need of it may both be strengthned and succoured by the superabundance of that Externall Nature” [CH4.6]. “When the fruits of the Earth, Aire, Water and Fire of the Microcosm are sick, they must be restored by fruits like themselves of the Macrocosm.” [CH4.6].

Stated. Nature heals, and the Physitian serves her. Nature “it selfe healeth its own infirmities by the innate Mummy” [CH4.6], and “when her own inward strength fayleth shee is to be supplyed with outward helpe by her servant the Physitian” [CH4.6]. Sometimes it is best “to leave the ooperation to the sole Archaeus or Art of Nature” [CH4.6]. The margin adds “The Inward Physitian doth the work, when the Naturall Physitian faileth.” [N188] (note-only, wording-dependent).

Stated. Vertue, not quality: “Nature is not a quality but a vertue” [CH4.6], “for without vertue the quallity is dead and wholly accidentall” [CH4.6]. Opium helps in the plague “not by the coldnesse of Opium, but but by the Specifick venemous vertue of the Opium” [CH4.6]. “Thus Nature destroyeth one poyson by another, it subdueth a weak evill by a stronger” [CH4.6].

Interpreted. The author also allows contraries: “But Contraryes cure because they piece up the defects and wants” [CH4.6]. On my reading this does not clash with like curing like. What must not be contrary is the medicine’s Nature to the part’s Nature. What may be contrary is a vertue set against the vertue of the disease, as with opium. The text itself draws the line between vertue and quality [CH4.6].

Stated. Where diseases come from: “Roots of diseases in the body of Man are neither hot nor cold” [CH4.6]. The most pernicious of them “do not spring out of the matter of the body, or out of the four Humours, but from the Nature of the Seed or Astra’s and Invisible mechanick Spirits of the Three principles” [CH4.6]. “These Forgers and Invisible Astra’s of diseases were not knowne to the Ancients.” [CH4.6].

Stated. So the medicine must be a spirit as well: “Physick is a Spirit (not a body) which the Magician or wise man only can discerne” [CH4.6]. “the body or Earth of Simples is to be cast away” [CH4.6], because “it is necessary that the life should worke upon the life, and the Spirit upon the Spirit by sepration of the impure body” [CH4.6]. “This is the true and lively Anatomy.” [CH4.6].

Interpreted. Here CH4 ties back to CH3. Casting away the body of the simple and keeping its vertue or Astrum is what the bark-and-rind head of CH3 promises. The “true and lively Anatomy” [CH4.6] matches “this true Analysis and vitall Anatomy of bodies” [CH3.8].

Stated. The cure goes to the root: “when the Centre, Root, and Seed of diseases are pluckt up and removed the worke is done” [CH4.6]. “That Physitian which cureth by complexion is like him that would extinguish the hurtless flame and let alone the fire in the coals” [CH4.6].

Several readings. Whose words these root sayings are. Margin notes beside them name Paracelsus and his writings: “Paracelsus in the Tincture of medicines.” [N189], “In his Book of ancient Physick.” [N191]. (a) The words are the author’s own, and the notes point to where Paracelsus says the same. (b) They are Paracelsus’s words, as the notes beside them suggest [N189; N191].

Stated. The Three of CH1 return as a rule of cure: “As there are Three from which all Physick proceedeth, Mercury, Sulphur, Salt; so likewise there are three sorts of diseases, Mercuriall, Sulphurean, Saline” [CH4.10]. “All Ulcers are cured by incarnating Mercury; All mattery gluish sores by Salt; All Inflamations by Sulphur.” [CH4.10]. He adds “This needs no Reasons to confirme it.” [CH4.10].

What manner of Physitian that Physick doth require

Stated. The head reads “What manner of Phisitian that Phisick doth require for its Minister.” [OPEN.28].

Stated. “Secondly, a Physitian must be a good SPAGIRUS” [CH5.12], “one that can seperate the pure from the impure, and restore his Patient to health by a wise Alchymicall preparation” [CH5.12]. The margin says “A Philosopher and Physitian spring one out of another” [N202] (note-only).

Stated. The Art is older than Paracelsus, “though he were not the Auther of that Art” [CH5.14]. He sought to restore “the True and Philosophicall Physick which none in his time assayed to restore” [CH5.14], which had been “lost and obliterated for many years” [CH5.14].

Stated. Others followed him, and the author hopes that their writings will help their readers so that they “might by a daily practice of Piety fetch out that huge Talent and Treasury which is hid and shut up within themselvs” [CH5.20].

Several readings. How CH5.20 relates to the True Physick. (a) The inward Treasury is a further good that the author sets beside the Physick. These men’s study is the Physick, and their writings lead to the inner life. (b) The inward fetching out is the same course as the outward one, getting out the hidden, read in man. The words “fetch out” [CH5.20] and “hid” [CH5.20] echo the heads “fetcht out” [OPEN.26] and “lyeth hid” [OPEN.25].

Stated. Who may give Physick. Many physicians “cannot indure that Physick with its preparations should premiscuously be imparted to all sorts of men” [CH5.62]. The author calls the belief behind their fear “a foolish conceit of the frantick vulgar sort, who think that a Secret looseth its vertue when tis made known” [CH5.62]. Yet “neither God nor Nature hath made every man a Physitian” [CH5.62]. The margin adds “Paracelsus would not have Physick be concealed.” [N227] (note-only).

Stated. The ground beneath it is faith: “The best and chiefest Foundation of Physick is faith in God, and love to our neighbour, if this be wanting all Art is nothing worth.” [N227] (note-only).

The work wished for, and the author’s closing words

Stated. The author wishes the Great Ones would bestow some part of their wealth “upon men piously and consciensciously learned and skilfull in Chymistry” [CH6.30], setting each “to find out one out of those Three Families of Nature” [CH6.30], so that “they might fetch out the most choyse Physicall Mysteries” [CH6.30], “separating them by Fire into their Three First” [CH6.30].

Interpreted. This wish repeats the whole course of the True Physick in its own words: the Three Families of CH2, the fetching out and the Fire of CH3, and the Three of CH1 and CH4. It stands in CH6 and speaks of Mysteries in the plural, not of the one chief Medicine that CH6 is headed for.

Stated. The cost of the work: the Prince supplied the author with necessary expences, “who was altogether unable to bear and undergoe so great a burden as all these Medicins come to, which must be prepar’d and try’d by Fire” [CH6.76].

Stated. Physick “is not yet come to the Limit of perfection, but many things remain to be discovered to future ages” [CH6.76]. Of the secrets by which life was lengthened, he says: “Wherefore we want nothing but the knowledge of Secrets, and their use.” [CH6.71].

Stated. What he holds back: he writes “so far as was lawfull, and so much as was allowable by God, have I imparted candidly” [CH6.76]. He also writes “I have not handled all things here in this place, to avoyd prolixity” [CH6.76], and “It is provided for by the Philosophical law that some tedious things should be left obscure to young Schollers and for intelligent & wise men to find out” [CH6.76].

Several readings. The closing hope is that “that Ancient, True, & Philosophical Medicin, which by reason of the long continuance and injury of time” [CH6.76] is not yet fully known may be restored. (a) This is the True Physick under another name: CH5.14 calls what Paracelsus restored “the True and Philosophicall Physick” [CH5.14], also lost through time. (b) It is the chief Medicine, which the author gives a head of its own in CH6.

Open questions

  • What the author means by mysteries “mingled together in a fit place”, and where they are mingled [OPEN.10].
  • Whether Vulcan in “by the help of Vulcan” means the work of fire or the inner spirit that a note equates with Astrum and Archaeus [OPEN.3; N123; CH3.8; CH6.76].
  • Whether Mummy is another name for Salt or the healing power of Nature [N053; CH4.6].
  • Whether the three first (qualities) of vinegar are the Three Principles or qualities [CH3.40; N181; CH2.2].
  • Whose words the root sayings of CH4.6 are, the author’s or Paracelsus’s [CH4.6; N189; N190; N191].
  • How the inward Treasury of CH5.20 relates to the outward getting out of the Physick [CH5.20; CH3.0].
  • Whether “that Ancient, True, & Philosophical Medicin” is the True Physick or the chief Medicine [CH6.76; CH5.14].
  • What the author holds back, having imparted only “so far as was lawfull” [CH6.76] and having “not handled all things” [CH6.76].

The True Physic in Plain Language

This page tells what the author’s preface says about the True Physic: the medicine that cures people’s diseases and that few physicians know. It draws on this book alone and retells it in everyday words; the page titled The True Physic in Plain Terms gives the evidence for each point.

Is this a process?

A reading of the whole preface finds that the book marks a process here. A chapter is headed by how the True Physic, wrapped in bark and rind, is got out and prepared by fire, as the preface’s headings also announce. The book names an art that separates the good from the bad, and three steps as one way: making fine, drawing out and separating. Their aim is a pure, clear matter to give our bodies. The finding is that reading’s, not a claim the book makes about itself.

In brief

  1. The True Physic has five headings: what it is, where it lies hidden, how it is got out by fire, how it works, what physician it needs.
  2. Every natural body has three parts.
  3. It is a power the Creator hid in the seeds of plants, animals and minerals, not a quality.
  4. Without chemistry all medicine is lifeless.
  5. Nature heals and the physician serves her, keeping only the medicine’s invisible body.
  6. There are three kinds of disease, each cured by one of the three parts.
  7. The physician must separate the pure from the impure.
  8. Our reading is that man, a little world made of all things, is described because the medicine works on him.
  9. Some things are left obscure on purpose.

What the author offers the reader

The author offers two of the finest secrets of the art of separating, each preparation first tested with his own hands after mistakes that cost him money, time and work.

He refuses to fill a huge volume with copies of long formulas; a margin note says the biggest formulas are mostly the biggest deceits. The many descriptions he gathered from the men he sought out he reduced to one, first tested by his own chemical trial.

He examined it with the help of Vulcan. Vulcan may stand for the work of fire, as the book speaks of the alchemy of Vulcan and of a school of Vulcan beside medicines tried by fire; or, on a margin note alone, for one spirit with the invisible body of things and nature’s own art.

Our reading is that his own tested preparations already point to the True Physic, as a later margin note sets medicine duly prepared by separation against the mixtures in the shops.

He expects the philosophers who kept these secrets in silence to accuse him of breaking open the seal of chemistry. His answer: the true heirs of wisdom will find far better treasures by asking, seeking and knocking, with tireless work and watchfulness, while the lazy who despise these secrets, unfit for work with their hands, will never lay hands on them.

He also says the mysteries are mixed together in a fitting place. What this means stays open.

What the True Physic is

The first heading asks what the medicine is that cures diseases and few physicians know; the first chapter answers it as the True Physic. The book spells physic two ways.

No natural body can be split, by any skill of dissolving, into more or fewer than three parts: mercury, or a liquid; sulphur, or an oil; and salt. The threefold God spoke a threefold word of making and so made all things threefold, as the art of separating plainly shows.

A margin note explains this separating as parting purity from impurity, or truth from falsehood. Like words return in the physician’s duty to separate the pure from the impure.

Those skilled in the art, the author says, can show this by plain experience and with certainty. Green wood has three moistures: fire consumes the watery and the oily one; the third, the salt, greasy, very small, thin and lasting, remains in the ashes.

The salt sets bodies firm and gives solidity, colour and taste; the sulphur gives substance and change; the mercury gives things their powers. The earth, endowed with these three, is the cause of man’s material body.

A margin note says that when the salt, or mummy, is spent, things come to nothing but worms; part of one word cannot be read. Mummy can be read two ways. In one, it is a second name for the salt, the lasting part, as the note joins the two. In the other, it is the preserving power in a body: the fourth chapter says nature heals herself by her inborn mummy, and calls nature herself the inward mummy or inward balm.

Our reading is that mercury, sulphur and salt are not only the common substances but three parts every body has: the book explains them as a liquid, an oil and a lasting part, and finds them in green wood.

Man as a little world, added to the first heading

The author adds to the first heading a description of man as a little world, almost forgotten and unknown.

Man is made of all bodies and created things; the properties of animals, plants and minerals entered into him; and all outward things are nothing but man’s body.

Adam had two bodies: a visible one of the elements, and an invisible one from the stars.

What the book calls the stars are not the seven visible lights of heaven, only their bodies, but the invisible body of all things. The seed of a grain of wheat is such an invisible body; visible bodies are nothing but what these invisible bodies cast off.

A margin note says these invisible bodies send the plague into us through our skin, as the sun sends its beams through glass.

Our reading is that this description is added because it bears on the True Physic: the medicine is a power hidden in seeds, a seed is an invisible body, and in a cure only the medicine’s invisible body is taken. Man is called a little world, the book says, because the whole world keeps, feeds and cures him.

Where the True Physic lies hidden

The second heading asks where the True Physic lies hidden and can be found; the second chapter answers.

It lies in plants, animals and minerals, which commonly give medicines enough to keep health and cure all bodily diseases. In them is the true and particular medicine for bodily disorders.

It does not lie in the four outward qualities, as Theophrastus learnedly shows. It is a particular power hidden in the very seeds, arising by nature without art, which the Creator planted in every growing thing at the beginning by the word that brought all things out of darkness into light.

Our reading is that nature makes this power and the art only frees it from its cover: it arises without art, yet the next heading says it must be got out by fire.

In the last chapter the author wishes each worker set to search one of these three kinds of things.

Our reading is that man, made of the properties of animals, plants and minerals, is cured from the same three.

How it is got out and prepared by fire

The third heading says the True Physic is covered with bark and rind and must be got out and rightly prepared by fire.

Our reading, the narrowest, is that bark and rind are the outward body of a thing and the kernel its power: in Galen’s day they could not separate the bark and husk from the kernel, and the body of simple medicines is to be thrown away.

Without chemical skill all medicine is lifeless. The author blames physicians who leave their medicines to a careless, greedy apothecary, to their patients’ great harm.

Breaking bodies down brings to light the properties that lay in their make-up. So a physician should practise much at this true analysis and living anatomy of bodies, for every lasting quality of a body is found in its salt, mercury or sulphur.

A margin note gives reasons to prefer medicine duly prepared by this art to the mixtures in the shops; the first is that many sick people will take it more easily and willingly.

The way, making fine, drawing out and separating, was imperfect and little used in Galen’s day. Galen, as the author reports him, sought to separate the contrary parts of vinegar, as of milk, and could have done so had he known distilling.

A margin note gives the three first things of milk as whey, butter and cheese, and says all that is earthy is salt. Galen, the author says, would gladly have served Theophrastus (Paracelsus) to learn to separate the three first parts of vinegar, and above all to make those high preparations and elixirs.

The book calls these three first parts qualities, in brackets: two readings, both resting on a single English word of the translation. In one, the bracketed word is an added gloss and the three are the three parts of the first chapter, as the milk note’s earthy salt suggests. In the other, it means qualities, though the second chapter denies that the True Physic lies in qualities.

Almost everyone burns to make gold but does not care to prepare medicines properly. A margin note names four pillars of medicine: philosophy, alchemy, astronomy, and the medicine’s own power.

The author calls this art divine, most wonderful and proven. Rough people have much disparaged it, yet abuse does not condemn it: the better a thing, the more harmful its abuse.

How medicine works on the body

The fourth heading asks how, and with what power, medicine works on the body and cures its diseases.

Like cures like. Medicines should agree with the sick part: the medicine has outwardly the nature the part has inwardly, so the inner nature in need is strengthened by the abundance of the outer. When what earth, air, water and fire bring forth in man falls sick, it is restored by what they bring forth in the great world.

Nature heals, and the physician serves her. She heals her own weaknesses by what the book calls her inborn mummy; when her inner strength fails, her servant the physician gives outward help, and sometimes it is best to leave the work to nature’s own art alone. A margin note adds that the inward physician does the work when the natural physician fails; this rests on the note alone and on its exact words.

Power, not quality. Nature is a power, not a quality, and without power a quality is dead and merely accidental. Opium, very cold, saves some in the plague not by its coldness but by its particular poisonous power, stronger than the plague’s hidden poison. So nature destroys one poison by another, a weak evil by a stronger.

Our reading is that contraries curing, because they make up what is lacking, does not clash with like curing like. The book itself parts power from quality: the medicine’s nature must not be contrary to the part’s, but one power may be set against the disease’s power, as with opium.

Where diseases come from. Their roots are neither hot nor cold. The most harmful spring not from the body’s matter or the four humours, but from the nature of the seed, or invisible bodies, and from invisible spirits of the three parts that work like craftsmen, unknown to the ancients.

So the medicine must be a spirit too, not a body, which only the wise can discern. The body of simple medicines is thrown away, for life must work on life and spirit on spirit once the impure body is separated off: the true and living anatomy.

Our reading is that this is what the bark-and-rind heading promises, and the living anatomy of the third chapter.

The cure goes to the root. When the centre, root and seed of a disease are pulled out, the work is done. A physician who cures by what the book calls complexion puts out the harmless flame and leaves the fire in the coals.

Margin notes beside these sayings name Paracelsus and his writings, so they may be the author’s own, with the notes pointing to where Paracelsus says the same, or Paracelsus’s.

The three parts return as a rule of cure: three kinds of disease, as all medicine comes from mercury, sulphur and salt. All ulcers are cured by mercury, all sticky running sores by salt, all inflammations by sulphur; this, the author says, needs no reasons.

What kind of physician this medicine needs

The fifth heading asks what kind of physician this medicine requires to serve it.

He must be skilled in separating the pure from the impure and restore the patient by a wise alchemical preparation. A margin note says the philosopher and the physician spring from one another.

The art is older than Paracelsus, who was not its author. He sought to restore the true and philosophical medicine, which no one in his time tried to restore, lost and wiped out for many years.

Others followed him. The author hopes their writings will help readers, by daily practice of piety, to draw out the great treasure hidden and shut up within themselves.

This can be read two ways. In one, the inner treasure is a further good beside the medicine. In the other, it is the same course as drawing out the medicine, read in man, as the words drawing out and hidden echo the headings on the medicine.

Who may give medicine. Many physicians cannot bear that medicine and its preparations be shared with all sorts of people. The author calls the belief behind their fear, that a secret loses its power once known, a foolish notion of the common crowd. Yet neither God nor nature has made every man a physician. A margin note adds that Paracelsus would not have medicine kept hidden.

The ground beneath it all is faith: the same note calls faith in God and love of one’s neighbour the best foundation of medicine, without which all art is worth nothing.

The work the author wishes for, and his closing words

The author wishes the great ones of his time would spend part of their wealth on men pious, conscientious and skilled in chemistry, each set to search one of the three kinds of natural things, to draw out the choicest medical secrets and separate them by fire into their three first parts.

Our reading is that this wish repeats the whole course of the True Physic: three kinds of things, drawing out by fire, three parts. It speaks of secrets in the plural, not of the one chief medicine of its chapter.

The work is costly: the Prince paid the author’s necessary expenses, as he could not bear alone the burden of all these medicines, which must be prepared and tried by fire.

Medicine has not yet reached perfection; much remains for later ages to find. Of the secrets by which life was once lengthened, the author says we lack only the knowledge of them and their use.

He holds some things back: he has shared only as far as was lawful and allowed by God, has not dealt with everything, to avoid being long-winded, and by the law of philosophers some hard things are left obscure to young students, for the wise to find out.

He hopes the ancient, true and philosophical medicine, not yet fully known through the long wear of time, may be restored. Our reading is that it is either the True Physic, as the book calls what Paracelsus sought to restore the true and philosophical medicine, or the chief medicine, which has its own heading in the last chapter.

What the book leaves open

  • What the mysteries mixed together in a fitting place are, and where they are mixed.
  • Whether Vulcan means the work of fire or the spirit a margin note makes one with the invisible body of things and nature’s own art.
  • Whether mummy is a second name for the salt or nature’s own healing power.
  • Whether the three first parts of vinegar are the three parts of every body or qualities.
  • Whose words the sayings about the root of disease are, the author’s or Paracelsus’s.
  • How the inner treasure readers draw out of themselves relates to getting the medicine out of things.
  • Whether the ancient, true and philosophical medicine of the closing hope is the True Physic or the chief medicine.
  • What the author holds back, having shared only as far as was lawful and not having dealt with everything.

That One Only Chief Medicine of the Most Ancient Philosophers in Plain Terms

This page sets out what the author’s Admonitory Preface says about one unit: the Medicine named in the sixth chapter’s head, “Of that one onely chiefe Medicine of the most Ancient Phylosophers” [CH6.0]. His list of heads gives it the sixth place [OPEN.29]; he sets it apart in his opening [OPEN.6]; the fifth chapter names its limit [CH5.68]; the sixth treats it to the close of the Preface [CH6.76]. Each point is marked Stated where the text says it outright, Interpreted where the point is this page’s inference, or Several readings where the lines allow more than one. Points that rest on a margin note alone are tagged note-only; points that rest on a single English word of the translation are tagged wording-dependent.

Process or not

The division made from the readings found that the book marks a process, and that this Medicine is the second of its two units, a Medicine “wrought by Art” [CH6.38], apart from the True Physick because its result differs: one Medicine that heals all diseases of the humours [CH6.42]. For this unit it rests on the Moyst way [CH6.38], the joining the True Phylosophers require [CH6.21], the note on Gold [N263] and the Kings Highway [CH6.48]. It records that the steps of this course are covered [CH6.21; CH6.38].

In brief

  1. Stated. Nothing in Nature is more choice than the medicines the author offers, except this one, which the most ancient Philosophers had from the beginning of the world [OPEN.6; OPEN.29].
  2. Stated. Against the appointed term of life it is of no effect [CH5.68; CH6.38; CH6.42].
  3. Stated. The author never read of a greater gift of wisdom given to man; by it all things may be done [CH6.1].
  4. Stated. Few or none find it; God gives it to whom he will [CH6.18; CH6.44].
  5. Stated. Those who knew it swore never to write it plainly [CH6.21; N281].
  6. Stated. The True Phylosophers hold that it is reached only when the fat or blood of the Sun and the Dew of the Lunary are joined into one body, if God please [CH6.21].
  7. Stated. One drop of it raised a man at the point of death to full health in one night [CH6.38].
  8. Stated. It heals all diseases of the humours, except at the appointed term or where God sends the disease as punishment [CH6.42].
  9. Stated. All the virtues of the Creation are heaped together into one mass by ingenuity and Art [CH6.48–49].
  10. Stated. The Phylosophers of Hermes school say it may be the Fift Essence; the author says God created this Fift Essence as the root of life [CH6.50–51].
  11. Stated. Holy men used it before the flood, and Adam had it, as Lactantius witnesseth [CH6.71].
  12. Stated. At the close the author says the Ancient, True and Philosophical Medicine is not yet fully known, and may be restored [CH6.76].

Set apart from what the author offers

Stated. In all Nature “there doth not lye hid any more choyce and efficacious, then these chief secrets of the more hidden Medicines here offered” [OPEN.6], “except only that universall and most famous Medicine which the most ancient wise Philosophers had from the beginning of the world” [OPEN.6]. His list of heads: “Of the universall and chiefe Medicine or Phisick of the most Ancient Philosophers, commended of many, possessed, yea seen, or believed of very few.” [OPEN.29]

Interpreted. This page reads the exception as a ranking: his own medicines are not this Medicine, and he puts it above them. [OPEN.6]

Its limit: the term of life

Stated. “No Medicine can preserve the corruptible body from death, the wages and punishment of sin; but there is one thing that hindereth corruption, reneweth youth, and prolongeth the short life, as in the Patriarchs” [CH5.68]; yet “in this case the universall and chiefest medicine is of no effect, because no industry or skill of Artist or Nature can perpetuate the wedlock of life and death” [CH5.68].

Several readings. (a) The “one thing” [CH5.68] is this Medicine: the sixth chapter says “Many holy men used this universall Medicine before the flood” [CH6.71]. (b) It is something else: the same paragraph names “the universall and chiefest medicine” [CH5.68] separately. [CH5.68; CH6.71]

Stated. A weak Naturall Heat must be strengthened by “the macrocosmicall Heat of Sun and Moon, if it could be got, even the most simple matter which the most Great God generated of the spirit of the world” [CH5.44].

Interpreted. The sixth chapter says almost the same of this Medicine: “it is the most simple Matter which the Best and Greatest Lord generated out of the spirit of this world” [CH6.51]. On that likeness this page reads the fifth chapter’s “most simple matter” [CH5.44] as this Medicine, and “if it could be got” [CH5.44] as saying it is hard to come by. [CH5.44; CH6.51]

The greatest gift of wisdom

Stated. The sixth chapter opens on “that chief and universal Phylosophical Phisick, than which a greater gift of wisdom we never read that divine bounty bestowed on man” [CH6.1]: “this most secret Secret of secret Secrets, by which even wonders, yea all things may be done” [CH6.1]. A margin note: “This occult Minerva of Phylosophy & this only most precious Jewell is inestimable.” [N253] (note-only).

Stated. It works first on “the Plannets of inferior Astronomy, whose imperfection and drosse it causeth to vanish by piercing them with its most powerfull impressions” [CH6.1], “for it segregateth all extraneous Sulphureity and impure Terrestreity from metallick and Humane bodies” [CH6.1]; second on “the languishing and lost health of the Human body, by its fiery vigour” [CH6.1].

Interpreted. The bracket speaks of “metallick and Humane bodies” [CH6.1], and the second field is the human body; this page reads “the Plannets of inferior Astronomy” [CH6.1] as a covered name for metallic bodies. The chapter elsewhere names “the metamorphosis of Mettalls” [CH6.50]. [CH6.1; CH6.50]

Stated. A third use he passes over: “to say nothing now of the Magicall and super Celestial use” [CH6.1], “when the Gonetick influence of the beames of the Sun and Moon and the fourth revolution is finisht upon its native Earth” [CH6.1].

Interpreted. This page reads the finished “fourth revolution” [CH6.1] as a stage; the narrowest reading is that more than one turn goes before this use. [CH6.1]

Stated. As witnesses he gives the consent of learned men, “the ocular inspectation and certification of many of our time” [CH6.1], and their writings, “which are woven of so many Hieroglyphicall, Magicall, and Mathematicall Coverings” [CH6.1].

Who finds it

Stated. “among so many thousands that seek, there is not one to whom the doors of Nature are unlockt” [CH6.18], since God “giveth his gifts to whom he will, and withdraweth them from whom be pleaseth” [CH6.18]. A margin note: “The one only and true way to all Secrets is to have recourse to God the author of all good” [N261].

Several readings. Men “seek the Kingdome of God, and study to finde out the Celestiall Stone in the last place” [CH6.18]. (a) The Celestiall Stone is this Medicine, to be sought after the Kingdom of God; a note says “in this Stone the universal Nature of things shineth forth” [N283]. (b) Men put both last, and that is why none finds the doors unlocked [N261]. A later note uses the name for what purifies the mind: “By the access of this beam, ray, or celestiall Stone all drosse is purified” [N298] (note-only). [CH6.18; N283; N298]

The sworn secret

Stated. “This is that Secret laid up and buryed in the most secret Treasury of the Mind and Memory” [CH6.21], which the more occult Phylosophers “have in a wise and ingenious craft covenanted and sworn they would never write it plainly in any Book” [CH6.21], nor made known “to any but such who had an understanding allegorically.” [CH6.21] Being Lords of Things, “they should also have power to dispence or give Names at their pleasure” [CH6.21].

Interpreted. This page reads the freedom to “give Names at their pleasure” [CH6.21] as a warning that the names of this Medicine and its matter cannot be taken at their face. [CH6.21]

What must be joined

Stated. The True Phylosophers (QUOTED: True Phylosophers) affirm “that none can attaine the desired end” [CH6.21] “before the fat or blood of the Sun and the Dew of the Lunary, be joyned into one body by the circular wheel of the Elements with the help of Art and Nature in the form of an Hexagon” [CH6.21], “which can never be done except the most High God please” [CH6.21]; “they sleight all particulars, which naturally doe altogether want the virtue of tincturing, unlesse they proceed from the first fountaine” [CH6.21].

Interpreted. The narrowest reading: the making needs two things joined into one body, by both Art and Nature; and the “particulars” [CH6.21] lack the virtue of tincturing unless they come from the first fountain. [CH6.21]

Stated. A margin note beside it: “On this the hinge of the whole busines turneth” [N263], “as Gold animated by the Salt of Niture is made the chief subject of the Phylosophers metallick Physicks” [N263] (note-only).

Several readings. (a) The note names the chief subject of this Medicine. (b) Its “metallick Physicks” [N263], in the plural, may be a class of works of which this is one. (c) Its “Gold” [N263] may be a plain name, or one given “at their pleasure” [CH6.21] (note-only, wording-dependent). [N263; CH6.21]

The wonder seen: one drop

Stated. The paragraph tells of “the greatest miracle of Nature wrought by Art” [CH6.38], among “the various and manifold metamorphosis of the Inferior Astronomy made in the cold” [CH6.38], “the Moyst way of the Ancients not as yet intensly exalted to the eye of the Basilisk” [CH6.38]: “by giving one onely drop of that Latex or liquor” [CH6.38], “a man desperately sick and at the point of death was recovered” [CH6.38], “restoring him to firme and perfect health in one night” [CH6.38]. In that Latex “the whole world was centred” [CH6.38].

Interpreted. This page reads the Moyst way, “made in the cold” [CH6.38] and “not as yet intensly exalted” [CH6.38], as marks of the course of making: a moist way, worked cold, with a further exalting to come. A note calls something “The Physophycall Basilisk” [N274] that “like Lightning suddenly and unawares burneth up any imperfect mettall whatsoever” [N274], and the Medicine works “like as in Lightning and the eye of a Basilisk” [CH6.50]; so the Basilisk’s eye may name the Medicine at its height of power. [CH6.38; N274; CH6.50]

Stated. “this Kingly medicine, and the Empresse of all other, causeth Humane Bodies immediately and as it were miraculously to rise againe from what desperate diseases soever” [CH6.38], “if God hath not otherwise decreed” [CH6.38]; “this whole new regenerate world is able by vertue of its Regeneration to renew the little old corruptible world Man” [CH6.38], “till the appointed Terme of death imposed on mortalls for their sin” [CH6.38].

Interpreted. The great world made new renews the little world; the narrowest reading is that the Medicine holds the whole world in small, as the Latex in which “the whole world was centred” [CH6.38]. [CH6.38]

All diseases of the humours

Stated. “All diseases proceeding from the corruption of humors, how great and desperate soever their cures be, are healed by this universall medicine” [CH6.42], “except the party be at the utmost Terme appointed him by the supream Being; or the disease be inflicted by God, besides Nature, as a punishment and speciall affliction” [CH6.42].

The Artist and God

Stated. The author approves what Lullius “rightly concluded” [CH6.44] (QUOTED: Lullius): “between the Artist and God the first cause, there ought to be an agreement without contrariety” [CH6.44]. He warns that “two publick Phylosophers of divers Nations in our time abusing the Gifts of God” [CH6.44] “came to dolefull and lamentable end” [CH6.44].

Several readings. A margin note: “The originall of the Philosophicall Magistery.” [N278] (a) The originall is the first way of keeping it, “the first Hapocraticall silence” [CH6.44], which most now break by boasting [N278]. (b) It is its source, God moving the Artist [CH6.44]. (note-only) [N278; CH6.44]

The road and the one mass

Stated. Many “have been perswaded that they should never attaine the same, either by other means or any Arts” [CH6.48], “but by a wonderfull and most abstruse comprehending of all the vertues of the whole Creation flowing and running together in one certaine masse” [CH6.48], which “in this Rode or Kings Highway and Phylosophical Reason is to be accomplished” [CH6.48]. The voice of this persuasion is UNCLEAR. The next paragraph: “All these spirituall virtues and active qualities being by great help of ingenuity and Art like the lesser world heaped up together and concentred into one masse” [CH6.49] bring “the knowledge of all Naturall things and Heavenly seerets, and a perfect operation” [CH6.49]. A margin note: “The defect of Nature must be supplyed by the industry of Art” [N280].

Interpreted. Read with the Latex, whose virtues were “by a wonderfull artifice invisibly gathered together in an heap” [CH6.38], these lines give the making as a gathering of all virtues into one mass by Art, where Nature falls short. [CH6.48–49; N280]

The Reason given by Hermes school

Stated. The Phylosophers “train’d up in Hermes school” [CH6.50], though “altogether dumb in keeping close the secret of the Art by a constant Taciturnity” [CH6.50], give this Reason (QUOTED: the Phylosophers of Hermes school): “the greatest Medicine, being artificially prepared with the help of wise Nature the Mistresse of Science, should be (or is) the Life and inlightning Light” [CH6.50]; “it may be the Essence of our Life: the Fift Essence compounded of the four Elements, in which are all the Elements actually” [CH6.50]; “This is so in respect of the four qualities of the body as the Heaven is incorruptible in respect of the four Elements.” [CH6.50]

Interpreted. In their words the Medicine stands to the body’s four qualities as the Heaven to the four Elements. Their hedge, “should be (or is)” [CH6.50] and “it may be” [CH6.50], this page reads as theirs, not the author’s doubt. [CH6.50]

The Fifth Essence and its names

Stated. The author: “The most High created this Fift Essence as the root of life in Nature for the preservation of the four qualities of the Humane body” [CH6.51], “as he did the Heaven for the preservation of the universe” [CH6.51]. He names it “the Glasse of God’s Mysteries, and the Miracle of universall Nature; the Fift Essence of the whole frame of the world, and the whole world Regenerated” [CH6.51], and “the Son of the Sun and Moon, who by his ascending into Heaven and descending into the Earth hath obtained all power of superior and inferior things” [CH6.51].

Stated. The paragraph goes on: “yea that it is truly the spirit of Life which pierceth through all other spirits” [CH6.51]; “there is need of the Balsom of life as a means to be sought from Externalls, by which the internall is restored” [CH6.51]; it “hath been altogether unknown to the Physitians of our time” [CH6.51]. From “yea that it is” [CH6.51] the voice is UNCLEAR: the author’s own, or the Reason of Hermes school continued.

Interpreted. “the Son of the Sun and Moon” [CH6.51] answers the joining of “the fat or blood of the Sun and the Dew of the Lunary” [CH6.21]: the narrowest reading is that the Medicine is named as the offspring of the two things joined. [CH6.51; CH6.21]

Stated. Margin notes add names: “The Tincture is the Fife Essence of the Microcosm” [N283]; Paracelsus (QUOTED: Paracelsus) calls it “the Defensive of old age, the Universall medicament, which like the invisible Fire consumeth all diseases” [N283] (note-only).

Interpreted. This page reads Stone, Tincture, Fift Essence, Magisterie and universall Medicine as names for one thing (note-only). [N283; CH6.51; CH6.49; CH6.42]

Long life, up to the term

Stated. The author speaks “Phylosophically of the Naturall death, which is onely the wasting of the Naturall Moysture and Heat, as may be seen in a burning lamp” [CH6.70], “not Theologically of the Fatall death and utmost Terme which God hath prefixt to every one” [CH6.70]. “God hath created the aforesaid Medicine for the preservation of life” [CH6.71]; it can “chase away from us all diseases which cause the naturall death, untill ultimate death the most Terrible of all Terrible things” [CH6.71]. “Many holy men used this universall Medicine before the flood, which Adam also had in his Family, as Lactantius witnesseth” [CH6.71]. A margin note, beside “Archid. of the Elixirs” [N307], in an UNCLEAR voice: “It is the conservation of the Human body from all accidentall corruption.” [N307] (note-only).

Interpreted. The fifth chapter denies any help against the term [CH5.68]; the sixth says this Medicine preserves life [CH6.71]. This page reads them as agreeing once the two deaths are kept apart: the Medicine holds off the natural death [CH6.70] up to the fixed term, and can do nothing against the term itself [CH6.42]. [CH5.68; CH6.70–71]

Not yet fully known

Stated. The author has imparted “so far as was lawfull, and so much as was allowable by God” [CH6.76], and hopes that “that Ancient, True, & Philosophical Medicin” [CH6.76] “is not yet fully known, may in a shorttime by the heavenly assistance, be restored to its lost lustre & ancient splendor” [CH6.76]. A margin note: “I have not spoken of so many things, but there yet more left to be spaken.” [N317]

Interpreted. “Ancient, True, & Philosophical” [CH6.76] answers the head’s “most Ancient Phylosophers” [CH6.0]; this page reads the closing words as about this Medicine, lost to his age and still to be restored. [CH6.76; CH6.0]

Open questions

  • Whether the Celestiall Stone sought “in the last place” [CH6.18] is this Medicine [N283; N298].
  • Whether the “one thing” [CH5.68] that prolongs life is this Medicine [CH6.71].
  • What the Sun’s fat or blood, the Dew of the Lunary, the wheel and the Hexagon name [CH6.21].
  • Whether the note on Gold [N263] speaks of this Medicine or of metallic works in general.
  • What the four revolutions and the Magicall use are [CH6.1].
  • What the Moyst way is, what its exalting adds, and who saw the one drop [CH6.38].
  • Whose persuasion the Kings Highway is [CH6.48].
