  • Whose voice speaks from “yea that it is truly the spirit of Life” [CH6.51] onward.

That One Only Chief Medicine of the Most Ancient Philosophers in Plain Language

This page retells in everyday words what the author’s preface says about one medicine: the single chief medicine he says the most ancient philosophers had. His list of topics gives it the sixth place, he sets it apart in his opening, the fifth chapter names its limit, and the sixth chapter treats it up to the close of the preface. The page titled That One Only Chief Medicine of the Most Ancient Philosophers in Plain Terms gives the evidence for each point.

Is it made by a course of work?

The study that divided the book into units found that the book marks a course of work, and that this medicine is the second of its two units: a medicine made by skill. It stands apart from the other healing art, which the book calls the True Physick, because it yields something different: one medicine that heals all diseases of what the book calls the humours. For this unit the study rests on a moist way of working, a joining of two things that the true philosophers require, a note about gold, and a road the book calls the King’s Highway. It records that the steps of this course are told only in covered words.

In short

The author ranks this medicine above all he offers himself; the most ancient philosophers had it from the beginning of the world. It does nothing once a person’s set time to die has come. He never read of a greater gift of wisdom given to man, and by it all things may be done. God gives it to whom he will, and almost no one finds it. Those who knew it swore never to write it plainly. The true philosophers hold that it is reached only when two things, the fat or blood of the sun and the dew of the Lunary, are joined into one body, if God pleases. One drop of it brought a dying man to full health in one night. It heals all diseases of the humours, except at the set time of death or where God sends the disease as punishment. All the powers of creation are heaped together into one mass by skill. The philosophers of the school of Hermes say it may be the Fifth Essence, and the author says God created this Fifth Essence as the root of life. Holy men used it before the Flood, and Adam had it. At the close the author says it is not yet fully known and may be restored.

Above the author’s own medicines

The author says nothing in nature is choicer or more effective than the medicines he offers, except the universal medicine the most ancient philosophers had from the beginning of the world. His list of topics says many praise it but very few own, see or believe in it. Our reading is that this is a ranking: his own medicines are not this one, and he puts this one above them.

Its limit: the set time of death

The book says no medicine keeps the body from death, the wages and punishment of sin. Yet one thing holds off decay, renews youth and lengthens life, as with the patriarchs. Against the fixed end of life even the universal medicine does nothing, since no skill can keep life and death joined forever.

Two readings of that one thing are open. It may be this medicine, since the sixth chapter says holy men used it before the Flood. Or it may be something else, since the same passage names the universal medicine separately.

The book also says weak natural heat in the body must be strengthened by the heat of sun and moon in the great world, if that could be had: a very simple matter God made from the spirit of the world. Our reading is that this matter is this medicine, since the sixth chapter says nearly the same of it, and that the doubt about having it means it is hard to come by.

The greatest gift of wisdom

The author never read of a greater gift of wisdom given to man. It is the most secret of secrets, by which all things may be done. A margin note alone calls it a priceless jewel.

It works first on what the book calls the planets of the lower astronomy, making their flaws and dross vanish, and it clears foreign and impure earthy matter from metal and human bodies. Second, it restores failing and lost health by its fiery strength. Our reading is that those lower planets are a covered name for metals, since the passage speaks of metal bodies and the chapter elsewhere of changing metals.

A third use, magical and above the heavens, the author passes over. It comes when the beams of sun and moon have worked and the fourth turn is finished upon its native earth. Our reading is that this fourth turn is a stage, with more than one turn before it. As witnesses he gives the agreement of learned men, what many in his time have seen with their own eyes, and their writings, woven of picture-writing and of magical and mathematical coverings.

Who finds it

Among thousands who seek, the book says, not one finds the doors of nature unlocked, for God gives and withholds his gifts as he will. A margin note says the one true way to all secrets is to turn to God.

The book speaks of men who seek the Kingdom of God and study to find the heavenly stone in the last place. Two readings are open. The heavenly stone may be this medicine, to be sought last, after the Kingdom of God; a note says the whole nature of things shines in this stone. Or men put both last, and that is why none finds the doors unlocked. A later note, on its own, gives the same name to a beam or ray by which all impurity is cleared away.

The sworn secret

The book calls this medicine a secret stored in mind and memory. The more hidden philosophers swore never to write it plainly, and told it only to those who understood speech in figures. As lords of things, it says, they may give names as they please. Our reading is that this warns the reader not to take the names of the medicine or its matter at face value.

What must be joined

The true philosophers say no one reaches the goal until two things, which they call the fat or blood of the sun and the dew of the Lunary, are joined into one body. This is done by the circling wheel of the elements, by skill and nature together, in a six-sided form, and never unless God pleases. They set aside particular things, which lack the power the book calls tincturing unless they come from the first source. Our reading, the narrowest, is just that: two things joined into one by skill and nature, and particular things lacking that power unless they come from the first source.

A margin note alone says the whole business turns on this, as gold brought to life by the salt of nature is the chief subject of the metal medicines of the philosophers. Three readings are open. It may name the chief subject of this medicine. It may speak of a class of metal medicines, this being one. Or its gold may be a plain name, or one given at the pleasure of the philosophers; this last turns on the single English word gold.

The wonder: one drop

The book tells of the greatest wonder of nature worked by skill, among the many changes of the lower astronomy made in the cold, by the moist way of the ancients, not yet raised to what it calls the eye of the basilisk. One drop of a liquid, in which the scattered powers of all heavenly and earthly bodies were gathered and the whole world was centred, restored a man at the point of death to full health in one night.

Our reading is that the moist way, worked cold and not yet raised, marks the course of making, with a further raising still to come. A note speaks of a basilisk that, like lightning, burns up any imperfect metal, and the book says the medicine works like lightning and the basilisk’s eye. So that eye may name the medicine at full strength.

The book calls it the kingly medicine and empress of all others, which raises bodies at once from any desperate disease, unless God has decreed otherwise. As a whole world made new, it can renew man, the small old world, until the death set for mortals for their sin. Our reading is that it holds the whole world in small, like the liquid in which the world was centred.

Every disease of the humours

This medicine heals all diseases that come from corruption of what the book calls the humours, however hard to cure. The exceptions: when the sick person has reached the last term God set, or when God sends the disease, beyond nature, as punishment.

The maker and God

The author agrees with what a philosopher he calls Lullius concluded: the maker and God, the first cause, must agree without conflict. He warns that two philosophers of his own time, from different nations, misused God’s gifts and came to a miserable end.

A margin note names the origin of what the book calls the Philosophical Magistery. Two readings are open, both resting on that note. It may be the first silence kept about it, which most now break by boasting. Or it may be its source, God moving the maker’s mind.

The road and the one mass

Many have been persuaded, the book says, that it is reached only when all the powers of creation flow together into one mass, on a road it calls the King’s Highway; whose persuasion this is stays unclear. Those powers, heaped together by skill like a small world, bring knowledge of all natural and heavenly secrets and a perfect working. A margin note says skill must make up for what nature lacks. Our reading, taken with the liquid whose powers were gathered in a heap, is that the making gathers all powers into one mass by skill, where nature falls short.

The reason given by the school of Hermes

The philosophers of the school of Hermes kept the secret in silence, yet gave a reason. In their words, the greatest medicine, made by skill with nature’s help, should be, or is, the life and enlightening light. It may be the essence of our life: a fifth thing made from the four elements and holding them all, which the book calls the Fifth Essence. It stands to the body’s four qualities as the heaven, which does not decay, stands to the four elements. Our reading is that the hedging words, should be or is and may be, are theirs, not the author’s doubt.

What it is and what it is called

The author says God created this fifth thing as the root of life in nature, to keep the body’s four qualities, as he made the heaven to keep the universe. He calls it the mirror of God’s mysteries, the wonder of all nature, the fifth essence of the whole frame of the world, the whole world made new, and the son of the sun and moon, who by rising to heaven and coming down to earth gained power over all things above and below.

The passage then calls it the spirit of life that passes through all spirits, says a balm of life must be sought from outside to restore what is within, and says the doctors of his time knew nothing of it. From here on it is unclear whether the author speaks or still gives the reason of that school.

Our reading is that the son of the sun and moon is the child of the two things joined, the fat or blood of the sun and the dew of the Lunary. Margin notes alone add names: one calls the tincture the fifth essence of the small world, and says Paracelsus calls it the guard against old age, the universal remedy that consumes all diseases like an unseen fire. Our reading, resting on a margin note, is that the stone, the tincture, the fifth essence, the Magistery and the universal medicine name one thing.

Long life, up to the set time

The author speaks as a philosopher of natural death, the using-up of the body’s moisture and heat, like a lamp burning out, not as a theologian of the fixed end God sets for each person. God made this medicine to keep life. It drives off every disease that causes natural death, until the last death, the most terrible of all terrible things. Many holy men used it before the Flood, and Adam had it in his household, as Lactantius testifies. A margin note alone, beside the name of a writing on elixirs and in a voice that is unclear, says it keeps the body from all chance decay.

The fifth chapter denies any help against the fixed end; the sixth says this medicine keeps life. Our reading is that they agree once the two deaths are kept apart: it holds off natural death up to the fixed end, and does nothing against that end.

Not yet fully known

The author has shared only as much as was lawful and God allowed. He hopes the ancient, true philosophical medicine, not yet fully known, may soon be restored to its old splendour with heaven’s help. A margin note says much more remains to be said. Our reading is that these closing words concern this medicine, since they echo the chapter’s title: lost to his age, still to be restored.

What the book leaves open

  • Whether the heavenly stone men seek in the last place is this medicine.
  • Whether the one thing that lengthens life, as with the patriarchs, is this medicine.
  • What the sun’s fat or blood, the dew of the Lunary, the circling wheel and the six-sided form stand for.
  • Whether the note on gold speaks of this medicine or of metal medicines in general.
  • What the fourth turn and the magical use are.
  • What the moist way is, what its further raising adds, and who saw the one drop at work.
  • Whose persuasion the King’s Highway is.
  • Whether the origin of the Magistery is the first silence or its source in God.
  • Who speaks from the words about the spirit of life onward.

Word List

The words the Preface uses in a sense of its own or returns to often, in today’s spelling, with the book’s own spellings. Each sense is marked: plain where the word means what it ordinarily means, veiled where the text uses it in a covered or second sense; Stated where the text itself says the meaning, Interpreted where the meaning is our reading of the lines given, several where more than one reading has its basis; and whose words they are (CROLL, the author; QUOTED: <name>, a voice he reports; UNCLEAR). note-only marks a sense that rests on a margin note alone, wording-dependent one that rests on a single English word of the translator’s. Each quote is followed by the reference of the line it stands in.

Adept

The book spells it: adept, Adept

  1. One who ‘hath attained the same’; a complete philosopher and keeper of God’s secrets, who will not part with it for any reward; adept physicians alone know the mental diseases. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “he that is adept or hath attained the same” [CH6.12]
    • “he that is an Adept and compleat Phylosopher, a keeper of Gods Secrets” [CH6.27]
    • “it is to no purpose to tempt an Adept Phylosopher with promises of rewards” [CH6.20]
    • “these diseases which only Adept Physitians know, are healed without the help of Natural Physick” [CH6.73]
  2. ‘Phylosophy Adept’, explained as long life free from infirmity until natural death. (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Paracelsus)
    • “This is Phylosophy Adept, which Paracelsus in the Tincture of Physicks explaineth them to be Long Life and free from all infirmity” [CH6.47]

Alterity

The book spells it: alterity, Alterity

  1. The state set against unity, into which man fell at the Fall, departing from unity; in Regeneration it is swallowed up of unity. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “after the transgression and fall from unity to alterity” [CH3.11]
    • “The Fall of Man and our great evill is to depart from Unity to Alterity.” [N212]
    • “through Regeneration (alterity being swallowed up of unity)” [CH6.35]

Anatomy

The book spells it: Anotomy, Anatomy, Anotmy, Anotomize, Anot

  1. the Anotomy of a man and dead corpses or Carkasses, called Locall, set below the Essentiated and Elemented Anotomy (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “It is not the Locall Anotomy of a man and dead corpses” [CH1.38]
    • “more necessary for a Physitian them that Locall Anot of Carkasses” [N069]
  2. the inward make of the great world and of man, which agree, so that the outward world read as an Anotomy shows man; the knowledge a physician needs to find disease and cure (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “duly compare the Anotomy of the World with the Anotomy of Man” [CH1.4]
    • “The outward World is a speculative Anotomy, wherein we may see, as in a glasse, the lesser World Man” [CH1.5]
    • “and can Anotomize them, hath attained the true knowledge of their child Man” [CH1.5]
    • “the Anotomy of him in his Nature is the Anotomy of the whole world” [CH1.5]
    • “The knowledge of every sound and perfect Physitian proceedeth from the true and full Anotomy both of the great and little world” [CH1.6]
    • “From the Outward he finds the frame of the Inward, viz. the true Anotomy of the great and little World” [CH1.26]
    • “the Essentiated and Elemented Anotomy of the World and man that discovereth the disease and cure” [CH1.38]
    • “Anotomy is the Basis of true Phisitians, Diseases and Things.” [N070]
  3. one shared make or kind: things of the same Anotomy answer each other, so that disease and medicine are one Anotomy and like cures like (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL · wording-dependent)
    • “As in Man, Man is the Anotomy of the disease, so also in Physick Man is the Anotomy of the Physick.” [CH1.38]
    • “every malady and medicine is of the same Physiognomy, Chyromancy and Anotomy” [CH1.12]
    • “Like things are said to be the Remedies of diseases because they are of the same Anatomy of Nature” [CH4.6]
    • “as Citizens of the same Anotmy” [CH1.17]
    • “because of the like Anotomy it is that Mummy will stop the bleeding in Man” [CH1.38]
  4. the Chymists’ work of taking bodies apart by art, separating the pure medicine from the impure body (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “it is of necessity that the same must be seperated from the impure Elements by the artificiall Anotomy of Chymists” [CH2.1]
    • “this true Analysis and vitall Anatomy of bodies” [CH3.8]
    • “the life should worke upon the life, and the Spirit upon the Spirit by sepration of the impure body” [CH4.6]
    • “This is the true and lively Anatomy.” [CH4.6]
  5. the Anotomy of Death, which is said to find entertainment in the whole State of our Life (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “the Anotomy of Death findeth entertainment in the whole State of our Life” [CH3.6]

Animal man

The book spells it: Animall Man

  1. Man as he is nothing but earth; named beside the world, flesh and blood as what one must die to, to possess within what is sought without and to get into the inmost secrets. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “if they were indeed dead to themselves, even to the whole Animall Man, who is nothing but EARTH” [CH5.20]
    • “We must die to the World, Flesh, Blood, and the whole Animall Man” [CH6.68]

Arcana

The book spells it: Arcana

  1. Hidden things drawn by the Chymist out of the last matter, neither cold nor hot, which remove diseases; Fift Essences and Magisteryes are of this sort. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “These Arcana or hid things are actually neither cold nor hot yet removeth all diseases” [CH1.43]
    • “Of this sort are the Fift Essences, Magisteryes, and the like” [CH1.43]
  2. The secret things of Nature that the better sort of philosophers search out. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “others far more excellent and more truly deserving the name of Philosophers investigate the Arcana and more secret things of Nature” [CH5.7]

Archaeus

The book spells it: Archaeus

  1. The inward worker in man that digests food and separates the pure from the impure; called the inborn Chymist God planted in man, whose instrument is the stomach; each of stomach, liver and kidneys has one. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “ariseth out of the last matter of the Meat by the Archaeus, or digestion of the Stomack” [CH5.33]
    • “the Stomack which is the instrument of Mans Archaeus, or the Internal, innate Chymist which God hath planted in Man” [CH5.34]
    • “But if the Archaeus of our stomack, liver or kidneys, which separateth the pure from the impure be infected” [CH5.44]
    • “Therefore the Archaeus of the stomack must destroy the Tartar of the Elements least it be turned into the Tartar of Man” [CH5.44]
  2. The Art of Nature, to which the work of healing may at times be left without any medicine. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Though sometimes it may be the best medicine not to use any medicine at all but to leave the ooperation to the sole Archaeus or Art of Nature” [CH4.6]
  3. One Spirit with Astrum and Vulcanus, without Reason, and divers as the forms of things are divers (margin note only; note-only). (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Astrum, Vulcanus and Archaeus are the same thing, & but one Spirit yet without Reason, & divers, as are the divers formes of severall things.” [N123]

Archetype

The book spells it: Archetype

  1. The original copy and first pattern of the world, which the text says is the immortal Wisdom of God; called the true Archetype, from whom virtue is drawn by holiness. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “he bears the Image of the Archetype or originall copy and patterne of the world, that is, of the immortall Wisdome of God himselfe” [MICRO.12]
    • “to some measure and number in the Archetype or first pattern” [MICRO.17]
    • “when by holinesse we draw vertue from him who is the true Archetype” [MICRO.36]
  2. The first of the three worlds man partakes of: the God-like world in God. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “of the Archetype or God-like world in God” [MICRO.24]

Artist

The book spells it: Artist, Artists

  1. The skilled worker in Chymicall employments, a master of his craft, whom the preparations require. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)

    • “To the Artists aforesaid (I say) who are their Crafts-masters” [OPEN.7]
    • “the labour & paines of the Artist” [N011]
    • “no industry or skill of Artist or Nature can perpetuate the wedlock of life and death” [CH5.68]
  2. The worker in the art, who must agree with God the first cause, moved by him to understanding. (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Lullius)
    • “between the Artist and God the first cause, there ought to be an agreement without contrariety” [CH6.44]
    • “should move the Intelligence or Soule of the Artist to a true Understanding” [CH6.44]
  3. God, the perfect maker of all things. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “the perfect Artist of all things, even the most high Creatour and glorious God” [CH1.1]
  4. A name given to Elias, who is to come in the third age and restore all things. My inference: the name marks him as the one who restores. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)

    • “who is to restore all things, shall come” [OPEN.10]

Ascendant

The book spells it: Ascendant, Ascendants

  1. The sign in Heaven that each living creature has; Ascendants are among the things over which Magick or Faith has power. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL · note-only)
    • “every living Creature hath its Ascendant signe in Heaven” [N125]
    • “Magick or Faith transplanting minds hath power over all Spirits and Ascendants.” [N128]
  2. The Ascendant as a supposed source of man’s conditions and manners, denied: these come from God through the breath of life. (plain · Stated · voice: UNCLEAR)
    • “For we receive not our conditions, properties, and manners from the Ascendant, nor from the Constellation of the Planets, but from the hand of God through the breathing in of the breath of life” [CH1.16]

Astral

The book spells it: Astrall

  1. Of diseases: springing from the Firmament, in man or from the impressions of the stars, as against elementary diseases; they have astral remedies. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “There are other diseases whereof the most part are Astrall or Firmamentall” [CH1.40]
    • “which spring out of the Firmament of Man” [CH1.40]
    • “those acute and pernicious Astrall diseases, the Pleuresie, Plague.” [CH5.42]
    • “which diseases proceeding from the impressions of the Stars the universall medicine powerfull expelleth” [CH5.42]
    • “but also those Chronicall, Astrall and fixed diseases which by many are accounted desperate” [CH2.8]
    • “Elementary diseases are cured by Elemental means, and Astrall have Astrall remedies” [N074]
  2. Of man’s invisible part from the stars: the Astrall body, spirit or man, which is the subject of human reason, sense and wisdom. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “that Astrall body or Syderiall Spirit, which is the true Astrall Man” [MICRO.27]
    • “which Astrall Spirit is the subject of humane Reason that containeth the sences and wisdome in it” [MICRO.27]
    • “The invisible Body or true Internall Man consisting of Reason and an Astrall Spirit, agreeth with the Angells” [MICRO.24]
    • “nor know any thing of the Creation and composition of the internall Astrall Man” [CH3.40]
  3. Of the chief medicine’s nature, by which it recovered a dying man. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “was recovered by its Fiery, Astrall, and Celestiall Invisible Nature” [CH6.38]
  4. Of a kind of physician, to whom knowing the philosophical secret is principally necessary. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “To know this Phylosophicall Secret truly is principally necessary to an Astrall Physitian” [CH6.41]

Astral Man

The book spells it: Astrall Man, Astralick man

  1. The invisible astral body or sydereal spirit received from the firmament and stars, called the true man, since flesh and blood are not the man; it moves, guides and performs all skilful matters, is mortal, and is what Paracelsus is praised for having written of. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “He participateth with the Firmament and Stars of whom he received that Astrall body or Syderiall Spirit, which is the true Astrall Man” [MICRO.27]
    • “for flesh and blood is not the Man, but the Spirit contained in them” [MICRO.27]
    • “This Spirit is mortall” [MICRO.27]
    • “an incorporeall Astralick man which moveth, guideth, and performeth all skilfull matters” [MICRO.31]
    • “who alone since Noe’s time hath written of the Internall Astrall Man” [CH5.12]
    • “nor know any thing of the Creation and composition of the internall Astrall Man” [CH3.40]

Astrum

The book spells it: Astrum, Astra’s, Astras

  1. the visible stars in the Firmament (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “The visible Astra’s or Stars in the Firmament flame forth from the Fiery Body” [CH1.28]
  2. the invisible, insensible body or living spirit in every thing, its inward heaven and seed, from which its form, growth and visible body come; the seven visible coals of Heaven are but their bodies (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “are indued with a syderiall spirit, which is called Heaven or the Astrum, the secret Forger, from which every Formation, Figure and Colour of things proceedeth” [CH1.15]
    • “of its own internall Astrum, or inward Heaven” [CH1.17]
    • “the Spirit is the life, and is divided into Astra’s which out of themselves give their growth and fruit” [CH1.27]
    • “but from its own invisible Element and Astrum” [CH1.27]
    • “for the Seed or Astrum can doe nothing without the body” [CH1.35]
    • “by which I do not meane the seaven visible coales of Heaven which are but the bodyes of the Astra’s but I meane the invisible and insensible body of all things or Astrall Spirit” [MICRO.32]
    • “the seeds of any graine or Wheat is the Astrum, viz. the invisible body” [MICRO.32]
    • “the Astrum is nothing else but the insensible, invisible body, or living Spirit” [MICRO.32]
    • “There is no body without an Astrum” [MICRO.32]
  3. the Astra’s of the Elements: the seeds in the Elements’ wombs, invisible, shown visibly in the highest Element (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “the invisible Astra’s of the other Elements should be represented by a visible appearance in the supream Element” [CH1.12]
    • “the four Astra’s of the said Elements are the Seeds in the four matrices or wombs” [CH1.35]
  4. set apart from the stars, which are said to be their rods (veiled · Interpreted · voice: UNCLEAR · note-only)
    • “The Stars are the rods of the Astra’s.” [N242]
  5. the Astrum in man: the Sun of the Microcosm, from which man is generated and formed, one with the Astrum of Heaven; the sydereal body, called also the imagination, holds all the Astra’s (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “From this proper and internall Astrum, viz. The Sun of the Microcosm (which Paracelsus calls the Ens or Being of the seed and virtue or power) is Man also generated” [CH1.15]
    • “For the Astra’s now in Man doe by Man alwayes in generation produce those two” [MICRO.31]
    • “It is also called ahe imagination, which incloseth all the Astra’s, and is indeed all the Astra’s” [MICRO.32]
    • “The Astrum of Man and Heaven is but one.” [N046]
    • “The inner Man is Heaven it selfe or all the Astras.” [N124]
  6. the powers of the Angels (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “they are nothing else but the verue or powers of the Angells” [MICRO.32]
  7. the virtue of Simples, taken when their body or Earth is cast away, to be made to agree with man’s inward Astrum (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “the body or Earth of Simples is to be cast away” [CH4.6]
    • “the vertue Heaven or Astrum of it only to be taken” [CH4.6]
    • “how he may make the Astrum of the Physick or of the magicall Heaven agree with the internall Astrum and Olimpus of Man” [CH1.38]
  8. the invisible causes of disease, from which the worst diseases spring rather than from the body’s matter (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “the most pernitious diseases and Traytors of the body do not spring out of the matter of the body, or out of the four Humours, but from the Nature of the Seed or Astra’s” [CH4.6]
    • “These Forgers and Invisible Astra’s of diseases were not knowne to the Ancients” [CH4.6]
    • “from the internall Astra’s or impressions which cause the diseases” [N069]
    • “their malignant Astra’s must be averted by a supernaturall cure and magicall help into something else” [CH6.73]

Balsam

The book spells it: Balsam, Balsom, Balsome

  1. a Balsam in Vegetables, Mineralls and Animalls, in which the true Medicine is founded and from which every action in Nature hath its beginning; the Aire, and the Spirit, are each called the Balsam of all things, beside being called their life (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “is founded in the Balsam of Vegetables, Mineralls and Animalls, from which every action in Nature hath its beginning” [CH1.3]
    • “It is the Balsam of all created things, and the life of the other three Elements” [CH1.32]
    • “The Spirit is the life and Balsom of all Naturall things.” [N288]
  2. the inward Balsom of man: the Nature within the body, called also balsomicall or Internall Mummy, that preserves the body from all corruption and expels all ill contrary to itself (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “this very selfe same Nature, which is our Life and Balsom, or balsomicall Mummy, that preserveth the body from all corruption” [CH4.3]
    • “Nature her selfe (who is the Internall Mummy or inward Balsom) expelled all ill contrary to her selfe” [CH4.6]
    • “the Balsom of Nature” [CH6.62]
    • “which strengthneth the Internall Balsom” [CH6.71]
  3. our Balsom, which the greatest Medicine quickens or makes alive, glossed as the spirit, the celestiall not visible vapour of life (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Phylosophers our predecessors that have been train’d up in Hermes school)
    • “that which quickneth or maketh alive our Balsom, that is, the spirit or celestiall” [CH6.50]
    • “not visible vapour of life” [CH6.50]
  4. a Balsom in things outside the body, even in venomous things, agreeable to man’s Nature, which assists and restores the inward Balsom because of the agreement between them; the Quint-Essence is called the correspondent Balsom of our bodies; a Balsom has a solid and stable Nature, which Nature could not give the mixture of bodies (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “the Medicinall Balsom like a coadjutour or privy counsellour assisteth the Vitall or Radicall and Naturall Balsom because of the simpathy and common agreement between them” [CH4.3]
    • “there is need of the Balsom of life as a means to be sought from Externalls, by which the internall is restored” [CH6.51]
    • “All Venemous things have a Balsom agreeable to Mans Nature” [CH3.14]
    • “the Quint-Essence, which is the true Internall, sutable, freindly and correspondent Balsom of our bodyes” [CH3.14]
    • “could by no means so contemper the mixture of those bodies that they should attaine to such admirable power and admit of the solid and stable Nature of a Balsom” [CH3.24]
  5. a name the margin reports Paracelsus gives the Tincture, the Fife Essence of the Microcosm (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Paracelsus · note-only)
    • “The Tincture is the Fife Essence of the Microcosm” [N283]
    • “Paracelsus calleth it the perfect, perpetuall” [N283]
    • “Balsom of Physitians or Phylosophers; the Defensive of old age” [N283]
  6. the Word of God as a supernatural Balsom, set above man’s own natural Balsom (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “it is the supercaelestiall Mummy, the supernaturall Balsome comforting poor Mortalls more then Mans own Mummy or naturall Balsom” [CH2.19]

Basilisk

The book spells it: Basilisk

  1. A creature the weasel fights. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “The Weasill eateth Rue when it is to fight with the Basilisk.” [CH2.9]
  2. The ‘Physophycall Basilisk’, which like lightning suddenly burns up imperfect metal and produces a new form; the moist way of the Ancients is said to be not yet exalted to ‘the eye of the Basilisk’. Interpreted: something in the work, the eye of the Basilisk a degree the work is raised to. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL · note-only)
    • “The Physophycall Basilisk like Lightning suddenly and unawares burneth up any imperfect mettall whatsoever, and on a sudden Produceth another new forme.” [N274]
    • “the Moyst way of the Ancients not as yet intensly exalted to the eye of the Basilisk” [CH6.38]

Book of Nature

The book spells it: book of Nature, Book of Nature, BOOK of NATURE

  1. Nature spoken of as a book, second after the book of Grace: Physick is written in it, in Heaven and Earth, every region is a leaf of it, and it is to be turned over practically and experimentally; the margin says only the physician who follows the mind of Paracelsus can open it. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Physick is written in the book of Nature (1) in Heaven and Earth, and may there be read & found out by Chyromancy and phisiognomy” [N038]
    • “desireth chiefly to turn over the BOOK of NATURE (in which every region is a leaf) not profunctorily & superficially, but practically and experimentally” [CH6.76]
    • “And next after the great book of Grace, wherein the eternall health of our soules consisteth, more diligently to pry into that other also of Nature” [OPEN.19]
    • “that Physitian that walketh according to the mind of Paracelsus is onely able to open the Book of Nature” [N316]

Centre

The book spells it: Centre, Center

  1. The place of rest and truth, set against the labyrinth and circumference of toil and error. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “from the dark Labyrinth and Circumference of toyle and error, to the plain and clear path and Centre of Rest and Truth” [OPEN.2]

  3. Man, in whom all things of the universe meet, in imitation of his Creator. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Thus Man in imitation of his Creator is the Centre of the Creatures, and the Circle of them all.” [MICRO.13]
    • “all things of the whole Universe meet in him as in the Centre” [CH1.5]
  4. The one point to which Paracelsus laboured to recall the consonancy of the Sciences and Muses from the whole circle. My inference: their unity. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “from the compasse of the whole circle unto the one only Centre” [CH5.14]
  5. The root and seed of diseases, which must be plucked up for the cure. My inference: named in one row with Root and Seed as one thing. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “when the Centre, Root, and Seed of diseases are pluckt up and removed the worke is done” [CH4.6]
  6. The place where the wise lived together keeping wisdom, those without it being sent to the circumference. My inference: the circle of those who hold wisdom. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: QUOTED: Paracels.)
    • “wise men lived together in the Centre reserving wisdome among themselves” [N308]

Characters

The book spells it: Characters, Character

  1. Characters or Signes, framed at a certain time according to the power of Heaven, by which true magicians have effected great things, and by which the Magus performs much sooner what the physician does by herbs. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “by a Word written and Characters or Signes” [CH2.16]
    • “framed at a certaine time according to the power of Heaven, far from all superstition” [CH2.16]
    • “performeth suddenly and much sooner by Characters and Stones with a most powerfull impression” [CH2.21]
  2. Characters or constellated Names, whose force comes not from their figure or pronunciation but from the office God or Nature ordained to them. (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Agrippa)
    • “For Characters or constellated Names according to Agrippa, have no force from the Figureors Pronunciation, but by reason of the Vertue or Office which God or Nature hath ordained to such a Name or Character” [CH2.16]
  3. Magicall and Hieroglyphicall Characters which sage Antiquity attributed to minerals, and which disclose their hidden vertue to enlightened men and instructed philosophers. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Thus also among Mineralls there Magicall and Hieroglyphicall Characters” [CH2.5]
    • “which sage Antiquity hath without rashnesse or superstition attributed to them” [CH2.5]
    • “that doe sufficiently insinuate to Inlightned men, and Magically disclose their hidden vertue to those more secret Phylosophers that are instructed therein” [CH2.5]

Chemistry

The book spells it: Chymistry

  1. The art of fire that separates good from bad, joined to physick and set apart from the face-painting of mountebanks. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Chymistry, which sepereteth the good from the bad” [CH5.12]
    • “by the help of Pyrotechnye, or the Art of Fire and Chymistry” [OPEN.5]
    • “Physick and Chymistry cannot be separated” [CH3.7]
    • “Chymistry (not that which Mountebanks use to paint Faces with to make them White and Red)” [CH3.7]
    • “we should begin to learn the knowledge of Chymistry from our cradles” [CH3.12]
  2. A sealed truth, held captive until now, which the author is accused of opening. My inference from the figures of the seal and the prison: knowledge kept hidden. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: QUOTED: Mysticall Hermetick Philosophers)
    • “breaking open the seale of Chymistry” [OPEN.10]
    • “brought the truth of Chymistry, till this time held captive in the prison of shadowes and envy” [OPEN.10]
  3. A mount whose top few reach. My inference from the figure of the mount: the height of the art. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “get to the top of the mount of Chymistry” [CH6.13]

Chymists

The book spells it: Chymists

  1. Practitioners who prepare and make up medicines, whom the author calls the most skilful of any against those who cry out that they are no physicians; he also says most of them leave the truth for the court. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “But though they exclaime and cry out against the Chymists as if they were no Physitians, though they be the most skilfull of any” [CH5.57]
    • “learn of Paracelsus and other Chymists to prepare and make up their Medicines” [CH5.61]
    • “most Chymists betake themselves to Court, and are turned from the truth by the glitter of Court-service” [CH3.44]
  2. Those Chymists who by divine grace have attained the Mind and can unfold the philosophical difficulties wrapped up in enigmatical obscurity. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Those Chymists who by the assistance of divine Grace have attained the Mind” [CH1.12]
    • “these can easily and truly unfold all Phylosophicall difficulties that have been wrapt up in aenigmaticall obscurity” [CH1.12]

Circle

The book spells it: Circle, Circles, circle

  1. God as the Circle of all things he brought out of himself: he encloses and holds them all, as he is their Centre in that all flow from him. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “God is the Center and Circle of all things that he brought out of himselfe” [MICRO.12]
    • “He is the Circle, because like an all-capacious Tabernacle he concludeth and comprehendeth all things” [MICRO.12]
  2. Man as the Circle of the creatures, in imitation of his Creator: he contains all things in himself and leads them back to their source. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Thus Man in imitation of his Creator is the Centre of the Creatures, and the Circle of them all” [MICRO.13]
    • “Man is said to be the Circle in that he containeth all things in himselfe, and with himselfe leadeth back all things that gushed out of that Summum Bonum” [MICRO.13]
  3. The circle as the many around one centre: man made out of the centre of all circles; the sciences recalled from the compass of the whole circle to the one centre. Interpreted (this reading’s own inference): circle stands for the spread-out many, centre for the one they come from. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “out of the Center of all Circles” [MICRO.11]
    • “by a kind of divine copulation from the compasse of the whole circle unto the one only Centre” [CH5.14]

Cottage

The book spells it: cottage, Cottage

  1. A body as the dwelling of what lives in it: the visible body as cottage of the soul and of life; the invisible sydereal body as cottage of the spirit’s life. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Man hath a visible body from the Elements as a fit garment and sutable cottage for the Soul” [MICRO.17]
    • “He hath his Body or cottage from the Earth and Water; but the life that dwells therein is from the Aire and Firmament of Fire” [MICRO.23]
    • “the Invisible, Insensible, Sydereall body (which is the house or cottage of the Spirits life) is from the Astra’s of the Firmament” [MICRO.31]
  2. The immortal soul as the dwelling of God, or of his Image or holy Spirit, in man. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “an immortall Soule, which is the cottage of the Image of God or of the holy Spirit in Man” [MICRO.14]
    • “the house is the habitation of the Soule, the Soule is the habitation and cottage of God” [MICRO.24]
  3. The outer coat of a medicine, with its husks and dregs, to be separated from the soul that dwells in it. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “the Coate aed Cottage of Physick which are enemyes to mans body from the Soul the Inhabitant thereof” [CH3.14]

Earth

The book spells it: EARTH, Earth

  1. The whole frame of the world, which man is to till and manage by admiration, searching and knowledge of both the visible and invisible Earth. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “we ought to till and manure the EARTH, to wit, the whole frame of the world by admiration, searching into, and knowledge of both the Visible and Invisible (Limus) Earth” [CH3.12]
    • “that in the fear of God and love of his neighbor he should manage the Earth, recovering what he hath lost” [CH3.13]
  2. The Earth that is ordered with good husbandry by one who has learned to know God and himself. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL · note-only)
    • “He that hath learned to know God & himselfe hath ordered the Earth with good husbandry” [N170]
  3. The element earth, from which man’s visible body comes; one of the four in the Microcosm. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “The Physicall, Elementary, visible and Tangible body is from the Earth” [MICRO.31]
    • “When the fruits of the Earth, Aire, Water and Fire of the Microcosm are sick” [CH4.6]
  4. The bodily part of a simple, to be cast away, as against its virtue, which alone is to be taken. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “the body or Earth of Simples is to be cast away” [CH4.6]
    • “the vertue Heaven or Astrum of it only to be taken” [CH4.6]
  5. The crystalline Earth in which what is pure of the Elements shall remain, for ever at rest with the saved. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “shall remaine like a pure, cleare, incorruptible and fixed Essence in a serene resplendent Chrystalin Earth, and be for ever at rest with the happy saved Ones” [CH6.29]

Elements

The book spells it: Elements, Element

  1. The four Elements, Fire, Aire, Water and Earth: man’s matter is their extract, God made them to feed and heal man, and knowing them shows every disease and its cure. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “And because the Matter of Man is the Extract of the four Elements, it is requisite that he have in himselfe a familiarity with all the Elements and their fruits” [CH1.6]
    • “God created the Elements for their fruits sake, that they might sustaine and preserve Man with food and Physick.” [CH1.6]
    • “The knowledge of the 4 Elements doe shew every disease in man and its cure.” [N043]
  2. The four bodies into which the three Principles of the first matter were afterwards separated. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “afterward these three Species are seperated into four divers bodies, or Elements” [CH1.19]
  3. In the passage given out of Severinus: the true Elements, which are spiritual, the places, wombs and receptacles of the whole Creation. (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Severinus)
    • “The true and purely spirituall Elements are the keepers, nurses, places, Mines, wombs and receptacles of the whole Creation” [CH1.22]
    • “for the true Elements are Spirituall” [CH1.25]
  4. In the passage given out of Severinus: the Elementated Elements, the four as they are to us, which conceive, bring forth and take back all things. (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Severinus)
    • “There are to us four Elementated Elements, viz. Fire or the Firmament, Aire, Water, Earth, which conceive, bring forth, and againe receive or take into them all things” [CH1.25]
  5. The Element as the invisible life and spirit within its visible body, in which the Astra’s lie; the visible Earth is not the Element but its body. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “The Externall is not the Element, but the body of the Element, and is the Sulphur, Mercury, Salt; For the Element of the Earth is life and Spirit wherein lie the Astra’s of the Earth” [CH1.29]
    • “The Earth of it self is dead, yet is it the Element of an invisible and hidden life.” [N063]

Ens

The book spells it: Ens, ENS

  1. The Being of the seed: the inner Astrum, the Sun of the Microcosm, from which Man is generated, formed and governed; the name is given as Paracelsus’s. (plain · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Paracelsus)
    • “From this proper and internall Astrum, viz. The Sun of the Microcosm (which Paracelsus calls the Ens or Being of the seed and virtue or power) is Man also generated, produced, figured, formed, and governed.” [CH1.15]
  2. Agrippa’s name for the universall Spirit that fills the world: a Being that the senses cannot grasp. (plain · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Agrippa)
    • “Agrippa calleth it the subject of all mirability, the Ens or Being that cannot be comprehended by sence” [CH3.31]
  3. The first Being toward life in nourishment: what rises from the last matter of the meat by the Archaeus, from which the body is fed. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “The first Ens or Being toward life, from whence the body hath it’s nourishment & food ariseth out of the last matter of the Meat by the Archaeus” [CH5.33]
  4. The Ens of God: the divine source of certain diseases, whose causes cannot be searched out and whose cure is sought in Faith, not in Nature. (plain · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Paracelsus)
    • “Paracelsus calleth them Deal diseases, which are from the Ens of God, who inflicteth all such diseases” [CH5.82]
    • “The Causes of those diseases that are of the Ens of God are unsearchable, here the cure must be sought in Faith, not in Nature” [CH5.82]
  5. The Being as the second of the ways life is shortened: the source of bodily (Entall or Corporall) diseases, whose cure is sought from natural causes and means; a margin note names MENS and ENS together. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Or Secondly, The life is shortned PER ENTEM” [CH6.74]
    • “by the Being, as by Entall or Corporall diseases” [CH6.74]
    • “And this cure of the harmes of the Naturall members which come from the Ens or Being, is to be sought from Natural causes and means” [CH6.74]
    • “MENS & ENS abrumpunt nobis vitam.” [N309]
  6. The source of one of two kinds of death, a death the physician cannot hinder, set against death from the Iliad. Several defensible readings: (a) the Ens of God of CH5.82, whose cure lies in Faith, not Nature; (b) the bodily Being of CH6.74, which bears the same words “Ens or Being”, though there its harms are to be cured by natural means. (plain · several · voice: QUOTED: Paracelsus)
    • “the death which is from the resolution of the Iliad may be hindred by the industry of the Physitian, but that which is from the Ens or Being cannot” [CH6.71]
    • “There is a two fold death, from the Iliad or first principle, and from the Ens or Being.” [N307]
    • “here the cure must be sought in Faith, not in Nature” [CH5.82]
    • “which come from the Ens or Being, is to be sought from Natural causes and means” [CH6.74]

Envy

The book spells it: envy

  1. Grudging ill will that keeps knowledge and gifts from others; God gives without it, and it has tried to shut the doors of the Muses. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “he communicateth his light, without respect or envy” [OPEN.2]
    • “envy hath indeavoured to shut them again” [OPEN.2]
    • “lovers of God and their neighbor, without envy” [OPEN.10]
  2. The philosophers’ withholding of their knowledge, which is not turned against worthy seekers. My inference from the sentence, which goes on to the adept who will not publish to the unworthy. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “the envy of Phylosophers is not against the Children of Art and Science” [CH6.27]

Essence

The book spells it: Essence, essence, Esence

  1. The healing part of a thing, its physick, lying near its poison and got out by separation from husks and impurities. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “nothing in this lower world (which is subject to generation and corruption) is so noble but hath Poyson in it selfe and in respect of another very near the Essence or Physick” [CH3.1]
    • “that so at least that quickning essence may be got, whose faculty mounts more high and quick being loosed from its chaines” [CH3.14]
  2. The one Nature that is the essence and matter of all things. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “that one onely Nature the Essence and Matter of all things” [CH3.30]
  3. The physical essence in physick, which the note says is called Gold. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL · note-only)
    • “This Physicall essence in Phisick is called Gold.” [N038]
  4. That to which what is pure of the Elements is likened: it shall remain like a pure, clear, incorruptible and fixed essence. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “And all that is of the Virtues and pure Truth and Nature of the Elements, which feare not the Fire of Heaven shall remaine like a pure, cleare, incorruptible and fixed Essence” [CH6.29]
  5. Said, with a hedge, of the greatest Medicine: it may be the essence of our life. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: QUOTED: Phylosophers our predecessors that have been train’d up in Hermes school)
    • “it may be the Essence of our Life” [CH6.50]
  6. The being of the seed, from which all men after the first proceed. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL · note-only)
    • “the rest proceed from the Being of the seed or Esence” [N121]

Extract

The book spells it: Extract

  1. the purest substance drawn out of the four Elements or the whole world, of which Man is made (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL · wording-dependent)
    • “the Matter of Man is the Extract of the four Elements” [CH1.6]
    • “Man was formed of the most excellent Compound and purest Extract of the whole Word” [MICRO.11]
    • “most admirable Extract & kernell of the four Elements” [MICRO.30]

Faith

The book spells it: Faith, faith

  1. Faith in God: by faith eternal things are seen; the text names a Faith of Grace by Christ, set apart from Naturall Faith; faith in God is called the best foundation of physick. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Eternall things are to be seen onely by Faith” [MICRO.33]
    • “I speak not of the Faith of Grace by Christ” [CH5.75]
    • “The best and chiefest Foundation of Physick is faith in God, and love to our neighbour” [N227]
  2. Faith as a working power: by it we do after the manner of spirits what we do visibly by the body; it gives the Imagination; every creature is at the command of the faith of men innocent and taught in the Law; it resists the violences of mental diseases where no bodily guard avails; the text says it is the work of him in whom we believe. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “What we doe visiby by the body, that doe we by Faith after the manner of Spirits” [MICRO.33]
    • “Faith is at least a work, I mean his work in whom we believe.” [CH5.76]
    • “when we think, and have faith in our cogitations, then faith doth the work, but without faith we can doe nothing” [CH5.76]
    • “Faith giveth the Imagination” [CH5.76]
    • “every creature is at the beck and command of their faith who are men innocent and taught in the Law of the Lord” [CH2.22]
    • “No corporall guard or shelter availeth any thing against such like violences, but onely Faith which is able to resist them” [CH6.73]
  3. The sick man’s trust in the physician and his physick, which helps toward health. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “A firm Faith, undoubted Hope, Love and Confidence toward the Physitian and his Physick, doth very much conduce to health” [CH5.75]

FIAT

The book spells it: FIAT, Fiat

  1. The creating word of God, “Let there be”, by which the first matter, all things threefold, and man out of the dust of the Earth were made. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “By the word FIAT (or Let there be) God produced the first matter” [CH1.19]
    • “For the Holy Triunity when it spake that Triune word FIAT created all things Triune” [CH1.19]
    • “Man was created è Limo Terrae, out of the clay or dust of the Earth, by the word FIAT” [MICRO.22]
  2. The Word FIAT as what works in all creatures and heals: the seed of the whole world, by which God’s Spirit is one with Nature, called the true medicine. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “This Word then is the true medicine that healeth all things” [CH2.20]
    • “by this Word, FIAT, the seed of the whole world, were Heaven and Earth created, and this is that which is efficacious in all the Creatures” [CH2.20]
    • “by the Spirit of God made One with Nature by his Word FIAT” [CH2.17]
  3. The source of the second of man’s two Spirits, set against the first, which is from the Limbus. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL · note-only)
    • “The first Spirit is from the Limbus or greater world; the second from the word Fiat.” [N101]

Fifth Essence

The book spells it: fift Essence, Fift Essence, Fift Effences, Fife Essence, Fist Essence

  1. The perfect part of a sublunary thing, which fire can separate from its dregs, the imperfect part. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “a perfect and an imperfect, that is, a fift Essence and the dreggs may be separated one from the other by fire” [CH2.1]
  2. The chief Medicine: resulting from the purer essence of the four Elements, which God created as the root of life to preserve the four qualities of the human body, as he did Heaven to preserve the universe; incorruptible, of the nature and heat of Heaven, binding the Elements or Humors in peace; a margin note calls the Tincture the Fift Essence of the Microcosm. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “The most High created this Fift Essence as the root of life in Nature for the preservation of the four qualities of the Humane body” [CH6.51]
    • “as he did the Heaven for the preservation of the universe” [CH6.51]
    • “the Fift Essence of the whole frame of the world, and the whole world Regenerated” [CH6.51]
    • “the Fift Essence or Fift Virtue resulting from the purer Essence of the four Elements, purified, incorruptible is compared to Heaven” [CH6.52]
    • “that Fift Essence of the Human Body bindeth the Elements or Humors in Peace and Harmonicall league” [CH6.54]
    • “in this Fift Essence and Spirituall Medicine, which hath the Nature and Heat of Heaven” [CH6.55]
    • “The Tincture is the Fife Essence of the Microcosm” [N283]
  3. The same, as the reason the philosophers of Hermes’ school give: compounded of the four Elements, all of them actually in it. (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Phylosophers our predecessors that have been train’d up in Hermes school)
    • “the Fift Essence compounded of the four Elements, in which are all the Elements actually” [CH6.50]
  4. Occult qualities the Soul of the world infuses into things to make them fruitful, commonly so called (margin note only; note-only). (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “occult qualities which are commonly called the Fift Effences” [N178]

Fire

The book spells it: Fire, fire, FIRE

  1. Fire in the work, which tries, separates and prepares, and uncovers the agreement and difference of substances and new remedies. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “the due approved triall of fire, which discerneth and separateth that which is false, from that which is true” [OPEN.5]
    • “As all things are proved by Fire, so also the Tryall of the knowledge of Physick is to be made by Fire” [CH3.7]
    • “As Gold is tryed seven times in the fire” [CH5.12]
    • “fetcht out, and prepared truly, by fire” [OPEN.26]
    • “separating them by Fire into their Three First (things.)” [CH6.30]
    • “The ministry of Fire discovereth their agreement, operation, and difference” [CH4.7]
    • “the Fire discovereth new and most pleasant Remedies continually” [CH5.57]
    • “so doth Fire the good from the bad, the Quint-Essence from the body” [N171]
  2. The element of fire, whose fruits are the stars and signs of heaven. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “The twelve Caelestiall Signes in the Zodiak, with the other Stars of Heaven, are the fruits of Fire” [CH1.34]
    • “the seven Rulers of the world are nothing else but the fruits of Fire” [CH1.34]
    • “which fruits are separated from the Element of Fire” [CH1.34]
  3. That which Paracelsus calls the Element of FIRE, which may be the greatest Secret for removing all diseases: an essential fire of life that works within the body. (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Paracelsus)
    • “Paracelsus calleth it the Element of FIRE” [CH6.62]
    • “that Essentiall Fire worketh in the body, as the Flame and Nettle doth without the body” [CH6.62]
    • “this perfect Fire of Life” [CH6.62]
  4. A fire kept by laying on wood, to which the author likens life prolonged by remedies. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “as we may preserve a little fire by laying on more wood; so also may our life be prolonged” [CH6.71]
  5. The root of a disease, which is to be quenched rather than its signs. My inference: the smoke stands for what springs from the disease, the fire for its seed. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “Not the smoak arising from fire, but the fire it selfe is to be quenched” [CH4.6]
  6. Temptation and tribulation, through which man must pass to blessedness. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “through the Fire of Temptation and Tribulation” [CH6.35]
  7. The means by which the Holy Spirit is wont to renew, to make manifest what was hid. My inference: a covered fire, belonging to the third age in which Elias the Artist shall come. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “who to make that manifest which was hid, is wont to renew by fire” [OPEN.10]

Firmament

The book spells it: Firmament, firmament

  1. the heaven with its stars and planets (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “The firmament or heaven with all therein is the second Naturall book” [N031]
    • “The visible Astra’s or Stars in the Firmament flame forth from the Fiery Body” [CH1.28]
    • “The course of the externall Firmament is free with its constellations, and is governed by none” [CH1.17]
    • “all the celestiall virtues of the Firmament, as well of the Fixed Stars as of the Planets” [CH6.51]
    • “The School of Physick is not covered with tile, but with the Firmament” [N315]
  2. the Element of Fire, the heavenly Element; twofold, with an Element dwelling in it that holds all Astra’s and Seeds and sends out the bodies of the stars (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “one in the Firmament or Heavenly Element” [CH1.6]
    • “the nine Sphaeres or Firmament with all the Stars are the Fire” [CH1.27]
    • “The Firmament or Fire is likewise is twofold, and hath its own Element as an Inhabitant in it selfe, which Element hath in it all Astra’s and Seeds” [CH1.33]
    • “The Element of Fire, or the Corporeall Firmament sends the bodies of the Stars, Sun, Moon and Planets out of it selfe” [CH1.33]
  3. Fire or the Firmament, counted first of the four Elementated Elements (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Severinus)
    • “There are to us four Elementated Elements, viz. Fire or the Firmament, Aire, Water, Earth” [CH1.25]
  4. a Firmament within man, with its own stars and course, free of the outward one; the sydereal man is called the same with the Firmament of the Astra’s (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “So the course of the Firmament and Stars in Man is free, with their Constellations, and not at all governed by the outward Firmament” [CH1.17]
    • “coagulated or curdled Man is the same with the Firmament of the Astra’s” [MICRO.32]
    • “intoxicateth the microcosmicall Firmament” [CH5.75]
  5. a Terrene or inferiour Firmament with its own Sun; the Element of FIRE is likened to that Sun (veiled · Interpreted · voice: UNCLEAR)
    • “like the Sun of the Terrene or inferiour Firmament” [CH6.62]
  6. the Word of God as the ground of all Physick, without which no drug does good (veiled · Interpreted · voice: UNCLEAR · wording-dependent)
    • “which is the Firmament of all Physick” [CH2.13]
    • “without which no drug will doe good” [CH2.13]

First matter

The book spells it: first matter, First Matter

  1. The matter God first produced by the word FIAT, threefold in the three Principles, a confused Essence called the Chaos and Hylen and by Paracelsus the Limbus, out of which every creature was drawn; it was made a world. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “By the word FIAT (or Let there be) God produced the first matter, which is threefold in respect of the three Principles contained in the first” [CH1.19]
    • “which was a kind of ineffigiate confused Essence, which Phylosophers call the Chaos and Hylen, or Mother of the world” [MICRO.17]
    • “So the First Matter (which Paracelsus calleth the Limbus, whose Earth was the WORD of the Lord) was the seed of all things that were to be created” [MICRO.17]
    • “The first matter was made a world, and the great World was made Man.” [MICRO.17]
    • “so God extracted every creature out of the first matter.” [N105]
    • “ALL things in respect of the first matter were created perfect” [CH3.1]

Fundamental

The book spells it: Fundamentall

  1. A ground truth of Physick on which the physician must build: that great and little world answer to each other, so that malady and medicine share one Physiognomy, Chyromancy and Anotomy; the text also calls it a Foundation. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “every malady and medicine is of the same Physiognomy, Chyromancy and Anotomy; He that knows not this Fundamentall cannot be a good Phisitian” [CH1.12]
    • “this true Fundamentall of the occult Phylosophycal Physick” [MICRO.45]
    • “this firme and unmovable Foundation” [MICRO.45]
  2. A foundation that is at once theological and philosophical truth and piety, a foundation of Faith and new Regeneration, by which all Sciences are perfectly attained; the author speaks also of a Fundamentall knowledge of Nature, joined to the help of the Light of Grace. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “that invincible united Fundamentall of Theologicall and Phylosophicall Truth and perfect Piety” [CH5.18]
    • “according to the Fundamentall knowledge of the same by the supernall help of the Light of Grace” [CH5.13]
    • “All Sciences are perfectly attained unto by the Fundamentall of Faith and new Regeneration or Celestiall Transplantation” [N203]

Galenists

The book spells it: Galenists

  1. Physicians who hold axioms of qualities, such as that all bitter things are hot, and, the margin says, know nothing of Astral cures; one milder part of them is said for fear not to dare openly profess the truth. (plain · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “By this Art only the Galenists may make good their Axioms, who affirm that all bitter things are hot” [CH3.19]
    • “The Galenists doe know nothing of these Astral cures, which the grave experienced Physitians do well understand.” [N074]
    • “But the more single and subtle part of the Galenists, who like Nicodemus are of a milder temper” [OPEN.12]
    • “for fear of being excommunicated by some Athenian Rabbies, durst not openly professe the truth” [OPEN.12]
    • “Hippocrates wrote many things, which most Galenists reject at this day” [CH5.47]
    • “doe sometime exceed many Galenists in many great and perilous diseases” [CH5.60]

Gamahaea

The book spells it: Gamahaea

  1. The fourth kind of Naturall Magick, which by Art does invisibly and spiritually what Nature does visibly and bodily without that help. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “This fourth kind of Naturall Magick called Gamahaea doth all things invisibly and spiritually, by the help of Art, which Nature is able to doe visibly and corporally without such help.” [MICRO.35]
  2. The most powerful impression by which the Magus works with characters and stones: an influential wedlock to the earthly sign, a matrimonial joining of Superiors and Inferiors astrally. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “by Characters and Stones with a most powerfull impression, to wit, the Gamahaea of Influentiall Wedlock to the Terrestiall signe, by matrimoniall combination of the Superiors and Inferiors ASTRALLY” [CH2.21]

Globes

The book spells it: Globes, Globe

  1. the two Globes, the superiour and inferiour, which Astronomy and Philosophy explain; the properties of bodies in the upper are seen in the bodies of the lower (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Astronomy and Philosophy, which explaine those two Globes, the superiour and inferiour” [CH1.4]
    • “the properties of those bodies in the superiour Globe, which are seen in the Astra’s and bodies of the inferiour Globe” [CH1.12]
  2. the two Globes as the places of the Elements: the upper Fire or Firmament and Aire, the lower Water and Earth, likened to an egg (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Severinus)
    • “These (places) are divided into two Globes, viz. the superiour Fire, or the Firmament, and Aire” [CH1.22]
    • “the inferiour, Water and the Earth, like the yolk of an egge” [CH1.22]
  3. the Globe of the Moon, under which God created all metallick, minerall and vegetable Forms, and under which the more incorruptible substance is found (plain · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “all metallick, minerall, and vegetable Forms, which God created under the Globe of the Moon” [CH6.51]
    • “the more incorruptible substance that is to be found under the Globe of the Moon” [CH6.60]

Gold

The book spells it: Gold

  1. The metal as a medicine, which would show most Divine actions if it were rightly refined; few have the gift to make it potable; set down for the heart, and said in the margin to thicken the too much sublimation of Mercury. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “There is no question to be made but the Gold would discover most Divine actions if it were rightly refined” [CH2.5]
    • “but few men have this gift of God bestowed on them, so as to make solid and massy Gold potable, that it may be drunk” [CH2.5]
    • “for the heart, Gold, Pearles, Saffron” [CH5.46]
    • “Gold thickneth the too much sublimation of Mercury.” [N194]
    • “As he that hath much Gold may know nothing of its medicinall vertue.” [N135]
  2. Gold as wealth and money; the aside in CH2.5 sets it beside gold the medicine, and the author says he did not seek to make Gold. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Gold (which cheareth up the Spirits when a man hath enough of it in his purse)” [CH2.5]
    • “not to the end that I might make Gold and Silver” [CH6.36]
  3. The name the margin gives to “this Physicall essence in Phisick” (N038). (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL · note-only · wording-dependent)
    • “This Physicall essence in Phisick is called Gold.” [N038]

Heathen

The book spells it: Heathen

  1. Those, set against Christians, who wrote on Nature: their writing is for the most part false, their philosophy corrupt, the light of truth not yet risen to them; they lack the Regeneration by which Christians truly know. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “what the Heathen wrote concerning Nature is for the most part false, because their Phylosophy and other abilities were polluted and corrupt” [MICRO.8]
    • “the Light of Truth not yet risen to them, nor the Light of Nature as yet kindled by the holy Spirit” [MICRO.8]
    • “by the means of Regeneration which the Heathen have not” [MICRO.8]
    • “not after the manner of the Heathen in hollow empty language and temporaneous Arts” [CH5.13]
  2. Their loathsome errors are said to have been wiped away by men of greater wisdom who came after them. (plain · Stated · voice: UNCLEAR)
    • “many loathsome errours of the Heathen have been as by a second song” [CH5.4]
    • “wip’d away by men of greater wisdome coming after them” [CH5.4]

Hermes

The book spells it: Hermes

  1. An ancient philosopher, called most wise, the Antesignan (foremost) of natural philosophers and first prophet, whose pillars stood in Aegypt and whose long labour the adept repeats. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “the most wise Hermes and most pious Phylosopher of reverend antiquity the Antesignan of Naturall Phylosophers and first Prophet” [CH6.68]
    • “hath happily labored like Hermes more then twenty years” [CH6.27]
    • “to Hermes his Pillars in Aegypt” [CH1.18]
  2. ‘Hermes school’: the line of earlier philosophers trained in the secret Art. Interpreted from the setting: these are the philosophers who kept the secret by silence. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “the Phylosophers our predecessors that have been train’d up in Hermes school” [CH6.50]

Hermetic physicians

The book spells it: Hermetick Physitians

  1. Physicians the author calls the best of this age, naming among them Quercetan, Bovius and Muffet, who confuted the adversaries of Severinus; their custom, which physicians should imitate, is to pray for a blessing on their work. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “who have been sufficiently confuted and for ever silenced by my honoured friend Joseph Quercetan” [MICRO.45]
    • “my honoured friend Joseph Quercetan Councellour and most worthy Physitian to the King of France, by Th: Bovius an Italian of Verona, and Th: Muffet an Englishman, the best Hermetick Physitians of this age” [MICRO.45]
    • “imitate that most commendable and religious custome of the Hermetick Physitians, who always use to pray for a blessing upon their endeavours” [CH5.62]

Iliad

The book spells it: Iliad

  1. The first principle, from which one of two kinds of death comes (margin note only). (veiled · Stated · voice: UNCLEAR)
    • “There is a two fold death, from the Iliad or first principle, and from the Ens or Being” [N307]
  2. That whose resolution brings a death the physician’s industry can hinder, as against death from the Ens or Being, which cannot be. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: QUOTED: Paracelsus)
    • “Paracelsus saith that the death which is from the resolution of the Iliad may be hindred by the industry of the Physitian, but that which is from the Ens or Being cannot” [CH6.71]
  3. Something that contains all things in itself, named where diseases come from the minerals of man. Reading 1: man himself. Basis: the paragraph says there are as many minerals in man as in the world, and MICRO.17 says man carries the world in himself. Reading 2: the first principle, as N307 glosses the word. Basis: N307. (Margin note only; rests on one word of the translation.) (veiled · several · voice: CROLL)
    • “Many diseases proceed out of the mineralls of man, which Iliad containeth all things in it selfe” [N072]

Imagination

The book spells it: imagination, Imagination, IMAGINATION

  1. The imagination of a maker, the Smith, used as a likeness: the Formation of things is in the Astra’s as iron is in his imagination. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL · note-only)
    • “as iron in the imagination of the Smith” [N047]
  2. A name of the sydereall body of man: it holds and is all the Astra’s, keeps their course, nature and power, and is one with the Astra’s of the Firmament; therefore it works many operations. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “It is also called ahe imagination, which incloseth all the Astra’s, and is indeed all the Astra’s or Starrs and holdeth the same course, Nature and power with heaven” [MICRO.32]
    • “whereas the imagination of Man is not one, but all the Astra’s, it is as true that it produceth not onely one, but many operations” [MICRO.33]
    • “the whole Imagination of Man dependeth on the Astra’s of the Firmament, yea is the same and abideth one with them” [MICRO.35]
  3. A working power of man: it marks the unborn child, draws other men’s fantasie like the Loadstone, infects the stars, breeds the Plague, and with true Gabalia can produce growing things and metals. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “The child in the mothers womb is strangely marked by the impression of the imagination without any bodily touch” [MICRO.33]
    • “Thus the Imagination breedeth the Plague and such like Firmamentall diseases; it brings sicknesse and health.” [MICRO.33]
    • “Thus by Imagination and true Gabalia all hearbs, all growing things, all mettalls may be produced.” [MICRO.37]
    • “Thirdly, by a strongly exalted IMAGINATION” [MICRO.40]
    • “The Imagination or Fantasie of Man is like the Load stone in its Nature, attracting the Fantasie of other men” [MICRO.40]
    • “but Man rather inclines the Stars, and by his Magicall imagination infecteth them” [CH1.16]
  4. Naturall Faith, inborn from the first Creation, as distinct from the Faith of Grace: it can both cause and cure diseases. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Naturall Faith (I speak not of the Faith of Grace by Christ) inborn of God the Father in the first Creation, which is our IMAGINATION, is so powerfull that it can both cause and cure diseases” [CH5.75]
    • “when out of his own proper Imagination, from fear and terrour it breedeth that Basilisk of Heaven” [CH5.75]
  5. An imagining power that the Firmament, and the whole Heaven, also has, but without Reason, as man has it with Reason. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “it must needs be that the Firmament also hath an Imagination but without Reason, as Man the off-spring of the world hath with Reason” [MICRO.35]
    • “The whole Heaven is nothing else but the Imagination” [N125]
  6. The imagination of the five Spirits in the Elements, which must also be in man; man’s Reason, used according to God’s will, is as a chain by which those five Spirits are bound, that they may acquiesce with his Imagination. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “the Imagination also of these five Spirits in the Elements must be in the Microcosm Man” [MICRO.28]
    • “but the use of Mans Reason according to the will and command of God, is as a Chaine were with those five Spirits are knit and bound together that they may acquiesce with his Imagination” [MICRO.28]
  7. One of the invisible mental diseases that arise by the Mind and affect us in it; the margin lists five, Imagination among them. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “whence arise mentall diseases which are invisible, and affect us in our Mind, as Inchantment, Imagination, Estimation, Influence; Superstition” [CH6.73]
    • “i. e. Inchantment, Imagination, Estimation, Influence, Superstition.” [N311]

Impostors

The book spells it: Impostors, Imposters

  1. Base cheats who are wont falsely to accuse innocent men; the paragraph opens on the spurious Theophrasteans, who carry their “cheating impostures” (OPEN.11) about the Country, and that the Impostors are these same men is an inference. (plain · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “those base Impostors which deserve to be made Mine-diggers” [OPEN.11]
    • “are wont not only falsly to accuse many innocent men of upright, vertuous and blamelesse life” [OPEN.11]
    • “carry their cheating impostures up and downe the Country” [OPEN.11]
  2. Cheats named in the chapter on the chief Medicine of the philosophers: some have got the true Philosophical Ferment by unlawful means, prepared by others, but cannot multiply it for ignorance of the principle; others cheat great men with golden promises, being destitute of the liberal arts. The “transmutatory virtue” (CH6.26) given them is the author’s irony: it turns rich men poor (Interpreted). (plain · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “some Impostors in our age who right or wrong by unlawfull means having got the most true Phylosophycall Ferment or Leaven” [CH6.19]
    • “but prepar’d by others” [CH6.19]
    • “have not been able to improve or multiply it any further by reason of the ignorance of the principle or beginning” [CH6.19]
    • “by the golden promises of circumforaneous, vitious, and most lying Impostors” [CH6.22]
    • “of which those Phylosophists and Imposters are altogether destitute” [CH6.22]
    • “this efficacious transmutatory virtue of these Impostors, which makes wise men fools, strong men weak, rich men poor” [CH6.26]

Inner Man

The book spells it: inner Man

  1. The inward man, said to be Heaven itself or all the stars, and to ascend the inward Heaven. Interpreted (this reading’s own inference): the notes stand beside the passages on the sydereal or astral man, which MICRO.24 also calls the true internal man. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL · note-only)
    • “The inner Man is Heaven it selfe or all the Astras.” [N124]
    • “The inner Man ascendeth the inward Heaven.” [N122]
    • “The invisible Body or true Internall Man consisting of Reason and an Astrall Spirit, agreeth with the Angells” [MICRO.24]

Last matter

The book spells it: last matter

  1. The matter of things as they now stand, set against the first matter in which they were created perfect: the Chymist perfects it by fire and draws the secret things out of it. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “ALL things in respect of the first matter were created perfect, but the Chymist perfecteth the last matter by Fire” [CH3.1]
    • “by the secret things or Astra’s which the Chymist can reduce out of the last matter into the first” [CH1.43]
