    • “is known whether (according to those Epithites in Scripture) he be a Dog, Fox, Wolfe, Sheep, Sow, or generation of Vipers, of which I shall discourse more at large in my Treatise of Signatures” [MICRO.15]
    • “cast away the Signatures of cursed Sloth, of Drones, in their hollow cracks and clamors which at least make a terrible noyse, they may become Bees” [CH6.76]

Soul of the world

The book spells it: Soule of the world, Soule or Spirit of the world

  1. The universal spirit that lives in, quickens and fills the whole world, a united life knitting all things together. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “That universall Spirit which liveth in and quickneth this whole Masse, which worketh all in all, and filleth the whole world” [CH3.31]
    • “The Soule of the world is a kind of united life, filling, gathering and knitting together all things” [N178]
    • “the magnetick spark of the Soule of the world liveth and worketh effectually” [MICRO.35]
  2. Avicen’s name for that universal spirit, as poured into all things, building on Plato, the Arabians and Caldeans. (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Avicen)
    • “Avicen saith, it is the Soule of the world powred into all things, building on the authority of Plato, the Arabians and Caldeans” [CH3.31]
  3. The matter of which the chief Medicine is made, holding the force of all heavenly and earthly things. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “this Medicine is made of the more incorruptible and efficacious Matter that can be under Heaven, that is, the Soule or Spirit of the world, which hath in it the force of all Celestiall and Terrestriall things” [CH6.55]

Spagyric

The book spells it: Spagyrick, Spagyriks, SPAGIRUS

  1. One who can separate the pure from the impure and the true from the false, exercised in Chymicall employments. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “the Spagyriks, or those who know how to discern between true and false, who are well grown and exercised in Chymicall employments, I mean the Sons of Art” [OPEN.7]
    • “a Physitian must be a good SPAGIRUS” [CH5.12]
    • “one that can seperate the pure from the impure, and restore his Patient to health by a wise Alchymicall preparation” [CH5.12]
  2. Belonging to that art of preparing medicine and its practitioners: Spagyrick physick, profession, physicians. Interpreted from its use beside the first sense. (plain · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “Reasons why the Spagyrick Physick duely prepared is to be preferd before other Hetroclite mixtures in the shops.” [N172]
    • “the present Spagyrick Profession” [CH3.41]
    • “the due honor of Spagyrick Phisitians” [CH6.76]
  3. Of the body of practitioners of that art, spoken of as a state with citizens and as a ‘Spagyrick mellifice’ that others may join. Interpreted: ‘state’, ‘citizens’ and ‘mellifice’ are used of a body of practitioners. (plain · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “By which singular care toward the whole Spagyrick state” [CH6.76]
    • “as becometh good & sincere citizens of the Physicall & Spagyrick State and profession” [CH6.76]
    • “conspire together with us into an union and Spagyrick mellifice” [CH6.76]

Spagyric physic

The book spells it: Spagyrick Physick

  1. Physick duly prepared, to be preferred before the Hetroclite mixtures of the shops; the text glosses Spagyricall as divinely-distinguishing. (plain · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “Reasons why the Spagyrick Physick duely prepared is to be preferd before other Hetroclite mixtures in the shops.” [N172]
    • “who have not learned so much as their A. B. C. in the Spagyrick Physick” [CH3.40]
    • “that Spagyricall or Divinely-distinguishing Sanctuary” [OPEN.11]

Specific

The book spells it: Specifick, specifick

  1. Noun: what the one Nature is to every creature; the narrowest reading is that which gives each creature its own kind. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “which Nature is the Specifick of every Creature” [CH3.30]
  2. Of a virtue: a thing’s own inward power, set against its outward qualities, by which it cures. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “not (as I have said) by their Externall superficiary qualities, but by an Internall, proper, specifick, harmonious, similer virtue” [CH2.7]
    • “by the Specifick venemous vertue of the Opium which hath a greater degree of poyson” [CH4.6]
    • “by a specifick and Harmonicall vertue serve to cure and comfort the heart” [CH2.5]

Spirit

The book spells it: Spirit, spirit, Spirits

  1. A fourth Principle that some Theophrastaeans add to Salt, Sulphur and Mercury: it may be got out of Mineralls and Vegetables, but in Animalls it cannot be extracted, and it answers to Aire. (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Some Theophrastaeans)
    • “which they call the Spirit, which though it may be got out of Mineralls and Vegetables, yet in Animalls by reason of its subtility it is subjected unto, nor can it be extracted” [CH1.21]
    • “Mercury to Water, Spirit to Aire” [CH1.21]
  2. The syderiall spirit in all things, the Astrum: an invisible, insensible, living body, without Reason in growing things and with Reason in man. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “are indued with a syderiall spirit, which is called Heaven or the Astrum” [CH1.15]
    • “But the Astrum is nothing else but the insensible, invisible body, or living Spirit, yet without Reason in things that grow; but with reason in living things” [MICRO.32]
  3. Man’s two Spirits: one syderiall, from the Firmament; the other from the breath of life, the Intellectuall Soul inspired from God. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “There are two Spirits in Man” [MICRO.14]
    • “one a syderiall Spirit from the Firmament, the other from the breath of life, which is the Intellectuall Soul inspired from God” [MICRO.14]
  4. The Spirit of God moving upon the face of the Waters at the Creation; the margin sets these words beside the fourth Principle called the Spirit. (plain · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Severinus)
    • “and of the Spirit moving upon the face of the Waters” [CH1.23]
    • “The Spirit of God upon the face of the waters.” [N054]
  5. The universall Spirit that lives in and quickens the whole mass of the world and fills it. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “That universall Spirit which liveth in and quickneth this whole Masse, which worketh all in all, and filleth the whole world” [CH3.31]
  6. The spirit, or supernall Fire, got by art out of things in which the virtue of Sun and Moon is most potent; a margin note calls the Spirit the life and Balsom of all natural things. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “the spirit or that supernall Fire is got out of them by art” [CH6.57]
    • “The Spirit is the life and Balsom of all Naturall things.” [N288]
  7. Interpreted: the four Elementary Spirits, spirits belonging to the four Elements, named as of the four inhabitants of the world; with a fifth kind they are five Spirits in the Elements, whose Imagination is in man. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “the four Elementary Spirits of the four inhabitants of the world” [MICRO.28]
    • “the Imagination also of these five Spirits in the Elements must be in the Microcosm Man” [MICRO.28]

Spirit of life

The book spells it: Spirit of life, spirit of Life, Spirit of Life, spirit of life

  1. The life-spirit in the body: one through the whole body yet varied by its parts, the source of the body’s strength, moving the radical humour and nourished by the humours of life. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Thus also the Spirit of life is united through out the whole body of man, but is various according to parts of the body in which it is.” [N177]
    • “for the strengthning power of the Human body and of Animalls proceedeth from the spirit of Life” [CH6.54]
    • “the Native or Radicall Humor which the Spirit of Life moveth or acteth” [CH6.62]
    • “The Humors of life do nourish the spirit of life.” [N295]
  2. Said of the Fift Essence: it is truly the spirit of life, piercing all other spirits and one with the spirit of our body. (veiled · Stated · voice: UNCLEAR)
    • “it is truly the spirit of Life which pierceth through all other spirits” [CH6.51]
    • “and is altogether one and the same with the spirit of our body” [CH6.51]
  3. The spiracle or breathing hole. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL · note-only)
    • “The Spirit of life is the Spiracle or breathing hole.” [N122]

Spirit of the World

The book spells it: Spirit of the World, spirit of the world, Spirit of the world

  1. The invisible and insensible body of the Astra’s, also called Nature; the invisible Man and the spirit of man’s body are one and the same with it. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “That invisible and insensible body of the Astra’s is the Spirit of the World, or Nature” [CH1.14]
    • “is altogether one and the same thing with the Spirit of the world, as whitenesse is with snow” [CH1.14]
    • “the spirit of the world (one and the same with the spirit of our body)” [CH5.44]
    • “The Celestiall or that Spirit of the world and the Naturall Spirit of our body is one and the same Spirit” [N290]
  2. The matter of the chief Medicine, which has in it the force of all heavenly and earthly things; by it impure metals are cleansed as men’s bodies are, and of it God generated the most simple matter for restoring human nature. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “that is, the Soule or Spirit of the world, which hath in it the force of all Celestiall and Terrestriall things” [CH6.55]
    • “Imperfect and impure mettalls are cleansed from their infirmities and accidents by the same spirit of the world” [CH6.39]
    • “even the most simple matter which the most Great God generated of the spirit of the world” [CH5.44]

Sulphur

The book spells it: Sulphur, Sulphure

  1. One of the three Principles of every natural body, the oily one: it tempers the Salt’s coagulation and gives substance and Transmutation; a sweet Narcotick Sulphur is found in Opium, and the superfluity of Sulphur in the body is purged through the entrails. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Into Mercury or liquor, Sulphur or Oyle, and Salt” [CH1.19]
    • “The Sulphur by a pleasant mixture tempereth the coagulation of the Salt, and gives the Body Substance and Transmutation” [CH1.19]
    • “the body of the Element, and is the Sulphur, Mercury, Salt” [CH1.29]
    • “in Opium there is a sweet Narcotick Sulphur” [CH3.19]
    • “that of Sulphure is divided and purged through the Entrals” [CH5.33]
  2. Interpreted: the vitall Sulphur, a life-bearing Sulphur in bodies, which holds the true Medicine as the treasure of Nature and which the Plague infects in man; named beside Mummy in both places. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL · wording-dependent)
    • “this Medicine, as naturall Mummy and kernell of Nature, is contained in the vitall Sulphur, as in the treasure of Nature” [CH1.3]
    • “it infecteth the Mummy and Sulphur of the microcosm, Man” [CH5.75]

Sun

The book spells it: Sun

  1. The heavenly Father, who gives his gifts to all alike as the sun shines on all. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “our Heavenly Father who is that Sun which richly preventeth us all with his gifts” [OPEN.2]
    • “shining on all alike” [OPEN.2]
  2. The sun of the sky, used in comparisons for heat, growth and melting. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “as heat from the Sun beams, which produceth all kind of flowers and herbs” [CH1.1]
    • “as the intangible Sun Melteth Snow” [CH4.6]
  3. Properly, the spirit in the sun’s body, not the visible body. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “not the body of the Sun, but the Spirit in the body, is the Sun properly” [CH1.34]
  4. A sun of the lower world: the inferior astronomy has its own Sun, and the inferior firmament has its Sun. My inference: a thing below answering to the sun above. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “both have their own Heaven, their own Sun, their own Moon” [CH1.12]
    • “like the Sun of the Terrene or inferiour Firmament” [CH6.62]
  5. Theophrastus, whom the ambitious physicians should have endured. My inference from the sentence, which speaks of him before and after. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “have indured that rising Sun without gazing on his clouds” [CH3.41]

Supernatural mischiefs

The book spells it: supernatural mischiefs

  1. The five mental diseases that arise from the Mind: enchantment, imagination, estimation, influence and superstition; they need a supernatural or mental cure. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “least our spirit should be suffocated with these five supernatural mischiefs” [CH6.73]
    • “i. e. Inchantment, Imagination, Estimation, Influence, Superstition.” [N311]
    • “mentall diseases which are invisible, and affect us in our Mind, as Inchantment, Imagination, Estimation, Influence; Superstition, all which proceed from a spirituall affection” [CH6.73]
    • “their malignant Astra’s must be averted by a supernaturall cure and magicall help into something else” [CH6.73]

Sympathy and Antipathy

The book spells it: Sympathy and Antipathy, Simpathy and Antipathy

  1. A pair God by his Wisdom ordained to be alike good, as a spectacle of Nature to stir men to search out his secrets; the text’s example is one man hating another, and the hated covering and curing the defect of the malicious. (veiled · several · voice: CROLL)
    • “God by his Wisdome hath ordained that Sympathy and Antipathy should be alike good, by which spectacle of Nature he would stir up mortall men to contemplate and search out his secrets” [CH3.5]
    • “that if one man hate another, he that is hated might cover and cure the defect of the malicious” [CH3.5]
    • “The cause of Simpathy and Antipathy.” [N165]

Taciturnity

The book spells it: Taciturnity

  1. Constant silence by which the philosophers kept the secret of the Art closed, and in which its gifts are to be used without pride. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “though they have been altogether dumb in keeping close the secret of the Art by a constant Taciturnity” [CH6.50]
    • “in a constant Taciturnity, without pride, which provoketh the envy of all men” [CH6.10]

Talent

The book spells it: Talent

  1. What the Father of Lights committed to the author, which he will no longer hide in silence. My inference from the silence and the gates of wisdom in the same paragraph: he means to publish it. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “to hide as in a Napkin, the Talent committed to me by the Father of Lights, by an impious silence” [OPEN.2]
  2. What is committed to each man in himself, set beside the treasure hidden in his earthen vessel. My inference from the words around it. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “who neglectest the large patrimony and Talent and the thing committed to thy charge” [MICRO.1]
  3. What is committed to a man, of which he must give account before the Judge, and with which good is to be done to God in the poor neighbour. My inference: in CH6.27, the secret the adept keeps. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “except he can give a good account of his Stewardship and the Talent committed to him” [CH6.27]
    • “to do good to God, with this Talent, in our poor neighbour and his members” [CH6.33]

Tartar

The book spells it: Tartar

  1. The excrement of food and drink, coagulated in man by the spirit of Salt; present in all food, it becomes the Tartar of man unless the stomach destroys it, and is the mother of almost all diseases. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Tartar therefore is the excrement of meat and drink, which by the spirit of Salt is coagulated in Man” [CH5.35]
    • “Tartar therefore or the Naturall superfluity (the mucilage of Salt) is the mother of almost all diseases of all coagulate bodies.” [CH5.37]
    • “Whatsoever we eat and drink hath in it a mucilagenous, clayish, and sandy Tartar hurtful to our Health” [CH5.34]
    • “the Archaeus of the stomack must destroy the Tartar of the Elements least it be turned into the Tartar of Man” [CH5.44]
  2. Tartar divided into a foreign kind from food and drink and an innate kind from hereditary blood. (plain · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Paracelsus)
    • “Paracelsus distinguisheth Tartar into that which is strange or forraine proceeding from meat and drink, and that which is innate of the cruor or hereditary blood” [CH5.36]

Temple of the heart

The book spells it: Temple of the heart, Temple of the Heart

  1. The inward part of the soul, into which man turns and where God dwells and Christ is found. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “turning inward unto themselves into the Temple of the heart” [CH5.20]
    • “who dwelleth in his holy Temple, in the Abysse of the heart or inward parts of our Soule” [CH5.20]
    • “Christ is to be found in the Temple of the Heart.” [N210]

Term

The book spells it: Terme

  1. The fixed end of life God appointed to man for sin, unknown to mortals, which no art or medicine can pass. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “that utmost and fixed Terme or determined poynt of DIVINE PREDESTINATION which is unknown to mortalls” [CH6.70]
    • “that Terme of Life which the Omnipotent God hath appointed to Man for disobedience” [CH6.54]
    • “the Terme of Life approacheth, which cannot be prolong’d by any art or help of Man” [CH5.68]
    • “till the appointed Terme of death imposed on mortalls for their sin” [CH6.38]
    • “imposed on our first Parents and their heires, for their Fall from Originall Righteousnesse, beyond which Bound no man can goe” [CH6.70]

Theophrasteans

The book spells it: Theophrasteans

  1. Men who contemplate and admire the workmanship of Nature, the heavens and sublunary bodies, and come to understand and imitate its secrets. That these are the genuine kind is inferred from CH5.59 calling some “also false and lying Theophrasteans” (CH5.59). (plain · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “Theophrasteans contemplate and admire the workmanship of Nature throughout this mighty frame of the whole Creation” [CH5.10]
    • “There are also false and lying Theophrasteans prophaning by their avarice and timerity that Divine Physick” [CH5.59]
  2. False, spurious or “adulterous” (OPEN.11) Theophrasteans: cheats who carry their impostures about the Country, claim to use Theophrastus his medicines, prepare remedies unlawfully and put the sick in danger of death. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “As for the spurious and adulterous Theophrasteans (the worst and wickedest sort of men)” [OPEN.11]
    • “carry their cheating impostures up and downe the Country” [OPEN.11]
    • “who under disguised names vaunt and vapour that they make use of Theophrastus his medicines” [CH5.59]
    • “these circumforaneous and adulterous Theophrasteans, with their unlawfully prepared Remedies” [CH5.60]
    • “wherewith they put the sick in danger of death by doubtfull and uncertaine experiments” [CH5.60]

Theophrastus

The book spells it: Theophrastus, Theophraestus, Theophrast.

  1. The physician and writer whom the author defends against envious physicians; the text uses this name and Paracelsus for the same man. His physick is neither wholly to be rejected nor wholly embraced. (plain · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “if Paracelsus speak any thing against those that follow the rule of their Experience” [CH5.29]
    • “Some of them are so incensed against that Theophrastus” [CH5.29]
    • “so would he gladly have served Theophraestus for nothing many years” [CH3.40]
    • “who cast a Serpents eye upon Theophrastus” [CH3.41]
    • “who have the true medicines prepared according to Theophrastus his minde” [CH3.41]
    • “neither the Physick of the Ancients, nor that of Theophrastus, is totally to be rejected” [CH5.7]
    • “Flagae which Theophrastus in his works affirmeth” [CH1.11]

Three first

The book spells it: three first, Three First, Three first, three first things

  1. Three that show themselves with their hidden virtues when the first life of herbs and medicines dies and the second is reached by the Chymist’s help, and that are shown naked in a mercurial, sulphurean and saline form; the physical body is said to be nothing but Sulphur, Salt and Mercury. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “the Three First discover themselves with their hidden vertues” [CH1.37]
    • “the Physicall body is nothing else but Sulphur, Salt and Mercury” [CH1.37]
    • “in their Three First (principles) with their garments put off; made naked and visible” [CH6.31]
    • “a most limpid or clear MERCURIAL, a most garnished SULPHUREAN or oyly, and a most bright pure SALINE form” [CH6.31]
  2. The three from which all compound bodies come and into which they are resolved, found in every matrix. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: QUOTED: Severinus)
    • “from the three first are all compound bodies, into which they are againe resolved” [CH1.25]
    • “these three are found in every matrix” [CH1.25]
  3. The three whose union in a body is health and whose disunion is sickness: the cause of all diseases. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “So that the Three First are the cause of all diseases” [CH1.40]
    • “in what body soever they are united that may be concluded to be a sound body” [CH1.40]
    • “The Originall of all diseases is from the Three First upon which the Astra’s” [N073]
    • “Mans health consisteth in the agreement and union of the Three First Substances” [CH6.53]
    • “the Three First Substances of diseases are valitile” [CH6.53]
  4. The three whose proportion broken is the origin of bodily diseases, set beside what common physicians call the disorder of the four Elements or Humors. (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: best Physitians)
    • “the true originall of all corporall diseases, in the judgement of the best Physitians, is the enormity of the Naturall proportion of the Three First” [CH6.52]
  5. The three first of one particular substance, vinegar, which Galen could not separate; a bracket glosses them as qualities. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “to separate the three first (qualities) in vineger” [CH3.40]
  6. The three first things of milk: whey, butter and cheese, all earthiness being Salt (margin note only). (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Whey, Butter and Cheese are the three first things of milk, for all Terresterity is Salt” [N181]
  7. A set of three of which man partakes, named where the metals’ use in physic is defended. Reading 1: the same three as elsewhere, in which all bodily things are contained. Basis: the phrase as used in CH1.37 and CH1.40. Reading 2: the animal, vegetable and mineral seed named in the next words. Basis: their place directly after. (veiled · several · voice: CROLL)
    • “although man partaketh of those Three first, let them know that the Animall, Vegetable and Minerall sperm or seed have but one rise or originall” [CH3.28]

Three Principles

The book spells it: THREE Principles, Three principles, Three Principles, Three First

  1. Mercury, Sulphur and Salt, the three found in every compound body, into which it can be parted and no more or fewer; their mechanick sciences have their vigour in the spirits from which all natural actions proceed; diseases spring from their invisible mechanick spirits, and, in the judgement of the best Physitians, from the enormity of their natural proportion, and happen by their exaltation. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “there are THREE Principles of all things which are found in every compound body” [CH1.19]
    • “No body compos’d by Nature can by any dissolving skill be parted into more or lesse then Three, viz. Into Mercury or liquor, Sulphur or Oyle, and Salt” [CH1.19]
    • “For as all Naturall actions proceed from Spirits and spirituall Tinctures, in which the mecanick sciences of the Three principles have their vigour” [CH2.3]
    • “from the Nature of the Seed or Astra’s and Invisible mechanick Spirits of the Three principles” [CH4.6]
    • “the true originall of all corporall diseases, in the judgement of the best Physitians, is the enormity of the Naturall proportion of the Three First” [CH6.52]
    • “the diseases that hapned by the exaltation of the Three Principles” [CH6.52]

Tincture

The book spells it: Tincture, Tinctures

  1. That in things, named with their Spirits, from which their natural actions, and the actions of cures, proceed, as against the bodies and their dead qualities. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “all Naturall actions proceed from Spirits and spirituall Tinctures” [CH2.3]
    • “the actions of Cures proceed from the Spirits and vitall Tinctures of the spirits, not from the bodies or dead Relloceous qualities” [CH2.3]
  2. Something some bodies have in which the qualities agree though no actions proceed from them; and a ‘Radicall Tincture’ that, with the Balsam, preserves the Elements in all things. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: QUOTED: Severinus)
    • “some bodies doe imitate the properties or qualities of Seeds, and have the Tinctures in which though heat, cold, moysture and drinesse accord, yet no actions proceed from them” [CH1.25]
    • “mingled with and preserved by the Balsam and Radicall Tincture” [CH1.25]
  3. New Tinctures that came in by God’s curse after the fall; the bracket calls them infinite evils. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “by the curse of God new Tinctures came in (even infinite evills)” [CH3.11]
  4. Something named beside colour and form, which precious stones take from a metal by the stars; each kind of stone has the Tincture of one metal. (plain · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “precious stones have the Colour, Forme and Tincture more or lesse from Mettalls by formation of the Stars” [CH3.27]
    • “Rubies and Garnats have the Tincture of Gold” [CH3.27]
  5. What a margin note names the Fife Essence of the Microcosm (margin note only). (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “The Tincture is the Fife Essence of the Microcosm” [N283]
  6. The thing sought, set against the mere colour that those whose brains grow giddy find instead. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: QUOTED: Alphidius)
    • “instead of the Tincture they should find only the colour” [CH6.25]
  7. Something that cleanses the Balsom, with an effect seen to the tenth generation (margin note only). (veiled · Interpreted · voice: QUOTED: Paracels.)
    • “The Tincture doth so cleanse the Balsom, that our children in the tenth generation may see the effect of it” [N294]
  8. Said of the Astra’s of the Firmament in relation to the mind: they are called the Tincture of its speculation (rests on one word of the translation). (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “all the Astra’s of the Firmament, which are the Tincture of the speculation of our mind” [MICRO.35]
  9. Part of the name of a writing of Paracelsus cited by the author. (plain · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “which Paracelsus in the Tincture of Physicks explaineth” [CH6.47]
    • “Paracelsus in the Tincture of medicines.” [N189]

Transmutation

The book spells it: Transmutation, transmutation

  1. A change of a body or substance into another state: of one’s own or another’s body by a vehement imagination; of the Salt in man, the disease being such as the transmutation is; and of things of natural composition, which are subject to it. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “A vehement Imagination doth not onely cause a transmutation of ones own body, but sometimes also of anothers” [MICRO.41]
    • “Which Salt may be changed and turned into all sorts of Salts, and such as is the Transmutation such also is the disease.” [CH4.8]
    • “are subject to corruption and transmutation” [CH6.58]
  2. Something the Sulphur gives to bodies, beside substance. Interpreted (my inference), narrowly: the body’s capacity to change. (plain · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “The Sulphur by a pleasant mixture tempereth the coagulation of the Salt, and gives the Body Substance and Transmutation” [CH1.19]

Transplanted

The book spells it: transplanted, transplantation

  1. Of the whole Creature after the Fall: its beauty transplanted, a transplantation which the note says is by the calamity and coming in of sickness upon it. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “the beauty of the whole Creature was transplanted” [CH3.11]
    • “The transplantation of the Creature is by the calamity and coming in of sicknesse upon it” [N169]
  2. Of precious stones: metals carried over into stones, which have their colour, form and tincture more or less from metals. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL · wording-dependent)
    • “for they are transplanted Mertalls” [CH3.27]
    • “precious stones have the Colour, Forme and Tincture more or lesse from Mettalls by formation of the Stars” [CH3.27]
  3. Of Eve: Adam set over into another. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL · note-only)
    • “Eve is Adam transplanted.” [N121]

Treasures of Nature

The book spells it: Treasures of Nature, treasures of Nature

  1. Goods of nature which the ancient Philosophers forbade to be rashly given to unworthy men. My inference: the same hidden things as the Secrets named beside them. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: QUOTED: ancient Philosophers)
    • “rashly to prostitute the Treasures of Nature to unworthy and filthy minded men” [OPEN.2]
  2. Stores in nature where the most High has laid up medicines for the worst diseases. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “those choise treasures of Nature, wherein the most High hath laid up medicines for our greatest and worst diseases” [OPEN.19]

Treasury

The book spells it: Treasury

  1. The knowledge of God, called the store in which all things are laid up. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “the knowledge of God is the Treasury of the whole world wherein all things are laid up” [MICRO.9]
  2. The Light of Nature, called the Treasury of God confirmed in the Scriptures. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “The Light of Nature, which is the Treasury of God confirmed in the Scriptures, maketh a true Phylosopher” [CH5.19]
  3. A store hidden and shut up within a man, to be fetched out by daily piety rather than sought outside. Interpreted (this reading’s own inference): the narrowest reading is the things the same paragraph says men seek in vain without them, by books, masters and journeys, which they might possess within themselves; MICRO.1 speaks likewise of a treasure hid in man’s earthen vessel. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “fetch out that huge Talent and Treasury which is hid and shut up within themselvs” [CH5.20]
    • “should in vaine seek those things without them by many books, mortall masters, long journeys” [CH5.20]
    • “which yet inwardly they might possesse abundantly within themselves” [CH5.20]
    • “who considerest not the Treasure that is hid in thy earthen vessell, and may thereout be digged” [MICRO.1]
  4. The author’s heart as the inmost store from which he imparted what he learned. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “imparted candidly from the intimate and inmost Armory or Treasury of my Heart” [CH6.76]

True physic

The book spells it: True Physick, true Physick

  1. The Physick the author sets out to treat: the gift of God alone, not learned from the Heathens, covered in bark and rind and to be got out and prepared by Fire; what he says he desired to find. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “CHAP. I. Of the True Physick.” [CH1.0]
    • “THE true Physick whereof by the Devine assistance I intend here to treat, is the mear gift of the most high God” [CH1.1]
    • “it is not to be sought for or learned from the Heathens, but from God alone” [CH1.1]
    • “How the True Physick, which is covered bark and rind, is to be got out, and rightly prepared by Fire.” [CH3.0]
    • “but with an exceeding love to find out the True Physick” [CH6.36]
  2. True Physick as a branch of knowledge named beside Astronomy and Chymistry, needed to understand the truly philosophical preparations of Theophrastus his medicines. (plain · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “which cannot be understood without true Physick” [CH3.41]
    • “Astronomy, and Chymistry” [CH3.41]

Universal medicine

The book spells it: universall medicine, universall Medicine

  1. The medicine that heals all diseases from the corruption of humors and expels the acute diseases that proceed from the impressions of the stars, used by holy men before the flood; it does not help at the term God appointed or against a disease God inflicts. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “All diseases proceeding from the corruption of humors, how great and desperate soever their cures be, are healed by this universall medicine” [CH6.42]
    • “except the party be at the utmost Terme appointed him by the supream Being; or the disease be inflicted by God, besides Nature” [CH6.42]
    • “which diseases proceeding from the impressions of the Stars the universall medicine powerfull expelleth” [CH5.42]
    • “Many holy men used this universall Medicine before the flood” [CH6.71]
    • “And in this case the universall and chiefest medicine is of no effect” [CH5.68]

Universal spirit

The book spells it: Universall spirit, universall Spirit

  1. The spirit that lives in and quickens the whole mass of the world and fills it, called the Power of God; by its working, with the place and receptacle, bodies differ. What is said of it is to be heard without superstition or idolatrous worship, honour going to God alone. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “That universall Spirit which liveth in and quickneth this whole Masse, which worketh all in all, and filleth the whole world” [CH3.31]
    • “that Power of God, which comprehendeth all the world in it” [CH3.31]
    • “according to the agitation and operation of the Universall spirit” [CH3.30]
    • “but this we must hearken to without any superstition or Idolatrous worship, giving honour onely to One God” [CH3.31]
  2. Agrippa’s names for it: the subject of all mirability, the Being not grasped by sense. (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Agrippa)
    • “Agrippa calleth it the subject of all mirability, the Ens or Being that cannot be comprehended by sence” [CH3.31]
  3. Avicen’s name for it: the Soul of the world poured into all things. (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Avicen)
    • “it is the Soule of the world powred into all things” [CH3.31]

Virtue

The book spells it: vertue, Vertue, virtue

  1. The healing or harmful power in a thing, which the text sets apart from its quality (hot, cold): Nature itself is a vertue, not a quality, and without vertue a quality is dead. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Nature is not a quality but a vertue” [CH4.6]
    • “for without vertue the quallity is dead and wholly accidentall” [CH4.6]
    • “by the Specifick venemous vertue of the Opium” [CH4.6]
    • “How, and with what Vertue, Physick worketh upon Mans body and cureth his Diseases.” [CH4.0]
    • “before they can impart their hidden and restrained vertues” [CH3.6]
    • “by a specifick and Harmonicall vertue serve to cure and comfort the heart” [CH2.5]
    • “the Naturall innate vertue of things that grow of the Earth, the Celestiall influence causing that vertue” [CH5.7]
  2. Moral goodness, which draws envy. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “But virtue can never be found without the marks and wounds of envy” [OPEN.12]

Vulcan

The book spells it: Vulcan

  1. Fire as the means by which the author tried things before commending them. My inference: OPEN.5 names the Art of Fire and the trial of fire as the test of what is true. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “by the help of Vulcan search and diligently examine the chief and choycest things” [OPEN.3]
    • “without Nature’s Vulcan” [OPEN.4]
    • “the due approved triall of fire, which discerneth and separateth that which is false, from that which is true” [OPEN.5]
  2. The true inventor and teacher of the mysteries of the arts, as the Poets praise him. (veiled · Stated · voice: QUOTED: Poets)
    • “which the Poets commend as the most true Inventer and Teacher of Arts Mysteries” [OPEN.4]
  3. The name set on an alchymy that serves the Common Wealth and on the Spagyrick and Hermetick school. My inference: the art of fire. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “serve the Common Wealth as the Alchymy of Vulcan” [CH3.8]
    • “the Spagyrick and Hermetick School of Vulcan” [CH6.76]

Woarchadumie

The book spells it: Woarchadumie

  1. One of three, with the true Cabala and Magick, in which far better treasures are laid up than those offered here; the margin note N015, set in the same sentence just after these words, says a word is not understood. My inference: an art or body of knowledge, and the note may refer to this word. (veiled · Interpreted · voice: CROLL)
    • “in the true Cabala, Magick and Woarchadumie, there are laid up far better Treasures” [OPEN.10]
    • “A word I doe not understand.” [N015]

Word

The book spells it: Word, WORD, word

  1. God’s creating word FIAT (Let there be), by which the first matter, heaven and earth and all things were brought out of darkness into light. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “By the word FIAT (or Let there be) God produced the first matter” [CH1.19]
    • “by this Word, FIAT, the seed of the whole world, were Heaven and Earth created” [CH2.20]
    • “were called out to the visible Light by the WORD speaking, and the Spirit cherishing” [MICRO.35]
    • “by vertue of that omnipotent Word, whereby all things were brought out of darknesse into Light” [CH2.2]
  2. The WORD of God as the heavenly medicine that alone heals all things: incarnate, found in the creatures, by whose efficacy all medicines become powerful, and not known to every one; herbs are only its sign; in it the true Cabalist performs what he does. (veiled · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “The Caelestiall Medicine onely, or the WORD of God” [CH2.13]
    • “and by the efficacy of the WORD (in which lyeth hid, and from which proceedeth all force beyond any naturall actions) all Medicines become powerfull” [CH2.13]
    • “hearbs are not the medicines, but a signe onely of the Word signified” [CH2.13]
    • “he did it by One Word, and he is that Word, to wit, the increated Mercy of God” [CH2.18]
    • “Physitians also have wrought great cures by the Created Word, or the incarnate Mercy” [CH2.18]
    • “This Word then is the true medicine that healeth all things, but is not known to every one” [CH2.20]
    • “the WORD of God INCARNATE which may be found in the Creatures” [CH2.20]
    • “that is he doth performe it in the WONDERFULL WORD by the Mind, Faith and Prayer” [CH2.22]
  3. Words spoken, written or carved as means of cure: invisible bodies, as medicines are visible ones. (plain · Stated · voice: CROLL)
    • “Medicines are visible bodies; Words are invisible bodies” [CH2.17]
    • “Concerning Characteristicalll Cure which affecteth Naturall operations by words pronounced, written, carved and hanged about the neck” [CH2.17]
    • “by a Word written and Characters or Signes” [CH2.16]
